🧠Memory Aid

No specific memory aids available for this section due to lack of detailed content in the provided text.

For pediatrics, focus on common pediatric emergencies (e.g., respiratory distress, sepsis) and key diagnostic criteria (e.g., WBC counts,

imaging protocols).

Use mnemonics like "ABC" (Airway, Breathing, Circulation) for initial resuscitation steps.

Remember "PALS" (Pediatric Advanced Life Support) guidelines for managing cardiac arrest.

page 1

Treatment

Ganciclovir (inhibits viral DNA synthesis)

Administered intravenously at 5 mg/kg every 12 hours

Duration: 6 weeks (may vary based on treatment response and drug tolerance)

Monitoring: Quantitative CMV DNA level measurement

Foscarnet (inhibits viral DNA polymerase)

Genital Herpes Virus Infection (HSV-1 and HSV-2)

HSV-1: Causes oral herpes

HSV-2: Causes genital lesions

Pregnancy note: HSV-2 infection is a contraindication for cesarean section

Clinical Symptoms

Skin: Blister-like lesions

Systemic:

Inflammation of mouth and salivary glands

Hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice

Severe form: Generalized infection with involvement of OUN (high mortality)

Diagnosis

Viral isolation from:

Blisters fluid

Cerebrospinal fluid

Saliva, blood, stool

PCR detection of viral DNA

Treatment

Acyclovir:

10 mg/kg every 8 hours IV for 10–14 days

Eye complications:

1% trifluridine drops

Memory Aid

HSV-1 vs. HSV-2 Mnemonic:

HSV-1 = Mouth (oral herpes)

HSV-2 = Genitals (genital lesions)

Drug Comparison Table:

Drug

Mechanism

Use

Dose

Acyclovir

Inhibits viral DNA polymerase

HSV infections

10 mg/kg IV every 8 hours

Ganciclovir

Inhibits viral DNA synthesis

CMV infections

5 mg/kg IV every 12 hours

Foscarnet

Inhibits viral DNA polymerase

HSV/CMV infections

Not specified in text

Clinical Pearl:

CMV monitoring during ganciclovir therapy relies on quantitative CMV DNA levels.

HSV-2 in pregnancy requires avoiding cesarean section due to risk of transmission.

Wybrane Choroby Infekcyjne Wieku Dziecięcego | 105

1 secClinical Symptoms

6 secFirst Period (7–14 days)

Feverish state

Runny nose

Sore throat

Laryngitis

Conjunctivitis

Dry cough

18 secSecond Period (2–4 weeks)

Intensification of paroxysmal cough with large amount of phlegm and gurgling breaths (mainly at night)

Expectoration of thick mucus

Dizziness

Vomiting

Hoarseness

Swelling in the area around the face, neck, and joints

Auscultation reveals rales over lung fields (bronchial rales)

6 secRecovery Period (1–2 weeks; can last up to 10 weeks)

Gradual reduction of cough

15 secComplications

Laryngitis

Pulmonary infection, segmental pneumonia; pleural effusion

Otitis media

Brain inflammation, encephalopathy, convulsions, tremors

Death (0.1–4% cases)

Complications primarily occur in infants and children with respiratory or circulatory system disorders (heart defects, asthma, bronchial asthma,

cystic fibrosis)

16 secLaboratory and Imaging Diagnosis

High leukocytosis with a predominance of lymphocytes

Bacteriological and serological diagnosis

Chest radiography:

First period: Widening of the interstitial markings

Second period: "Base triangle" (linear opacities from the hilum to the periphery along the lung markings)

7 secTreatment

Antibiotic therapy: ampicillin, macrolides, trimethoprim/sulfamethoxazole

Symptomatic treatment

Hospitalization of infants due to the risk of complications

5 minPrevention

Vaccination according to the vaccination schedule

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics for Clinical Periods

First Period: Fever, Runny nose, Sore throat, Laryngitis, Conjunctivitis, Dry cough (F-R-S-L-C-D)

Second Period: Paroxysmal cough, Phlegm, Gurgling breaths, Expectorating mucus, Dizziness, Vomiting, Hoarseness, Swelling (P-P-G-E-D-V-

H-S)

Recovery Period: Reduction of Cough (R-C)

🧠Acronym for Complications

Laryngitis, Pneumonia, Otitis, Brain inflammation, Encephalopathy, Death → L.P.O.B.E.D.

🧠Comparison Table: Periods and Key Features

Period

Duration

Key Symptoms

Imaging Finding

7–14 days

Fever, runny nose, dry cough

Widening of interstitial markings

2–4 weeks

Paroxysmal cough, phlegm, gurgling breaths

N/A

🧠Clinical Pearl

Hospitalization is critical for infants due to the high risk of complications like pneumonia, encephalopathy, and death.

Chemoprophylaxis after Direct Contact with an Infected Person

Indication: Unvaccinated children or those with incomplete vaccination schedules.

Treatment:

10–14 days of ampicillin or erythromycin.

Earliest possible vaccination following exposure.

DIPHTHERIA

Etiopathogenesis

Cause: Infection by Corynebacterium diphtheriae.

Pathology:

Necrotic changes in respiratory tract mucous membranes.

Damage to heart muscle and nervous system.

Incubation period: 1–5 days.

Clinical Symptoms

Gray, fibrinous exudates (bleed when removed):

Tonsils, pharynx, larynx, trachea, bronchi, ears, skin (rarely).

General symptoms:

Fever, malaise, sore throat, neck muscle swelling.

Throat involvement:

Sore throat, high fever, difficulty swallowing, soft tissue swelling, cardiac conduction disorders.

Laryngeal involvement (rare):

Hoarseness, cough, breathlessness, respiratory difficulties (multiple complications).

Diagnosis: Based on clinical picture. Symptoms depend on infection site.

Complications

Airway obstruction.

Nephritis, hepatitis, gastritis.

Neurological and cardiac complications:

Myocarditis.

🧠Memory Aid

Clinical Symptoms Mnemonic:

GFE (Gray Fibrinous Exudates) on Tonsils, Pharynx, Larynx, Trachea, Bronchi, Ears, Skin.

Complications Acronym:

ANCHOR:

Airway obstruction

Nephritis

Cardiac complications (myocarditis)

Hepatitis

Obstruction (of airway)

Respiratory difficulties.

Key Dose: 10–14 days of ampicillin/erythromycin for chemoprophylaxis.

Diagnosis Tip: Clinical picture is the primary diagnostic criterion for diphtheria.

Selected Infectious Diseases of Childhood

Page 107

Diphtheria

Laboratory diagnostics

Bacteriological examination

Treatment

Diphtheria antitoxin (administered as soon as possible after the allergy test)

Penicillins, macrolides

Prevention

Vaccination according to the vaccination schedule

In contacts of previously vaccinated individuals, a booster dose

TETANUS

Etiopathogenesis

Infection with Clostridium tetani

Source of infection: contaminated soil

Tetanospasmin (tetanus toxin) blocks physiological inhibitory processes (blocks GABA and glycine-inhibiting neurotransmitters)

Incubation period: 3–21 days; the shorter the incubation period, the more severe the disease

Types of tetanus: generalized, localized, and cerebral

Clinical Symptoms

Prodromal period

Malaise

Feeling of increased muscle tension

Increased sweating

Paresthesia at the site of injury

Muscle spasms

Increased tone of the masseter muscles → trismus

Progressive increase in muscle tone in the neck, back, abdomen, and limbs (mainly lower limbs)

Painful iridescent contractions

Stiffness of the neck muscles

Involvement of the respiratory muscles

Laryngeal spasm

Increased blood pressure

Diagnosis based on the clinical picture

Memory Aid

TETANUS Mnemonic:

Toxin (tetanospasmin)

Etiology (Clostridium tetani)

Transmission (contaminated soil)

Action (blocks GABA/glycine)

Neonatal risk (shorter incubation = worse prognosis)

Using antitoxin (after allergy test)

Symptoms: trismus, spasms, respiratory involvement

Comparison of Tetanus Types:

| Type | Location of Spasms | Prognosis |

|--------------|--------------------------|-----------|

| Generalized |全身 (neck, back, limbs) | Poor |

| Localized | Limited to injury site | Better |

| Cerebral | Involves brain function | Rare |

Clinical Pearl: Tetanus toxin blocks inhibitory neurotransmitters → leads to unopposed muscle contraction.

Complications

Mortality complications (up to 30%).

Spinal cord compression (most commonly in the thoracic segment).

Hypoxia of the lungs during prolonged respiratory distress.

Laboratory Diagnosis

No characteristic deviations in test results.

Treatment

Symptomatic treatment under OIM-u conditions.

Vaccination - prophylaxis (see vaccination calendar).

Prophylaxis

Vaccination according to the vaccination schedule.

🧠Memory Aid

Key Points Mnemonic:

Mortality (30%) → Spinal cord compression (thoracic) → Hypoxia (lung).

Prophylaxis Tip:

Vaccination is the cornerstone of prevention – always refer to the official vaccination schedule.

Clinical Pearl:

No lab abnormalities are typical in this condition, so focus on clinical signs (e.g., respiratory distress, spinal symptoms) for diagnosis.

Comparison Table:

| Complication | Management |

|-------------------------------|------------------------------------|

| Mortality (up to 30%) | Symptomatic treatment, OIM-u care |

| Spinal cord compression | Monitor thoracic segment |

| Lung hypoxia | Address prolonged respiratory distress |

2 secSelected Issues in Pediatric Gastroenterology

2 minIntussusception (Invagination, Intussusceptio)

Etiopathogenesis

Condition in which the proximal segment of the intestine, following peristaltic movement, telescopes into a more distal segment. Antiperistaltic

invagination is rare.

80% of cases involve children aged 4–12 months; occurs significantly more often in boys.

Most commonly leads to small bowel intussusception in the ileum.

95% of cases have no identifiable cause. Contributing factors include:

Anatomical factors: Meckel’s diverticulum, intestinal polyps, jejunal diverticula

Post-infectious ileitis: Enlargement of Peyer’s patches

Respiratory tract infection or diarrhea: Adenovirus!

Central peritonitis: In the course of Schoenlein-Henoch syndrome

Cholelithiasis, neoplastic masses

Clinical Symptoms

Sudden attacks with periods of colicky abdominal pain.

Vomits containing bile, followed by intestinal contents.

Stools with a mixture of bloody mucus ("mucoid currant jelly") and fresh blood.

Apathy, signs of shock.

Physical examination findings:

Tender, movable mass of the intussuscepted intestine

Variable peristaltic pattern: Metallic tones, high-pitched during pain attacks, silent between attacks

Right lower quadrant of the abdomen appears empty

Blood in stool on rectal examination

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for Clinical Presentation:

P.A.S.T.

- Pain: Sudden, colicky abdominal pain

- Abdominal mass: Tender, movable mass

- Stool: "Currant jelly" appearance (blood + mucus)

- Treatment: Air enema (first-line) or surgery

Key Statistics:

- Age group: 4–12 months (80% of cases)

- Gender: More common in boys

- Cause: 95% idiopathic; 5% secondary (e.g., Meckel’s diverticulum, Peyer’s patches)

Comparison Table: Common Causes of Intussusception

| Cause | Examples |

|-------------------------|---------------------------------------|

| Anatomical | Meckel’s diverticulum, polyps |

| Post-infectious | Enlarged Peyer’s patches |

| Infectious | Adenovirus, Schoenlein-Henoch syndrome|

| Others | Cholelithiasis, neoplasms |

Clinical Pearl:

- "Currant jelly" stool is a hallmark of intussusception.

- Air enema is the gold standard diagnostic and therapeutic intervention.

2 minPediatrics

Diagnostics

Surface abdominal X-ray in upright position (distention of bowel loops, fluid levels in case of bowel obstruction, signs of bowel perforation).

Ultrasound of the abdominal cavity (target image).

Treatment

Combating the symptoms of shock, correcting water, electrolyte, protein, and blood count deficiencies; insertion of a gastric tube.

Conservative treatment (possible in the first 12 hours if there are no symptoms of peritonitis and the infant's general condition is good) – attempt

to debulk the intestine with intrarectal infusion of a contrast medium under X-ray guidance.

Rectal enema is only performed in the early stages of the disease; later on, there is a risk of intestinal perforation.

Surgical treatment – more than 12 hours after the onset of disease symptoms, in the case of symptoms of intestinal perforation and after an

unsuccessful attempt at conservative removal of the bowel; a manual removal maneuver is undertaken, and if unsuccessful or necrotic intestinal

lesions are detected, resection of the pathologically changed section is performed with end-to-end anastomosis.

Diarrhea

Acute diarrhea – a condition characterized by a child passing an increased number of stools per day (3 or more stools with formula feeding and 6

or more stools with breastfeeding) with an abnormal consistency or with stool(s) containing blood, pus, or mucus. Acute diarrhea lasts 10–14

days, but usually up to 7 days.

A breastfed baby may pass stool after each feeding.

Chronic diarrhea – a condition lasting more than 14 days, characterized by the child passing an increased number of

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics for Diarrhea Classification

Acute: Abdominal pain, Chronic: Continuous symptoms.

Acute: <14 days, Chronic: >14 days.

🧠Treatment Steps for Bowel Obstruction

Conservative first (12 hours): Intrarectal contrast infusion.

Surgical if: Peritonitis, Necrosis, >12 hours.

🧠Comparison Table: Acute vs. Chronic Diarrhea

Feature

Acute Diarrhea

Chronic Diarrhea

Duration

<14 days (usually ≤7 days)

>14 days

Stool frequency

3+ (formula), 6+ (breastfeeding)

Increased frequency

Common causes

Infection, dietary changes

Malabsorption, chronic disease

Key diagnostic clue

Blood, pus, or mucus in stool

Persistent symptoms

🧠Clinical Pearl

Rectal enema is contraindicated after 12 hours due to perforation risk.

8 secKluczowe Objawy

Stolce o niewłaściwej konsystencji, zawierające śluz, tłuszcz lub krwi

Zaburzenia odżywienia wynikające z przewlekłego niedoboru substancji odżywczych

2 minEtiopatogeneza

Diarje ostra

Przyczyny:

- Wirusowe (rotavirus, adenowirus, astrovirus, Norwalk virus)

- Najczęstszy czynnik etiologiczny: rotavirus w okresie jesienno-zimowym

- Bakteriologiczne (Escherichia coli, Salmonella, Shigella, Klebsiella spp., Pseudomonas spp., Campylobacter jejuni, Yersinia enterocolitica,

Clostridium spp.)

- Przyczyna: toksyny bakteryjne

- Parazitologiczne (Giardia lamblia)

- Grzybicze (Candida albicans)

- Niedostateczność pokarmowa i alergia na białko mleka krowiego

- Infekcje układu oddechowego (pneumonia, zapalenie ucha środkowego) i infekcje układu moczowego – szczególnie u noworodków i niemowląt

Diarje przewlekła

Przyczyny:

- Czynniki infekcyjne

- Niedostateczność pokarmowa

- Niedobory enzymów

- Użycie antybiotyków

- Wrodzone wady anatomiczno-funkcyjne błony śluzowej jelit

- Zaburzenia immunologiczne

- Choroby nowotworowe (głównie nowotwory hormonalnie aktywne)

Okres noworodkowy:

Diarja chlorotyczna

Diarja sodowa

Wrodzony niedobór laktozy

Wrodzony brak mikroflory

Niedobór glukozy/galaktozy

Przewlekła niedoborowa choroba jelita cienkiego

Okres niemowlęcy:

Infekcje wirusowe

Infekcje bakteryjne

Alergia na białko mleka krowiego

Sekundarny niedobór laktozy i sacharozy

Niedojadanie

Diarja autoimmunologiczna

Niedobory odpornościowe

Przewlekła, nawrotowa diarja

VIPoma

- Virusy (rotavirus, adenowirus, itd.)

- Parazyty (Giardia)

- Fungi (Candida)

Czas trwania

<14 dni

>14 dni

🧠Kliniczne wskazówki:

Diarja ostra u niemowląt często wiąże się z infekcjami układu oddechowego i moczowego.

VIPoma to rzadki nowotwór hormonalnie aktywny powodujący przewlekłą diarję.

12 secClinical Symptoms

Abdominal pain, vomiting, fever - symptoms of upper respiratory tract infection (mainly in cases of viral diarrhea).

Diarrhea (small, moderate, severe).

Physical development disorders in children (chronic diarrhea).

15 secAcute and Chronic Diarrhea Laboratory Diagnosis

Microbiological diagnosis is not necessary for most cases of acute diarrhea.

In the case of bloody diarrhea:

Isolation of bacteria from stool culture

Stool examination for parasites

Serologic test for viral antigens

1 secRecognition

13 secHistory

Duration of symptoms

Stool characteristics (watery, bloody, fatty, oily)

Presence of undigested food parts

Contact with individuals who are potential sources of transmission

Assessment of the child's feeding method and antibiotic use

9 secPhysical Examination

Evaluation of the child's nutritional status based on body mass index in relation to centile grids

Assessment of dehydration degree

8 secExclusion of Other Conditions

Exclusion of respiratory tract infection (pneumonia)

Exclusion of urinary tract infections - common causes of acute diarrhea

5 secSpecific Tests for Chronic Diarrhea

(No specific tests listed in original text)

55 secChronic Diarrhea

Causes:

Carbohydrate malabsorption disorders (fructose, sorbitol) mainly found in apple and grapefruit juices

Excessive intake of fluids causing bowel distension

Constipation with a low fat diet

Age range: Occurs primarily between 6 to 36 months of age

Stool characteristics: May contain undigested food remnants

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Chronic Diarrhea Causes

Fructose/Sorbitol in juices

Excessive fluid intake

Constipation with low-fat diet

🧠Comparison Table: Acute vs. Chronic Diarrhea

Feature

Acute Diarrhea

Chronic Diarrhea

Duration

Short-term (<2 weeks)

Long-term (>4 weeks)

Common Causes

Viral infections, food poisoning

Malabsorption, dietary factors, constipation

Stool Characteristics

Watery, bloody, or fatty

May contain undigested food remnants

Lab Tests

Often not required

Specific tests for underlying causes

🧠Clinical Pearl

Chronic diarrhea in children is often linked to dietary factors (e.g., fructose in juices) and should prompt evaluation of nutritional status

and feeding practices.

3 secAssessment of Dehydration in Children (WHO Criteria)

Key Note:

- To classify a patient into a given category, at least 2 symptoms listed must be present, including 1 severe symptom.

- Severe symptoms include: sunken eyeballs (sub-cranial), absence of mouth/tongue moisture, very slow skin fold retraction, and weight loss >10%.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Dehydration Symptoms

"Calm, Alert, Normal" = No dehydration

"Restless, Sunken, Thirsty" = Moderate dehydration

"Coma, Swollen Eyes, No Moisture" = Severe dehydration

🧠Clinical Pearl

Skin turgor (skin fold test) is a critical indicator:

Normal = rapid retraction

Severe dehydration = retraction delayed >2 seconds.

Weight loss is a quantitative measure but may not always be available in clinical settings.

1 minTabela 2. Wstępne różnicowanie przyczyn biegunki przewlekłej (opracowanie własne)

## Character of Stool

Bloody

- Positive stool culture (+) → bacterial diarrhea (despite a very high probability of infectious etiology, bacteriological examination is not always positive)

- Negative stool culture (-)/interview/provocative tests/RIST, RAST → food intolerance

- Additional markers of the inflammatory process, inappropriate nutritional parameters, weight loss, symptoms outside the gastrointestinal tract, p

Variable or bloody

- Confirmation of G. lamblia presence → giardiasis

Variable, presence of undigested parts in stool

- Excessive fluid intake in history, low-fat diet, normal body weight and height → chronic diarrhea

Variable or fatty

- Interview; chloride concentration > 60 mmol/l in urine → maldigestion

Fatty

- Gluten in the diet/Ema-IgA (+)/absence of villi in small intestine biopsy → celiac disease

Watery

- Przyczyny wrodzone:

→ Chloride diarrhea

→ Sodium diarrhea

- Diarrhea begins immediately after birth; examination of small intestine biopsy in electron microscopy → structural abnormalities of microvilli and b

Chronic secretory diarrhea

- pH stool > 6

- Na+ in stool < 70 mmol/l

- Osmolarity < 140 mOsm/l

- Substances reducing < 0.5%

- Przyczyny nabyte:

→ Bacterial diarrhea (despite a very high probability of infectious etiology, bacteriological examination is not always positive)

→ Autoimmune diarrhea

→ VIPoma

🧠Clinical Pearls

Celiac disease is strongly linked to gluten and Ema-IgA positivity.

Giardiasis is confirmed by detecting G. lamblia in stool.

Secretory diarrhea is osmolarity-low and pH-neutral, distinguishing it from osmotic diarrhea.

Maldigestion is suggested by high urine chloride and fatty stools.

2 secCongenital Syphilis (Syphilis congenita)

Fetal death

🧠Clinical Pearl

Congenital Syphilis: Bone changes ("bones eaten by moles") are pathognomonic and distinguish it from other congenital infections.

Enterovirus Infections: No specific antiviral treatment exists; management focuses on symptom relief and monitoring for complications like

DIC or meningitis.

Tabela 2. Wstępne różnicowanie przyczyn biegunki przewlekłej (opracowanie własne), cd.

Character of stool

Interview, laboratory diagnostics and possible

Liquid

Diarrhea osmotic pH stool <6 Na in stool <70 m

Causes:

- ↓ Lactase deficiency

- ↓ Isomaltase deficiency

- Monosaccharide absorption disorders (rarely)

Causes acquired:

- Interview → Chronic diarrhea post-infectious

- Interview → Chronic diarrhea post-antibiotic use

- Interview → Poreseptic syndromes

- Interview (lactose intolerance, additional atopic interview) → Disorders of monosaccharide absorption

Key Notes:

- No dehydration or acid-base disturbances observed.

- Diagnosis is made by excluding other causes of chronic diarrhea.

- Treatment: Use of appropriately balanced diet (protein, fats) suitable for the child’s age, with restriction of excessive fluids (mainly juices).

Treatment

- Mild to moderate dehydration:

- Rehydration with small portions of cold fluids (excluding carbonated drinks, fruit juices, and Coca-Cola, which can cause osmotic diarrhea) – 50–100

ml/kg over the first four hours or 5–10 ml/kg after each loose stool.

- Severe dehydration:

- Parenteral rehydration in cases of frequent vomiting after each feeding attempt or signs of life-threatening conditions.

Memory Aid

- Mnemonic for osmotic diarrhea causes: LIM (Lactase, Isomaltase, Monosaccharide absorption).

- Fluid restriction tip: Avoid Coca-Cola, juices, and carbonated drinks in osmotic diarrhea.

- Dehydration management:

| Dehydration Severity | Rehydration Method |

| --- | --- |

| Mild–moderate | Oral rehydration (cold fluids, 50–100 ml/kg in 4 hours) |

| Severe | Parenteral rehydration (IV fluids) |

- Clinical pearl: Chronic diarrhea with pH <6, Na <70 mmol/l, and osmolality >140 mOsm/l strongly suggests osmotic causes (e.g., lactose

intolerance).

3 secPediatrics - Fluid and Electrolyte Management in Diarrhea

15 secGeneral Principles

Balancing water-electrolyte and acid-base disturbances is critical in pediatric diarrhea management.

Routine use of lactose-free/low-lactose preparations is not indicated in children except in:

Diarrhea following introduction of milk products into the diet.

Confirmed lactose intolerance.

20 secIndications for Antibiotic/Chemotherapy Use

Only in the following scenarios:

Bloody diarrhea (specific pathogens and treatments):

Salmonella: Cefalosporins III generation, ampicillin, trimethoprim/sulfamethoxazole.

Shigella: Ampicillin, trimethoprim/sulfamethoxazole.

Campylobacter spp.: Erythromycin.

Clostridium difficile: Vancomycin, metronidazole.

Confirmed infection symptoms persisting after bowel movements.

Diarrhea during the neonatal period (up to 3 months of life).

9 secRehydration Strategies

Rapid rehydration based on diet before illness onset, within 4 hours of oral feeding.

Adjunct therapies:

Probiotics, prebiotics, and smectite.

1 minManagement of Prolonged Diarrhea

1. Nutritional treatment:

2. Mixed hypoallergenic, low-lactose, lactose-free, gluten-free, sugar-free, and homogenized milk diet.

3. Energy requirements:

4. Cover increased energy consumption due to additional feeding.

5. Chemotherapy drugs:

6. Cholestyramine for excess bile acids.

7. Metronidazole (20 mg/kg mc./24 hours) or cotrimoxazole (5–10 mg trimethoprim/kg mc./24 hours) for 7–10 days in dysbacteriosis (<6

months old).

8. Immunosuppressive treatment:

9. For autoimmune diarrhea.

10. Probiotics:

11. Continue use.

12. Chloride diarrhea:

13. Requires only oral supplementation with NaCl and KCl; no dietary treatment needed.

14. VIPoma-related diarrhea:

15. Surgery is the treatment of choice.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Pathogens requiring antibiotics:

S.C.C.C. = Salmonella, Shigella, Campylobacter, Clostridium difficile.

Drugs for Clostridium difficile:

Vancomycin + Metronidazole (V+M).

Dysbacteriosis in <6 months:

Metronidazole or Cotrimoxazole (M/C).

VIPoma management:

Surgery is the gold standard (S = Surgery).

Chloride diarrhea:

No diet needed; only NaCl/KCl supplementation.

🧠Clinical Pearl

Lactose-free diets are reserved for confirmed lactose intolerance or post-milk product diarrhea; avoid routine use.

2 secGastrointestinal Bleeding (Krwawienie z przewodu pokarmowego)

23 secClinical Presentation and Classification

Definition: Presence of blood in the digestive tract, manifesting through vomit and/or stool with blood admixture.

Classification:

Upper gastrointestinal bleeding: Source of bleeding above Treitz's ligament.

Lower gastrointestinal bleeding: Source of bleeding below Treitz's ligament.

Systemic disease-related bleeding: Bleeding throughout the entire digestive tract (e.g., hemorrhagic rash, intracranial syndrome, hemolytic-

uremic syndrome, neoplastic diseases).

syndrome, Rendu-Osler-Weber disease, Ehlers-Danlos syndrome)

Neoplastic diseases

Neonatal hemorrhagic disease is caused by vitamin K deficiency – remember "K" for Kidney (vitamin K is needed for clotting factors).

1 minClinical Considerations

Infants: Bleeding may result from esophageal inflammation during gastroesophageal reflux. Blood in stool may accompany chronic

constipation.

Diagnosis:

Key step: Conduct a detailed interview about vomiting patterns, stool characteristics, and medical history.

Fresh blood in vomit: Indicates upper gastrointestinal bleeding (e.g., esophageal varices).

Coffee grounds vomit: Indicates gastric mucosa bleeding.

Diarrheic stools with blood: Suggests intestinal inflammation.

Stools with small amounts of fresh blood: May indicate anal fissure.

Large amounts of fresh blood: Suggests lower gastrointestinal bleeding.

diverticulum)

Youth: HIP (Hemorrhoids, Inflammatory bowel disease, Polyps)

36 secDiagnostic Methods

Laboratory and Imaging Studies

1. Blood morphology

2. Reticulocytosis (immediately after bleeding; morphological analysis is not an exact test)

3. Endoscopic examination:

4. Gastrofiberscopy

5. Colonoscopy

6. Rectoscopy (identifies bleeding source)

7. Scintigraphy:

8. Technetium-99m: Diagnoses intestinal torsion and Meckel’s diverticulum

9. Red blood cell markers: Localizes blood accumulation from extravasation

10. Angiography: Identifies active bleeding sources

11. Hidden bleeding detection:

12. Especially in children with low hemoglobin and elevated reticulocytosis

13. Occult blood in stool testing

14. Ultrasound Doppler: Diagnoses esophageal varices

15. Laparotomy: For life-threatening, unexplained bleeding

2 minTreatment

1. Immediate hospitalization

2. Manage hypovolemic shock

3. Rapid localization of bleeding source

🧠Clinical Pearl

Melena is a red flag for upper GI bleeding; hematochezia suggests lower GI bleeding.

Scintigraphy with technetium-99m is the gold standard for Meckel’s diverticulum in children.

Endoscopy is the first-line diagnostic tool for identifying bleeding sources.

Treatment of Bleeding

Pragmatic treatment options:

Endoscopic sclerotherapy or surgical variceal ligation

Esophageal varices coagulation

Gastric varices polypectomy

Systemic-vessel complexes in the portal vein

Laser photocoagulation or vessel obliteration

Treatment of inflammatory bowel diseases

In non-inflammatory bowel diseases: Treatment for joint disease

Prevention of upper gastrointestinal bleeding:

Pharmacological (e.g., proton pump inhibitors, H2 blockers)

Non-pharmacological (e.g., lifestyle modifications, avoiding NSAIDs)

Abdominal Pain (Abdominalgia)

Recurrent Abdominal Pain

Definition: At least one painful episode per month over three consecutive months

Prevalence:

Most common in children aged 6–12 years

Occurs in 10–16% of children

More common in girls

Characteristics of Abdominal Pain

Functional abdominal pain:

Sudden onset, colicky

No characteristic localization (often around the umbilicus)

Lasts <1 hour

Associated with nausea, vomiting, discomfort

Relieved by movement, exacerbated by rest

Somatogenic abdominal pain:

Well-localized, sharp and persistent

Greatest intensity at the site of pathology

Exacerbated by movement, coughing, sneezing

Relieved by rest

Division of Abdominal Pain (Ronald G. Barr)

1. Functional (70%):

2. Associated with habitual constipation, lactose intolerance, menstrual cycle-related pain

3. Psychogenic (10–15%):

4. Unexplained by environmental factors (e.g., family/school stress)

5. Organic (10–15%):

6. Pain due to somatic diseases affecting the gastrointestinal tract or other organs

Memory Aid

Mnemonic for Barr’s classification:

Functional, Psychogenic, Organic (FPO)

Comparison Table: Functional vs. Somatogenic Abdominal Pain

Well-localized (pathology site)

Duration

<1 hour

Variable (often persistent)

Feature

Functional Abdominal Pain

Somatogenic Abdominal Pain

Aggravating factors

Rest

Movement, coughing, sneezing

Relieving factors

Movement

Rest

Clinical Pearl: Recurrent abdominal pain in children aged 6–12 years is often functional, but organic causes must be ruled out in 10–15% of cases.

3 secSelected Problems of Pediatric Gastroenterology | 121

23 secClinical Presentation: Red Flags for Persistent Abdominal Pain

Unsettling symptoms requiring expanded diagnostic evaluation include:

- Nocturnal pain or diarrhea

- Vomiting (especially with blood)

- Swallowing disorders

- Blood in stool

- Fever

- Weight loss or growth retardation

- Delayed sexual development

- Joint inflammation

- Familial occurrence of hereditary diseases (e.g., colitis, Crohn’s disease)

1 minDiagnostic Approach

1. Initial Evaluation

Detailed medical history

Differentiate between functional and organic pain

Identify potential causes of discomfort

Physical examination

Includes digital rectal examination

2. Laboratory Tests

Blood tests:

OB (occult blood)

Blood smear with differential count

Bilirubin, AspAT, AIAT, amylase (serum)

Urinalysis: General urine test and urine culture

Stool analysis: Parasite examination

3. Imaging

Abdominal ultrasound (USG)

4. Specialized Assessments

Psychological evaluation

Targeted diagnostic tests:

Food allergy testing (RIST, RAST, skin tests)

Lactose intolerance diagnosis

Gastroscopy, rectoscopy, colonoscopy

Contrast studies of the gastrointestinal tract

5. Specialist Consultations

Neurology

Gynecology

Endocrinology and metabolism

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Red Flags in Abdominal Pain

VIBRANT

- Vomiting (especially with blood)

- Intestinal symptoms (diarrhea, nocturnal pain)

- Blood in stool

- Respiratory or neurological symptoms (not directly related, but part of systemic evaluation)

- Abnormal growth (weight loss, growth retardation)

- Neurological/joint issues (seizures, joint inflammation)

- Therapeutic history (familial hereditary diseases)

🧠Clinical Pearl

Persistent abdominal pain in children with familial history of inflammatory bowel disease or systemic symptoms (e.g., joint inflammation,

growth delay) should prompt early endoscopic evaluation to rule out organic pathology.

19 secTreatment Principles for Somatic Abdominal Pain

1. Identify the cause of organic pain.

2. Dietary adjustments:

3. Lactose intolerance: Use lactose-free or low-lactose diet.

9. Eliminate stress factors

43 secColic in Infants

Prevalence

Occurs in 10–40% of infants.

Diagnostic Criteria for Excessive Crying

Excessive crying is defined by the "3-3-3 rule":

Lasts at least 3 hours per day

Occurs at least 3 times per week

Persists systematically for at least 3 weeks

Associated Symptoms

Facial flushing

Leg cramps

Passing gas

Difficulty in defecation

Characteristics of Colicky Pain

1. Appears in infants by the third week of life

2. Regular occurrence during afternoon and evening hours (when the infant is asleep during the day)

3. Occurs after feeding with a predictable pattern

4. Sudden relief within a few hours

5. Spontaneous resolution by 4–5 months of age

25 secMemory Aid

Mnemonic for Colic Diagnosis

"3-3-3 Rule":

- 3 hours/day, 3 times/week, 3 weeks

Clinical Pearl

Colic resolves spontaneously by 4–5 months; no long-term complications.

Older

Peptic ulcer, gastritis, ileitis, IBS

Key Features of Colic

Timing: Afternoons/evenings

Triggers: Feeding patterns

Resolution: Spontaneous by 4–5 months

2 secPost-Treatment Approach in Pediatric Gastrointestinal Disorders

9 secGeneral Management Strategies

Limit exposure to external irritants

Keep the child calm

Provide comfort through cuddling and pacifier use (stimulates swallowing reflex)

19 secDietary Management

Breastfed Infants

Eliminate basic allergens from the mother’s diet

Avoid foods that may exacerbate symptoms (e.g., potential triggers for milk allergy or lactose intolerance)

Formula-Fed Infants

Use hydrolyzed protein formulas or casein-based diets with high protein hydrolysis

Avoid mixed soy-based formulas (ineffective for this condition)

4 secAdjunctive Therapies

2. Simethicone

7. Lactase (for lactose intolerance)

2 secCeliac Disease (Choroba Trzewna)

9 secDefinition

Autoimmune enteropathy with lifelong persistence in genetically predisposed individuals

Characterized by:

Villous atrophy of intestinal mucosa

Clinical symptoms triggered by gluten ingestion

9 secPathophysiology

Gluten is a heterogeneous protein in the endosperm of four staple grains

Relevant prolamins for celiac disease:

Gliadin (wheat)

Dekalin (rye)

2 minManagement

Gluten-free diet induces:

Clinical remission

Histological improvement

Reintroduction of gluten may cause:

Recurrence of intestinal mucosal damage

Return of clinical symptoms

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Celiac Disease: Key Points

Mnemonic for gluten-containing grains: Wheat, Rye, Barley, Oats (W.R.B.O.) – though oats are generally tolerated in small amounts if

gluten-free.

Protein triggers: Gliadin (wheat) and Dekalin (rye) – remember as G-D (Gliadin-Dekalin).

🧠Post-Treatment Approach: Quick Recall

Comfort: Cuddle, Pacifier, Calm (CPC)

Dietary adjustments: Hydrolyzed formulas over soy-based (avoid mixed formulas)

Pharmacological options: Simethicone, Trimebutin, Lactase – remember as S-T-L (S for Simethicone, T for Trimebutin, L for Lactase).

🧠Comparison: Breastfed vs. Formula-Fed Infants

Factor

Breastfed

Formula-Fed

Dietary adjustment

Eliminate maternal allergens

Use hydrolyzed/casein-based formulas

Ineffective options

N/A

Avoid mixed soy-based formulas

Gluten-Related Disorders

Avenin – oat protein

Hordein – barley protein

Clinical Sequelae of Gluten Exposure

Non-celiac gluten sensitivity

- Symptoms from the digestive tract and beyond after gluten consumption

- Occurs in individuals without celiac disease or wheat allergy

Wheat allergy

- Immune reaction to wheat proteins

- Symptoms from skin, digestive tract, and respiratory system

Celiac disease

- Autoimmune disorder triggered by gluten in genetically predisposed individuals

Forms of Celiac Disease

1. Classic celiac disease

2. Subtypes:

Full-blown classic form: Typical gastrointestinal symptoms (e.g., diarrhea, weight loss)

Partial-blown classic form: Extra-intestinal symptoms (e.g., anemia, dermatitis herpetiformis)

3. Diagnosis:

Villous atrophy (Marsh stage III or IV) on small intestine biopsy

Positive serological markers (e.g., anti-tTG IgA)

4. Silent celiac disease

5. Symptoms: Some clinical manifestations (no malabsorption signs)

6. Diagnosis:

Villous atrophy (Marsh stage II or III) on biopsy

Positive serological markers

7. Latent celiac disease

8. Symptoms: No clinical symptoms

9. Diagnosis:

Villous atrophy (Marsh stage I) on biopsy

Serological markers may be present or absent

10. Potential celiac disease

11. Risk factors: Genetic predisposition (HLA-DQ1, DQ2, DQ8)

12. Diagnosis:

Increased intraepithelial lymphocytes in small intestine mucosa

No villous atrophy (Marsh stage 0)

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for gluten-related disorders: G-W-C

G: Gluten (Non-celiac gluten sensitivity)

W: Wheat allergy

C: Celiac disease

Celiac disease forms: C-S-L-P

C: Classic (gastrointestinal symptoms)

S: Silent (subclinical)

L: Latent (asymptomatic)

P: Potential (genetic risk without structural damage)

Key diagnostic criteria for classic celiac disease:

Villous atrophy (Marsh III/IV)

Serological markers (anti-tTG IgA, EMA)

Clinical symptoms (gastrointestinal or extra-intestinal)

Adults

Persistent ulcers

Diarrhea or diarrhea (repeated emphasis)

BMI <18

Resistant to treatment of anemia

Osteopenia, osteoporosis

Lack of menstruation (amenorrhea in women)

Infertility in men

Depression

Increased incidence of other autoimmune diseases:

Thyroiditis

Autoimmune liver inflammation

Joint and connective tissue disorders

5 secChronic Duhring’s Disease (Intestinal-Skin Syndrome, Dermatitis Herpetiformis, DH)

Skin manifestation of gluten intolerance

10 secDiseases Associated with Celiac Disease

Type 1 diabetes

Autoimmune thyroiditis

Down syndrome

Rheumatic diseases

Psoriasis

16 secPrevalence and Related Conditions

Celiac disease prevalence: 1 in 300–1800 children aged 2.5–15 years (≈1% of adult population)

IgA deficiency in relatives:

~10% of healthy relatives and first-degree celiacs

~5% of those with IgA deficiencies suffer from non-celiac dermatitis

🧠Clinical Pearl

Celiac disease is 10x more common in children with Down syndrome compared to the general population.

Dermatitis herpetiformis (DH) is a skin manifestation of celiac disease, often presenting with symmetrical, itchy blisters on the elbows,

knees, and scalp.

🧠Comparison Table: Celiac Disease vs. Non-Celiac Dermatitis

Feature

Celiac Disease

Non-Celiac Dermatitis

Skin Lesions

diagnosis.

2 secCeliac Disease Diagnosis and Management

23 secDiagnostic Criteria

Serological markers (quantitative assessment):

Endomysial Antibodies (EMA) in IgA and/or IgG class

Antitissue Transglutaminase tTG type 2 in IgA and IgG class

Antideamidated gliadin peptide (DPG)

Histopathological confirmation (Marsh classification):

Typical changes in small intestine mucosa (type 2 or 3)

Assessment of:

Villous atrophy

Crypt depth

Degree of inflammatory exudate

Note: Not mandatory in every case!

3 secTreatment

Strict adherence to a gluten-free diet

21 secWhen Celiac Disease Cannot Be Diagnosed

1. Quantitative assessment of serological markers not performed (positive result +)

2. Serological testing conducted after starting a gluten-free diet (even briefly)

3. Marsh classification shows first-degree villous atrophy in the small intestine

4. Biopsy description is incorrect or ambiguous

5. Gluten-free diet initiated without any tests

1 minCommon Errors in Diagnosing Celiac Disease in Children

1. Inadequate history taking

2. Failure to assess body weight/height using centile charts

3. Treating persistent diarrhea without identifying the cause

4. Starting gluten-free diet without specialist assessment

5. Performing serological tests after initiating the diet

6. Qualitative assessment of serological markers based solely on ± results

7. Testing only EMA or tTG in one antibody class (e.g., IgA without total IgA level determination)

8. Diagnosis based on vague biopsy descriptions

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Key Mnemonics

"SMART BIOPSY" (for histopathological confirmation):

Small intestine mucosa

Marsh classification (types 2/3)

Assess villous atrophy

R crypt depth

T inflammatory exudate

🧠Avoid These 5 Errors

1. No quantitative serology → Invalid diagnosis

2. Diet before testing → False negatives

3. First-degree atrophy → Not celiac disease

4. Ambiguous biopsy → Recheck histology

5. No specialist assessment → Misdiagnosis risk

🧠Clinical Pearl

Always confirm total IgA levels when testing for EMA or tTG in IgA class to avoid false negatives due to IgA deficiency.

Key Mnemonics for Pediatric Gastroenterology

- Common GI Issues in Pediatrics:

- Celiac disease (gluten-sensitive enteropathy)

- Irritable bowel syndrome (IBS)

- Gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD)

- Eczema (associated with food allergies)

- Diarrhea (common in infections, lactose intolerance)

- Deficiency in Folate → Megaloblastic anemia

- Vitamin D deficiency → Rickets, Osteomalacia

Clinical Pearl:

- Lactose intolerance is a functional disorder, while celiac disease is an immune-mediated condition.

- Rotavirus is the leading cause of viral gastroenteritis in children.

- H. pylori infection is a major cause of peptic ulcer disease in children.

2 secSELECTED PROBLEMS OF CHILDREN'S NEPHROLOGY

1 minURINARY CALCULUS IN CHILDREN

Etiopathogenesis

Stone formation occurs due to:

Increased concentration of substances prone to crystallization

Decreased inhibitor concentrations (e.g., citrate, Mg, Zn, pyrophosphates)

Urine pH favoring stone formation

Common stone types (by prevalence):

Calcium oxalate (70–80%)

Struvite (5–10%)

Uric acid (5–10%)

Ammonium magnesium phosphate (10%)

Cystine (6–8%)

Key metabolic predispositions:

Hypercalciuria

Hyperoxaluria

Hyperuricosuria

Hypomagnesuria

Cystinuria

Hypocitraturia

Crystallization inhibitors:

Citrate ions

Mg, Zn, F

Pyrophosphates

Tamm-Horsfall protein

Uropontine

Calgranulin

Fibronectin

Nephrocalcin

Glycosaminoglycans

Osteopontin

Bikinin

Most common metabolic cause:

Hypercalciuria (60% of urinary tract stone cases)

Hypercalciuria

Definition: Increased urinary calcium excretion

Diagnosis: Based on:

Urinary calcium concentration

Calcium-to-creatinine ratio

Types:

Absorptive hypercalciuria (90% of cases):

Caused by excessive intestinal calcium absorption

Nephrocalcinuria:

Due to defective tubular calcium reabsorption

Can occur in any segment of the nephron where calcium reabsorption occurs

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Stone Types Mnemonic:

COST (Calcium Oxalate, Struvite, Uric Acid, Other)

Note: "Other" includes ammonium magnesium phosphate and cystine.

Key Inhibitors:

Citrate, Mg, Zn, Pyrophosphates, Tamm-Horsfall protein, Uropontine, Fibronectin, Nephrocalcin, Glycosaminoglycans, Osteopontin, Bikinin

Use acronym: C M Z P T U F N G O B**

Hypercalciuria Types:

Absorptive = Intestinal absorption ↑

Nephrocalcinuria = Tubular reabsorption ↓

Diagnosis Tip:

Always check calcium-to-creatinine ratio in urine for hypercalciuria (not just absolute calcium levels).

2 minSelected Problems of Pediatric Nephrology

Resorptive Hyperkaliuria – a Consequence of Primary Hyperparathyroidism

Pak's Test: Differentiates absorptive hyperkaliuria from renal hyperkaliuria.

Involves determining calcium-creatinine indices in urine after:

A 7-day dairy-free diet

Oral administration of a calcium preparation

Hyperoxaluria → Calcium Oxalate Deposits

Key Features:

Increased urinary oxalate excretion

Diagnosis: Based on oxalate concentration in 24-hour urine and magnesium-calcium index

Types of Hyperoxaluria:

Primary Hyperoxaluria:

Increased intracellular production of oxalates due to metabolic disturbances (rare)

Secondary Hyperoxaluria:

Excessive dietary oxalate supply or increased intestinal absorption (e.g., malabsorption syndromes, chronic intestinal inflammation)

Chronic vitamin C intake, antibiotic therapy destroying intestinal flora (e.g., Oxalobacter formigenes, Pseudomonas oxalicus – bacteria that

utilize oxalates)

Hyperuricosuria → Urate Deposits

Key Features:

Increased excretion of uric acid in urine

Low-purine diet reduces hyperuricosuria from excessive purine intake

Does not reduce uric acid excretion in cases of endogenous overproduction (e.g., gout, Lesh-Nyhan syndrome, storage diseases, leukemias,

lymphomas)

Hyperphosphaturia → Phosphate-Ammonium-Magnesium (Struviate) and Phosphate-Calcium (Apatite) Deposits

Key Features:

Increased urinary phosphate excretion

Diagnosis: Based on urinary phosphate concentration and phosphate-creatinine ratio

Crystallization occurs at urine pH > 7.2, especially in infections with urease-producing bacteria

🧠Memory Aid

Pak's Test Mnemonic:

Product of Absorptive vs. Kidney hyperkaliuria – calcium-creatinine index after 7 days dairy-free and calcium supplementation.

Hyperoxaluria Types:

Primary: Intracellular overproduction (rare)

Secondary: Dietary or intestinal causes (e.g., vitamin C, antibiotics, malabsorption)

Hyperuricosuria Pearls:

Low-purine diet helps only in dietary cases, not in endogenous overproduction (e.g., gout, Lesh-Nyhan).

Hyperphosphaturia Crystallization:

pH > 7.2 + urease-producing bacteria → struvite stones.

Phosphate-creatinine ratio is key for diagnosis.

2 secUrinary Tract Stones in Pediatrics

52 secEtiology and Pathogenesis

Hypermagnesuria

Bacterial causes: Proteus, Pseudomonas, Klebsiella, Serratia marcescens, Enterococcus.

Mechanism: Ability to alkalinize urine.

Clinical feature: Stones form quickly and can fill all urinary tract systems (renal calculi).

Hypomagnesuria

Cause: Reduced excretion of magnesium in urine.

Diagnosis: Evaluation of magnesium-creatinine ratio, calcium-creatinine ratio, and urine magnesium concentration.

Association: Stone formation in uric acid stones.

Cystinuria

Cause: Increased excretion of cystine in urine.

Genetic basis: Defect in transport of cysteine, ornithine, lysine, arginine in renal tubules and digestive tract.

Diagnosis:

Acidic urine pH.

Presence of cystine crystals.

Mayer-Brada test (colorimetric cyanide-nitroprusside test).

Hypocitraturia

Cause: Reduced excretion of citrates in urine.

Role of citrates: Inhibitor for uric acid stones and calcium phosphate stones; increases Tamm-Horsfall protein excretion.

Influencing factors: Age, sex, time of day, physical activity, and meat diet.

53 secClinical Symptoms

Nephrolothiasis: Most common symptom of urinary tract stones.

Pain:

Flank pain (radiating along the ureter toward the bladder).

Burning sensation during urination.

Dysuria: Pain upon urination.

Hematuria: Hematuria or hematuria (note: likely a repetition in original text).

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Stone Types and Causes

Hypermagnesuria: Bacterial (Proteus, Pseudomonas, etc.) → Alkalinize urine.

Hypomagnesuria: Magnesium-creatinine ratio Measured.

Cystinuria: Cystine crystals + Cysteine transport defect.

Hypocitraturia: Citrate Citrate excretion Citrate Citrate (reduced).

Stone Type

Cause

Diagnostic Test

Key Feature

Uric Acid Stones

Magnesium-creatinine ratio

Urine pH acidic

🧠Clinical Pearl

Dietary influence: Meat-heavy diets reduce citraturia, increasing risk of uric acid and calcium phosphate stones.

2 secSelected Issues in Pediatric Nephrology

25 secSymptoms

Renal colic symptoms

Asymptomatic progression of renal calculi in some cases

Recurrent abdominal pain

Vomiting

Urgency

Eating habits

Frequent use of medications

Previous urinary tract infections

Family history of renal calculi

Physical Examination

No abnormalities confirmed during asymptomatic periods

Laboratory Tests

Inflammation markers

Creatinine

Uric acid

Calcium, phosphorus, magnesium levels in urine

Alkaline phosphatase activity

Parathyroid hormone

Vitamin D metabolites

Urine tests:

Systematic general urine analysis (specific gravity, proteinuria, hematuria, leukocyturia, sediment analysis)

Urine cultures

Chest X-ray

5 minTreatment

Adequate fluid intake in daily diet (regardless of stone type):

Mineral water

Occasional fruit juices and vegetable juices

Maintain urine specific gravity <1.015

Fluid restriction only in cases of nephrogenic stones

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Key Diagnostic Tests

Lab tests:

- Levels (calcium, phosphorus, magnesium)

- Uric acid, Creatinine

- Parathyroid hormone, Vitamin D

Imaging:

- Ultrasound, Chest X-ray, Urography, Cystography, Densitometry, CT

🧠Clinical Pearl

Young children with renal calculi often present with non-specific symptoms (e.g., vomiting, diarrhea, mucus in diapers) rather than classic

renal colic.

Fluid management is critical: Avoid restriction unless stones are nephrogenic (e.g., due to diabetes insipidus).

🧠Comparison Table: Symptoms by Age Group

132 PEDIATRIA

Dietary Restrictions and Lifestyle Modifications

Dietary adjustments:

Follow a low-salt, low-animal-protein diet, high in phosphorus and purines.

Recommended and contraindicated products depend on the type of metabolic disorder (kidney stone type).

Lifestyle modifications:

Avoid sedentary lifestyle (physical activity regulates calcium metabolism physiologically).

Preventive measures:

Antibacterial prophylaxis for urinary tract infections (e.g., furagin, uroseptol).

Pharmacotherapy based on stone type:

Hypercalciuria (absorptive/nephrogenic forms): Citrates, non-organic magnesium phosphates.

Phosphate stones/hyperoxaluria: Magnesium preparations, vitamin B6.

Bladder stones: Alkalizing agents.

Cystinuria: Penicillamine, captopril, vitamin B6.

Phosphorus stones: Mannitol, acidic urine pH maintenance, aggressive bacterial infection treatment.

Surgical treatments:

ESWL (extracorporeal shock wave lithotripsy)

URS (ureterorenoscopy)

PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy)

Urinary Tract Infections in Children

Key principles for treatment:

Prioritize appropriate drug selection (symptomatic drugs, antivirals, anti-inflammatories, chemotherapeutics, antibiotics).

Antibiotic choice must be evidence-based, considering local epidemiology and pathogen resistance patterns.

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for Stone Types & Treatments:

- Hypercalciuria: Citrate + Magnesium phosphates

- Phosphate stones: Magnesium + B6

- Cystinuria: Penicillamine + Captopril + B6

- Bladder stones: Alkalizing agents

Acronym for Surgical Options:

ESWL, URS, PCNL

Clinical Pearl:

- Diet and activity are foundational in preventing kidney stones; pharmacotherapy must be tailored to the stone type.

- Antibiotic stewardship is critical in UTIs—always consider local resistance patterns.

Selected problems of pediatric nephrology

Urinary tract infections (UTIs) constitute 10–20% of all community-acquired infections (second only to respiratory infections in terms of frequency)

and approximately 40–50% of hospital-acquired infections.

In the neonatal period and in the first quarter of life, boys are more often affected due to the higher incidence of urinary tract defects; outside this

period, with the age of the child, the number of cases systematically increases among girls (the urethra is shorter and wider, located close to the anus).

Etiopathogenesis

Neonatal and early infancy period: UTI occurs via the hematogenous route

Outside this period: UTI most often develops via the ascending route (from the gastrointestinal tract – Enterobacteriaceae, from the sub-preputial

area – Proteus mirabilis)

Factors contributing to the development of UTI

Increased colonization of the external urethral opening with uropathogenic microorganisms

Change in vaginal microflora under the influence of antibiotics

Local inflammation of the vagina, vulva, and perineal skin

Constant stool contamination (lack of hygienic habits, constipation, enterobiasis)

Urinary tract defects (posterior urethral valve in boys, stenosis of the external urethral opening in girls), vesicoureteral reflux, neurogenic bladder

Catheterization, urinary tract instrumentation

Sexual activity

Factors preventing the development of UTI

Emptying the bladder frequently enough

Factors contributing to the recurrence of UTI

Metabolic risk factors for urolithiasis, urolithiasis

Long-term immunosuppressive treatment and antibiotic therapy

Diabetes, gout

Defecation disorders (constipation, stool incontinence)

Helminthiasis (mainly pinworm)

Lack of hygiene habits

Risk of recurrent UTI increases with the number of episodes:

Second episode: 50%

Third episode: 75%

The most common etiological factors of UTI

Gram-negative bacteria:

E. coli (33–90% of first-time UTI cases depending on gender and age)

Proteus spp: 30% of UTIs

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for common UTI pathogens:

E. coli (most common)

P.roteus (second most common)

Clinical Pearl:

- Gender differences in UTI risk:

- Neonates/infants: Boys more affected (due to congenital urinary tract defects)

- Older children: Girls more affected (shorter, wider urethra near anus)

Recurrence Risk:

- "2-50, 3-75": Second UTI = 50% recurrence risk; third UTI = 75% recurrence risk

Prevention Tip:

- Frequent bladder emptying is the best defense against UTI.

3 secPEDIATRIA: Urinary Tract Infections (UTI) in Children

40 secCommon Pathogens in Pediatric UTIs

Bacteria (Gram-negative):

Proteus mirabilis (causes UTI in boys; bacteria grow under the bladder)

Pseudomonas aeruginosa (causes necrotic-cystic changes; forms biofilm colonies resistant to antibiotics)

Chlamydia trachomatis or Ureaplasma urealyticum (opportunistic UTI; causes urinary tract obstruction)

Klebsiella spp. (common in newborns)

Serratia marcescens

Bacteria (Gram-positive):

Enterococcus faecalis

Staphylococcus epidermidis

Fungi:

Candida albicans (90% of fungal infections; causes bladder/kidney inflammation; may form fungal stones leading to difficulty urinating, anuria,

hydronephrosis)

Candida tropicalis

Aspergillus spp.

Viruses:

Adenovirus types 11/12 (urogenital viral infections)

Herpes simplex virus (HSV)

Protozoa:

Trichomonas vaginalis (infects newborns via mothers or young people with sexual activity)

9 secBacteria Commonly Causing UTI in Children with Urinary Tract Abnormalities

Enterococcus

Pseudomonas spp.

Staphylococcus aureus

Staphylococcus epidermidis

Haemophilus influenzae

Streptococcus group B

47 secTypes of UTI

1. Asymptomatic bacteriuria

2. True bacteriuria without clinical infection signs

3. Urine test results are normal

4. Asymptomatic UTI

5. Asymptomatic bacteriuria with dysuria

6. No clinical infection signs

7. Bladder inflammation

8. Cystitis

9. Characterized by:

Bacterialuria

Dysuria (painful urination)

Frequent urination

Long-lasting/recurring pain

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for Gram-negative pathogens:

Proteus, Pseudomonas, Klebsiella, Serratia, Chlamydia, Ureaplasma

(P-P-K-S-C-U)

Key distinction between asymptomatic bacteriuria and asymptomatic UTI:

Asymptomatic bacteriuria: No symptoms; normal urine test

Asymptomatic UTI: Dysuria present; no other infection signs

Candida albicans in UTI:

90% of fungal infections

Risk of fungal stones → hydronephrosis/anuria

Cystitis vs. bladder inflammation:

Cystitis = bacterial infection with dysuria/frequent urination

Bladder inflammation = general term; may be non-bacterial

12 secRecurrent Urinary Tract Infection (UTI)

Definitions:

2 or more UTIs in the upper urinary tract

1 upper UTI + 1 or more lower UTIs

3 or more lower UTIs

41 secKey Clinical Note

Any UTI in a child <2 years old should be treated as probable acute pyelonephritis, especially if accompanied by fever.

3 secAcute Pyelonephritis in Children: Symptoms and Diagnostics

39 secSymptoms of Acute Pyelonephritis in Children

Age Group

Symptoms

Newborns

- Fever or hypothermia

- Reluctance to suck

- Weight loss or failure to gain weight

- Vomiting, diarrhea

- Abdominal distension

- Prolonged jaundice

- Cyanosis

- CNS symptoms: restlessness, decreased muscle tone, seizures

Infants

- Fever

- Loss of appetite

- Failure to gain weight

- Vomiting, diarrhea

- Abdominal distension

- Pale or pale gray skin

- CNS symptoms: irritability or drowsiness, thrush, meningism

Older Children

- Fever, chills

- Loss of appetite

- Nausea, vomiting

- Abdominal and/or lumbar pain

- Dysuric symptoms

2 minDiagnostics

Bacteriological examination of urine – Findings include significant bacteriuria with assessment of drug sensitivity of cultured pathogens:

Supralipocentesis of the urinary bladder: Any number of G(-) bacteria or > several thousand G(+) bacteria

Catheterization of the urinary bladder: ≥ 10⁴ CFU/mL

Midstream urine: ≥ 10⁵ CFU/mL and any titer of Pseudomonas aeruginosa bacteria

Invasive tests (supralipocentesis, catheterization) are used only in exceptional circumstances.

Sterile urine collection is mandatory:

Clean the external urethral orifice and perineum before collection.

Use sterile containers or culture medium.

In infants and small children, urine is often collected in a perineal bag.

A negative result from this method is reliable, but a positive result may indicate contamination or infection.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Symptoms by Age Group

Newborns: Fever, Weight loss, Vomiting, Abdominal distension, CNS symptoms

Infants: Fever, Loss of appetite, Vomiting, Abdominal distension, Pale skin, CNS symptoms

Older Children: Fever, Loss of appetite, Nausea, Abdominal/lumbar pain, Dysuria

🧠Clinical Pearl

Urine collection in infants: A negative result from a bag-collected sample is reliable, but a positive result requires confirmation with a

2 secSelected Problems in Pediatric Nephrology

2 minUrinary Tract Infections (UTIs) and Related Issues

Key Considerations

Beyond UTIs: Focus on contamination, especially when multiple bacterial strains are present.

Diagnostic Criteria

Urine Analysis - Confirmation of Pyuria

Thresholds:

10 white blood cells (WBCs) per high-power field (HPF) in urine sediment

5 WBCs per low-power field (LPF)

Imaging Diagnosis

Indications for Imaging:

All children under 5 years old or those with:

Abnormal ultrasound (USG) results

Vesicoureteral reflux at birth

Second UTI episode

Procedures:

Ultrasound (USG) of the urinary system

Cystography with contrast dye (performed after urine culture and 4–6 weeks post-UTI treatment, followed by antibacterial ointment due to

recurrence concerns)

Technetium-99m DMSA scintigraphy (100% confirms acute pyelonephritis – focal reduction in the renal pelvis)

Laboratory Diagnosis

Assess:

Inflammatory parameters:

OB (occult blood)

CRP (C-reactive protein)

Leukocytosis

Procalcitonin

Kidney function tests

Treatment

Antibiotic Therapy

1. Children under 3 years old:

2. Hospitalization required

3. Parenteral therapy indicated

4. Children over 3 years old:

5. Hospitalization depends on general condition and outpatient treatment feasibility

6. Upper urinary tract infections (pyelonephritis):

7. Duration: 7–10 days (oral, intravenous, or sequential therapy)

8. Drugs:

Third-generation cephalosporins

Second-generation cephalosporins

Penicillins with beta-lactamase inhibitors

Aminoglycosides (especially in infants)

Fluoroquinolones (age-dependent choice)

9. Lower urinary tract infections (cystitis):

10. Duration: 3–5 days (oral therapy)

11. Drugs:

Furazolidone

Trimethoprim/sulfamethoxazole

Cephalosporins

Amoxicillin

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Pyuria Thresholds:

"10 WBCs/HPF or 5 WBCs/LPF" (remember as "10 and 5" for high and low power fields).

Imaging Protocol for UTI:

USG first, then cystography (after 4–6 weeks post-UTI), and DMSA for pyelonephritis confirmation.

Antibiotic Regimen Mnemonic:

"3-Upper, 5-Lower" (3–10 days for upper UTI; 3–5 days for lower UTI).

Drug Choices by Age:

Infants: Aminoglycosides preferred

Children >3 years: Fluoroquinolones may be used (age-dependent)

2 secPEDIATRIA | Page 138

1 minUrinary Tract Infections (UTI) in Children

Antibiotic Treatment

Recommended antibiotics:

Amoxicillin/penicillin V, fosfomycin

Ciprofloxacin (not recommended for children due to safety concerns)

Sequential antibiotic therapy is used in older children with clinical symptoms after initial treatment.

Oral treatment can be continued if clinical improvement is observed.

Asymptomatic Bacteriuria

Do not treat in children.

Preventive Measures and Surgical Interventions

Vaccine: Use Uro-Vaxom (against Escherichia coli).

Surgical treatment for:

Stones

Hydronephrosis

Other urinary tract malformations causing UTI.

Chemoprophylaxis for UTI (especially in children with urinary tract malformations, neurogenic bladder, or post-surgery):

Nitrofurantoin: 1–2 mg/kg every 24 hours (6 weeks or longer)

Trimethoprim: 1 mg/kg every 24 hours (14 days)

Cotrimoxazole: 4/20–8/40 mg/kg every 24 hours (14 days)

Sodium nitrofurantoin: 50 mg/kg every 24 hours (10–14 days)

General Prophylaxis Strategies

Hygiene habits: Prevent infections and treat UTIs promptly.

Voiding habits: Establish regular urination patterns.

Diet: Adjust for children prone to kidney stone formation.

50 secIdiopathic Nephropathy Syndrome

Definition and Epidemiology

Nephropathy syndrome: Characterized by:

Proteinuria >40 mg/m²/hour (or >50 mg/kg/24 hours)

Edema

Hypoalbuminemia (<2.5 g/dL)

Hyperlipidemia

Incidence:

New cases: 2–7/100,000/year

Annual incidence (including recurrences): 16/100,000 children

Etiopathogenesis

Most common in children (77% of cases).

Cause: Unknown (idiopathic).

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Nephropathy Syndrome Features

PEEH

- Proteinuria

- Edema

- Elevated lipids (hyperlipidemia)

- Hypoalbuminemia

🧠Clinical Pearl

Asymptomatic bacteriuria in children does not require treatment to avoid antibiotic resistance.

Sequential therapy is key for UTI management in older children with clinical symptoms.

2 secNephrotic Syndrome in Pediatrics

14 secCauses

Primary glomerular inflammation of the kidneys

Secondary glomerulopathies

Infections

Neoplastic diseases

Drugs

Toxic and allergic kidney glomerulus damage

Thrombosis of veins

Peak incidence: Ages 2–5

Boys are twice as likely to be affected

9 secMorphological Variants (Idiopathic Nephrotic Syndrome)

1. Submicroscopic glomerular inflammation (minimal changes) – Most frequent

2. Mesangial proliferative glomerulonephritis

3. Segmental sclerosing glomerulonephritis

24 secClinical Symptoms

Edema

Sudden onset

Often associated with: respiratory tract infection, insect bite, allergic reaction

Systemic signs

Pallor of skin and mucous membranes

Weight gain

Abdominal swelling

Urinary abnormalities

Proteinuria (due to hypovolemia caused by proteinuria)

Hematuria (rarely)

Abdominal pain (hypovolemia and intestinal ischemia – colicky pain)

Vomiting

Diarrhea

Other

Severe thirst (hypovolemia and renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system activation)

39 secTreatment

1. First-line therapy

2. Glucocorticoids in a standardized regimen for 6 months

3. Outcomes

4. Remission achieved in ~90% of patients

5. ~70% of patients experience relapses

Glucocorticoid therapy for 6 months is the gold standard for idiopathic nephrotic syndrome in children.

1 secPEDIATRIA

35 secIndications for Kidney Biopsy and Alkylating Agents

Steroid resistance

Steroid dependence

Frequent relapses of disease

Significant side effects of glucocorticoids

Contraindications to glucocorticoid use

Treatment options:

- Cyclophosphamide:

- Dose: 2 mg/kg mc/24 hours

- Duration: 8 weeks

- Chlorambucil:

- Dose: <0.15 mg/kg mc/24 hours

- Duration: 8 weeks

- Cyclosporine (if ineffective):

- Dose: 6 mg/kg mc/24 hours

- Blood drug levels: 100–150 ng/ml

Key requirements:

- Kidney biopsy required before cyclophosphamide therapy if glucocorticoid verification has not been performed.

- Remission rate: 80% of cases.

4 secDietary Management

Specific guidelines not detailed in this section.

1 minDefinitions of Clinical Signs

Remission

Absence or trace (trace in urine) proteinuria lasting at least 3 consecutive days.

Relapse

Appearance of proteinuria (>1+ in urine) lasting at least 3 consecutive days, occurring at least three times within a week.

Glucocorticoid Sensitivity

Early response:

Clinical and biochemical remission achieved within the first 4 weeks of initial treatment.

Late response:

Remission achieved after the next 4 weeks.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics for Definitions

Remission: Remember Remission = Remove Proteinuria for 3 days.

Lapse: Late Lapse = Late Relapse = 3 days, 3 times in a week.

Glucocorticoid Sensitivity: Get Sensitive in 4 weeks (Early) or Later (Late).

2 secSelected Problems in Children's Nephrology

36 secRelapse Definitions in Glucocorticoid-Sensitive Nephrotic Syndrome

1. Frequent relapses

2. At least 2 relapses within 6 months

3. Or 3 relapses per year

4. Glucocorticoid dependence

5. At least 2 relapses during reduced glucocorticoid dose

6. Or relapse within 2 weeks after treatment completion

7. Primary steroid resistance

8. No response to initial 8-week glucocorticoid treatment

9. Persistent (established) steroid resistance

10. No response to long-term glucocorticoid use

11. Relapse after remission achieved with another drug

12. Secondary steroid resistance

13. Resistance develops in a child initially sensitive to glucocorticoids

14 secComplications of Nephrotic Syndrome

Secondary to:

Edema

Hypoproteinemia

Hyperfibrinogenemia

Secondary nephrosclerosis (due to coagulation inhibitors)

Systemic complications:

Hypovolemic shock

Non-inflammatory renal insufficiency

Infections (often generalized)

Bone marrow disorders

Hormonal disturbances (due to protein-bound hormones)

3 minAcute Glomerulonephritis (AGN)

Key Features

Sudden onset of:

Edema

Hypertension

Hematuria or pyuria

Proteinuria

Associated with:

Respiratory tract infections

Skin infections caused by group A β-hemolytic streptococcus

Epidemiology

More common in children than adults

Linked to streptococcal infections (including... [text truncated])

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics for Relapse Definitions

FREQUENT RELAPSES: "2 in 6 months, 3 in a year"

STERIOD RESISTANCE:

Primary: "No response in 8 weeks"

Persistent: "Long-term failure, relapse after other drug"

Secondary: "Resistance after initial sensitivity"

🧠AGN Clinical Pearls

"Hematuria, Hypertension, Edema" (HHE) – classic triad

Post-streptococcal origin – focus on recent infections

Children > adults – key epidemiologic point

142 PEDIATRIA

Streptococcus β-hemolyticus grupa A

- Very common among children

- Most commonly affects children over 3 years of age (diagnosis in younger children is rare)

- Peak incidence: preschool age (7–10 years)

Etiopathogenesis

Precipitating infections: Respiratory or skin infections caused by "nephritogenic" streptococcus types

Time to OPKZN development:

Tonsillitis: 5–21 days

Skin inflammation: 1–3 weeks

Pathogenesis mechanism:

Immunologic complexes (streptococcal antigens + IgG antibodies + complement components) deposit in submucosal layers of tonsils/pharyngeal

area

Transmission: Sporadic or epidemic

Clinical Symptoms

Sporadic form:

- Full-body symptoms

- Swelling

- Lymphadenopathy

- Purulent tonsillitis/pharyngitis

- Pus

- Acute kidney insufficiency signs

Epidemic form:

- Occurs during streptococcal infections

- Mild course with scabbing

- Typically only purulent tonsillitis

- Post-healing phase:

- Sudden abdominal pain

- Lymph node area pain

- Headaches, nausea, vomiting

- Decreased diuresis

- Swelling (often with lymphadenopathy)

Swelling (90% of cases):

- Severity varies (mild periorbital swelling to swollen tonsils)

- Resolves within 2 weeks (usually 3–5 days)

Lymphadenopathy (90% of cases):

- Elevated systolic/diastolic blood pressure

- Persistent for...

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for OPKZN timeline:

- Tonsillitis: 5–21 days

- Skin: 1–3 weeks

Comparison: Sporadic vs. Epidemic Forms

| Feature | Sporadic Form | Epidemic Form |

|----------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------------|

| Symptoms | Full-body, kidney signs | Mild, scabbing |

| Common infection | Tonsillitis, skin | Streptococcal outbreaks |

| Post-healing phase | Acute kidney insufficiency | Abdominal pain, swelling |

Clinical Pearl:

- Swelling and lymphadenopathy are hallmark signs in 90% of OPKZN cases.

- Monitor blood pressure changes in lymphadenopathy cases.

3 secWybrane problemy nefrologii wieku dziecięcego | 143

25 secClinical Presentation

Onset: 1 day to 2–3 weeks; rarely signs of encephalopathy with hypertension (symptoms: severe headaches, nausea, vomiting, visual disturbances,

tremors, difficulty swallowing).

Hematuria:

Occurs in ~30% of cases; lasts up to 2 weeks, then progresses to hemoglobinuria (characteristic of OPKZN), which may persist for 6–12 months.

Complications: Hemoglobinuria and anemia → acute kidney failure with hyperazotemia, hyperkalemia, and metabolic acidosis.

39 secLaboratory Diagnostics

Urinalysis:

Hematuria with fresh and degenerated RBCs (often dysmorphic RBCs on contrast-enhanced microscopy).

Proteinuria: Unstable sign of OPKZN; rarely <3 g/24 hours (mainly albuminuria), rarely leading to nephrotic syndrome.

Leukocyturia: Present primarily in early illness (alkaline urine).

Crystalline and granular casts: Seen in severe proteinuria (present in all OPKZN cases).

Serum markers:

↑ ASO titer (peaks 4–6 weeks after OPKZN) in all OPKZN patients, after glomerulonephritis, and in ~50% of patients with skin inflammation.

↑ IgG (>50% of cases), ↑ IgA (~30% of cases).

↓ Complement levels:

C3 and total hemolytic complement activity.

C5 and properdin.

18 secTreatment

Bed rest: Continue until swelling, hypertension, and hematuria resolve.

Glomerulonephritis:

Penicillamine: 50–100 mg/kg/day (divided into 3–4 doses; max 3 g/day for 10 days).

Alternatives for penicillin allergy:

Erythromycin: 1.2 g/day (3–4 divided doses).

Infants <2 months: 15–50 mg/kg/day (3–4 divided doses) or cephalosporins.

34 secMemory Aid

Mnemonic for OPKZN (Post-streptococcal Glomerulonephritis)

Opening: Onset 1–3 weeks after strep infection.

Proteinuria: Present but <3 g/24h.

Kidney: Kidney failure with hyperazotemia.

Zn: Zn (not a mnemonic—OPKZN is a Polish acronym for "nephritis post-infection streptococcal").

Key Lab Findings in OPKZN

Parameter

Finding

Complement C3

↓ (key diagnostic marker)

ASO titer

↑ (peaks 4–6 weeks post-infection)

Proteinuria

<3 g/24h (unlike nephrotic syndrome)

RBC morphology

Dysmorphic RBCs (not intact)

Clinical Pearl

Hemoglobinuria (not hematuria) is a hallmark of OPKZN, distinguishing it from other glomerulonephritides.

1 secPEDIATRIA

5 secLiquification of Inflammatory Foci

Occurs in the nasal-pharyngeal cavity, nasopharynx, and sinuses.

12 secDietary Recommendations

Limit fluid intake and salt consumption (depending on edema and body mass).

Reduce protein content in the diet (in cases of hyperazotemia).

Limit potassium-containing products (in cases of hyperkalemia).

2 secSymptomatic Treatment

Reduce hypertension and edema.

5 secAcute Renal Failure

In a small number of cases, dialysis is required.

22 secPrognosis

Good prognosis:

Symptoms subside within weeks or months in ~98% of cases.

Epidemic form of OPKZN:

~1% of children develop chronic kidney insufficiency or persistent hematuria.

Sporadic cases of OPKZN:

May lead to sclerosing glomerulonephritis, hypertension, and reduced proteinuria (hypocomplementemia lasting >6 months).

Recurrence of acute pyelonephritic renal inflammation:

Rare (0.7–7% of cases).

53 secHypertension in Children and Adolescents

Definition of Hypertension (HT)

Hypertension is defined as the mean value of systolic blood pressure (SCT) and/or diastolic blood pressure (DBP) exceeding age, sex, and

height-specific thresholds.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Dietary Restrictions:

Limit Fluids, Salt, and K (for hyperkalemia).

Prognosis Key Points:

98% resolve quickly; 1% risk chronic kidney issues in epidemic OPKZN.

Sporadic OPKZN may lead to sclerosing glomerulonephritis and hypocomplementemia.

Hypertension in Kids:

Use age-specific blood pressure charts for diagnosis.

Monitor for target organ damage (e.g., renal, cardiac).
