1 secPEDIATRICS

28 secHIV Infection in Neonates and Pregnant Women

Diagnosis Methods

CD4 and CD8 lymphocyte count determination

Virus cultivation

Detection of viral antigen (HIV-1-p24) – core protein

Detection of viral RNA by PCR

Anti-HIV-1 antibody detection (ELISA or Western Blot):

Indicates maternal infection only (antibodies persist in the child until 6–18 months of age)

Diagnostic Follow-Up

Second HIV-RNA, p24Ag, and viral culture tests should be performed between 6–8 weeks of age

51 secTreatment

For Pregnant Women with HIV+

HAART (highly active antiretroviral therapy) is recommended

Neonatal Treatment

Zidovudine (retrovir):

Administered intravenously to the mother during labor

Administered to the newborn immediately after birth

Treatment Initiation Criteria

Depends on maternal viral load at 36 weeks of pregnancy:

If viral load is below detection limit:

Use zidovudine (AZT, retrovir) and lamivudine (3TC)

Continue treatment until 4 weeks of age

If viral load is unknown or detectable:

Add nevirapine (viramune) to the newborn for first 2 weeks

Vaccination Guidelines

Hepatitis B vaccination:

Administered within 12–24 hours of birth

BCG vaccination:

Contraindicated in infants of HIV-positive mothers

Decision to vaccinate made after ruling out HIV infection

Additional Considerations

Method of pregnancy termination depends on maternal viral load at 36 weeks

Breastfeeding is prohibited

36 secPrevention

Prevention of Pneumocystis carinii infection until 1 year of age:

Trimethoprim + sulfamethoxazole

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics and Clinical Pearls

"6–8 Weeks Rule": Repeat HIV-RNA/p24Ag tests between 6–8 weeks of age to confirm infection

"AZT + 3TC": Standard regimen if maternal viral load is undetectable

"No BCG, Yes Hep B": HIV-positive infants avoid BCG but receive Hep B vaccine within 12–24 hours

"Nevirapine for 2 Weeks": Added if maternal viral load is unknown or detectable

"Trimethoprim-Sulfamethoxazole": Prevents Pneumocystis carinii until 1 year of age

"No Breastfeeding": Absolute contraindication in HIV-positive mothers

2 secSelected Issues in Pediatric Nephrology

2 minBlood Pressure Classification in Children

Definitions and Classification Criteria

Hypertension

At least three measurements ≥ 95th centile for sex, age, and height.

Prehypertensive state

Average of systolic (SCT) and/or diastolic (RCT) blood pressure from at least three independent measurements:

≥ 90th centile but < 95th centile for sex, age, and height.

White coat hypertension

Blood pressure (CT) > 95th centile measured in the doctor’s office, but normal outside healthcare facilities.

Normal arterial blood pressure

SCT and RCT < 90th centile for sex, age, and height.

Blood Pressure Measurement Guidelines

Children ≥ 3 years of age

- Measure CT during every doctor visit.

Children < 3 years of age

- Measure CT in cases:

- Very low birth weight or prematurity

- Congenital heart defect

- Recurring urinary tract infections, hematuria, proteinuria, or albuminuria

- Kidney disease or renal-metabolic disorder

- Splenectomy or organ transplant

- Aggressive tumor

- Use of medications that may increase CT

- Diseases that can progress with NT (neurological deterioration?)

- Signs of increased intracranial pressure

Measurement Protocol

Preparation:

Avoid stimulants and drinks before examination.

Child should sit calmly for 5 minutes before measurement.

Positioning:

Child sits with back supported, feet on the floor, right arm at heart level.

Use right arm for measurement.

Cuff requirements:

Cuff should cover 80–100% of arm circumference.

Cuff width must be ≥ 40% of arm circumference.

Too narrow cuffs increase measurement errors.

Interpretation:

Compare CT results with normative centile grids for sex, age, and height.

Classification of arterial hypertension depends on sex, age, and height.

Memory Aid

Mnemonic for classification thresholds:

"95 for Hypertension, 90 for Prehypertension"

"White Coat = Office High, Normal Elsewhere"

Cuff size rule:

"80–100% coverage, 40% width" (avoid narrow cuffs).

Key conditions for CT in children < 3 years:

"PREMATURE, HEART, URINE, KIDNEY, TRANSPLANT, TUMOR, DRUGS, NEURO"

(Prematurity, Heart defects, Urinary issues, Kidney disease, Transplant, Tumor, Drugs, Neurological signs).

Clinical pearl:

"Normal CT = <90th centile; always use right arm for consistency."

2 secClassification of Blood Pressure and Management

8 secNormal Blood Pressure

Criteria:

SCT and RCT < 90 centiles

Management:

Measure blood pressure during the next planned doctor's visit

26 secPrehypertension

Criteria:

SCT or RCT > 90 centiles but < 95 centiles for sex, age, height

In youth: CT > 120/80 mmHg (even if below 90 centiles)

Management:

Measure blood pressure within the next 6 months

In case of obesity:

Recommend weight reduction

Dietary modifications

Physical activity recommendations

Pharmacological treatment not recommended unless other indications (e.g., chronic kidney disease, heart failure, left ventricular hypertrophy)

31 secStage I Hypertension

Criteria:

SCT or RCT ≥ 95 centiles

Management:

Measure blood pressure within the next 1–2 weeks (earlier if symptomatic)

If reduced blood pressure persists during two subsequent measurements:

Start diagnostic procedures

Refer to a relevant center within one month

In case of obesity:

Recommend weight reduction

Dietary modifications

Physical activity recommendations

Start pharmacological treatment if:

Overt hypertension

Internal organ damage (heart, kidneys, retinas, blood vessels)

Type I or II diabetes

Other diseases (see prehypertension)

56 secStage II Hypertension

Criteria:

SCT or RCT > 5 mmHg above 95 centiles

Management:

Start diagnostic procedures or refer to a relevant center within one week (immediately if symptomatic)

In case of obesity:

Recommend weight reduction

Dietary modifications

Physical activity recommendations

Start pharmacological treatment (monotherapy or polytherapy)

page 76

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics for Blood Pressure Stages

Stage I: "≥95 centiles" → "Nine-Five" (remember the threshold)

Stage II: ">5 mmHg above 95" → "Five Over Nine-Five"

🧠Key Management Differences

Stage

Pharmacological Treatment?

Referral Timeframe

Prehypertension

No (unless comorbidities)

6 months

Stage I

Yes (if indicated)

1–2 weeks (earlier if symptomatic)

Stage II

Yes

1 week (immediate if symptomatic)

🧠Clinical Pearl

Obesity management is a universal recommendation across all stages (weight reduction, diet, exercise).

Pharmacological treatment is mandatory in Stage II but conditional in Stage I (based on organ damage or comorbidities).

7 secSevere Hypertension

Condition where systolic blood pressure exceeds 99th percentile for age by at least 30 mmHg.

12 secSudden and Urgent Hypertensive States

SCT or RCT greater than 5 mmHg above the 95th percentile with associated symptoms in both the patient and their environment requires

hospitalization and treatment.

2 minDiagnosis of Hypertension

1. Identifying Known Causes of Hypertension

Medical history:

Family burden NT, risk factors; problems with sleepiness during the day, excessive daytime sleepiness, snoring, diet, smoking, alcohol consumption.

Physical examination:

Measurement of blood pressure on all extremities.

Blood cell morphology:

Searching for anemia suggesting kidney disease.

Urine test:

Creatinine, electrolytes, and pH in a random sample; general urine analysis and urinalysis – searching for signs of urinary tract infections or other

kidney diseases.

Abdominal ultrasound (USG):

Searching for renal scars, congenital abnormalities of the kidneys or abnormal kidney size.

2. Excluding Coexisting Conditions

Searching for hyperlipidemia and other metabolic disorders:

Lipid profile; blood glucose test in fasting state and potentially glucose tolerance test.

Screening tests for substances that may cause NT (if history suggests it).

Polysomnography:

Identification of obstructive sleep apnea, if history indicates frequent or loud snoring.

3. Identifying Organ Damage Due to Hypertension

Echocardiography:

Searching for left ventricular hypertrophy (the most significant sign of organ damage in children with hypertension).

Eye fundus examination:

Searching for changes in retinal vessels.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Diagnosis Steps:

IEIO

- Identify known causes

- Exclude coexisting conditions

- Identify organ damage

🧠Comparison Table: Hypertension Criteria

Condition

Blood Pressure Threshold

Additional Criteria

Severe Hypertension

Systolic > 99th percentile + 30 mmHg

No specific symptoms mentioned

Urgent Hypertensive State

SCT/RTC > 5 mmHg above 95th percentile

Symptoms in patient and environment; hospitalization required

🧠Clinical Pearl:

Left ventricular hypertrophy on echocardiography is the most significant sign of organ damage in children with hypertension.

46 sec4. Other examinations depending on indications

Continuous monitoring of blood pressure

Necessity to know daily blood pressure profile and diagnosis of "white coat" hypertension

In doubtful cases, measurements of blood pressure at lower limbs

Determination of renin activity ratio (RAR)

In children with NT I stage and children/adolescents with NT II stage

Severe NT in relatives

Imaging studies of renal vessels

Renoscopy or angiography without captopril administration

Doppler study

Arteriography: digital subtraction angiography or classic angiography

TSH, rT3 and possibly rT4; USG thyroid gland

Diagnosis of abnormalities in the field of hormonal function

Assessment of steroid levels and amines in urine

Diagnosis NT caused by steroids and pheochromocytoma

Genetic testing for low-renin forms of NT

Age-Specific Causes of Hypertension in Children

Children aged 15–18 years

Idiopathic hypertension

Nephrogenic hypertension (nephrotic syndrome and obstruction, inflammatory lesions, glomerulopathy)

Hormonal causes

Vascular-nephrogenic hypertension

Heavy metal poisoning

Key Epidemiological and Diagnostic Notes

Acquired nephrotic syndrome (NT) accounts for 60–80% of cases in children.

Primary NT is rarely diagnosed before the 6th year of life.

In newborns, infants, and young children, NT can be suspected by finding elevated systolic blood pressure values.

- Café au lait spots

15–18 years: "Idio (idiopathic) + Nephro + Heavy Metals"

Clinical Pearl: In children, elevated systolic BP is a red flag for primary nephrotic syndrome or vascular hypertension.

Treatment

1. Eliminating risk factors (active and passive smoking, alcohol consumption, drug use).

Memory Aid

- Mnemonic for Pheochromocytoma Symptoms: "POPS" – Pallor, Orthostatic hypotension, Palpitations, Sweating.

- Compare Neuroblastoma vs. Pheochromocytoma:

- Neuroblastoma: Tachycardia + abdominal mass.

- Pheochromocytoma: Orthostatic hypotension + episodic hypertension.

- Williams Syndrome: Remember the "Elf Face" and supravalvular aortic stenosis (ECG finding).

- Treatment Mnemonic: "ERASE" – Eliminate risks, Reduce weight, Address compression, Support renal function, Educate patients.

Wybrane problemy nefrologii wieku dziecięcego | 151

Diet and Lifestyle Recommendations

Dietary modifications:

Increase intake of vegetables, fruits, and unsaturated fats.

Reduce sodium consumption.

Limit saturated fat intake.

Consume larger amounts of foods rich in potassium, magnesium, unsaturated fatty acids, and protein.

Physical activity:

Regular aerobic exercises (primary focus).

Weight-bearing exercises (secondary focus).

No contraindications for sportive activities except uncontrolled stage II hypertension.

Pharmacological Treatment Indications

Indications for antihypertensive therapy:

Symptomatic or secondary nephrotic syndrome (NT).

Failure of lifestyle modification/diet after 6 months of observation.

Organ damage (heart, kidneys, blood vessels).

Coexisting diabetes.

Severe, symptomatic NT: Immediate initiation of antihypertensive drugs.

Pharmacotherapy Goals and Principles

Goal of treatment:

Reduce central blood pressure (CT) to < 95th percentile.

If other diseases or risk factors are present: < 90th percentile.

Patient tolerance of reduced CT values must be ensured.

Drug selection:

Start with monotherapy from one of these groups:

ACE inhibitors (e.g., captopril, enalapril, perindopril, lisinopril).

Angiotensin receptor blockers.

Beta-blockers (e.g., propranolol, atenolol, metoprolol, acebutolol).

Calcium channel blockers.

Diuretics.

Verify drug registration for pediatric use before administration.

Antihypertensive Drug Groups Used in Ambulatory Treatment

ACE inhibitors:

Captopril

Enalapril

Perindopril

Lisinopril

Beta-adrenergic receptor blockers:

Propranolol

Atenolol

Metoprolol

Acebutolol

Alpha-adrenergic receptor blockers:

Prazosin

Doxazosin

Terazosin

Memory Aid

Mnemonic for drug groups: ACE-BETA-ALPHA (ACE inhibitors, beta-blockers, alpha-blockers).

Target CT values:

Normal range: < 95th percentile.

High-risk patients: < 90th percentile.

Clinical pearl: Only uncontrolled stage II hypertension is a contraindication to sportive activities.

Drug registration check: Always confirm pediatric approval for antihypertensive medications.

2 secSelected Problems in Childhood Neurology

27 secBrain Damage in Children (MPD – paralisis cerebralis infantum)

Definition: Not a strictly defined disease but a complex of heterogeneous etiologically and clinically manifest symptoms.

Characteristics: Non-progressive disorders of central nervous system motor neuron function, developing during pregnancy and childbirth.

Incidence: Estimated at 2/1000 live births.

Diagnosis: Made in children over 2 years old.

Before 2 years of age: Developmental disorders (central nervous system origin – DMPC).

5 minEtiological Factors

Prenatal

Congenital malformations of the central nervous system

Premature birth

Intrauterine infections:

TORCH (Toxoplasmosis, Other [syphilis, varicella-zoster], Rubella, Cytomegalovirus, Herpes simplex)

Listeriosis

Genetic factors: Most often damage to the central nervous system between 26–34 weeks of gestation

Perinatal

Asphyxia

Hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy

Hyperbilirubinemia

Hypoglycemia

Postnatal

Episodes of seizures in history

Vascular diseases

Maternal Risk Factors

Diabetes mellitus

Thyroid disorders

Prolonged estrogen use during pregnancy

Exposure to toxic substances:

Alcohol

Drugs

Tobacco

Pregnancy and Neonatal Factors

Intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR)

Birth asphyxia

Neonatal seizures

Intracranial hemorrhage

Hypotonia

Respiratory distress syndrome

Polycythemia

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics for Etiological Factors

Prenatal: Congenital malformations, Premature birth, Intrauterine infections (TORCH), Genetic factors

Perinatal: Asphyxia, Hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy, Hyperbilirubinemia, Hypoglycemia

Postnatal: Seizures, Vascular diseases

Maternal: Diabetes, Thyroid disorders, Estrogen use, Toxic substances (Alcohol, Drugs, Tobacco)

Pregnancy/Neonatal: IUGR, Birth asphyxia, Neonatal seizures, Intracranial hemorrhage, Hypotonia, Respiratory distress, Polycythemia

🧠Key Terms

MPD: Brain damage in children (paralisis cerebralis infantum)

DMPC: Developmental disorders (central nervous system origin) in children under 2 years

🧠Comparison Table: Etiological Categories

Key Factors

Neonatal

Birth asphyxia, neonatal seizures, intracranial hemorrhage, hypotonia

Clinical Pearl: MPD is diagnosed after 2 years of age, while DMPC refers to developmental disorders in children under 2 years. Always

consider TORCH infections in prenatal etiology.

Clinical Forms of Childhood Brain Injury (Ingram's Classification)

1. Unilateral Hemiplegia Spastic (Hemiplegia Spastica)

Etiology

Prenatal factors: Obstruction, bleeding, umbilical artery compression

Perinatal factors: Asphyxia, infection

Recognition

DMPC III-IV at approximately 4–5 months of age

Affected Side

Upper limb and lower limb on the same side

Neurological Examination

Asymmetry in Moro reflex

Stronger grasp reflex in the affected upper limb

Absent deep tendon reflexes on the affected side

Asymmetry in movement

Age-Related Features (5–7 years)

Swimming movements (50%)

Sensory disturbances (25%)

Other Features

Seizures (34–60%)

Spinal deformities due to delayed limb development

Normal mental development

2. Bilateral Hemiplegia (Hemiplegia Bilateralis)

Etiology

Prenatal factors: Asphyxia, hypoxic-ischemic injury

Developmental brain disorders

Most Common Form

MPD (likely a typo or abbreviation for a specific condition; retained as given)

Neurological Examination

Greater spasticity in upper limbs than lower limbs

Upper limbs: Always flexed and adducted; hands tucked into the axilla

Age 6–8 months: Walking difficulties, lower limb paresis, hypotonia along the head-to-tail axis, persistent postures, microcephaly

Clinical Variants

Spasticity forms: West syndrome (50–90%)

Other Features

Dysarthria (speech difficulties)

Delayed language development

Profound intellectual impairment

Children often remain in the stage of lying

3. Bilateral Spastic Paraplegia (Diplegia Spastica)

Etiology

Perinatal factors: Asphyxia

🧠Memory Aid

Key Mnemonic for Ingram's Classification:

Unilateral Hemiplegia: Upper and Lower limb on the Same side

Bilateral Hemiplegia: Both limbs affected, West syndrome common

Diplegia: Dual (lower) limb involvement, Aspiration (asphyxia) as main cause

Clinical Pearl:

West syndrome is strongly associated with bilateral hemiplegia (50–90% prevalence).

Swimming movements (50%) and sensory disturbances (25%) are hallmark signs in unilateral hemiplegia after age 5–7 years.

Diplegia is characterized by asphyxia as the primary cause and lower limb dominance in spasticity.

1.10 Listeriosis (Listeriosis)

The disease caused by gram-positive bacilli of Listeria monocytogenes.

Routes of Infection in Newborns

- Blood-borne

- Umbilical cord

Clinical Symptoms

Symptoms during the neonatal period:

- Jaundice

- Acute respiratory distress

- Hepatosplenomegaly

- Cerebrospinal meningitis

Diagnosis

Bacteriological tests (blood cultures, cerebrospinal fluid, vaginal discharge, amniotic fluid)

PCR test

Treatment

Ampicillin with aminoglycosides. Erythromycin and cotrimoxazole are also effective.

1.11 Congenital Tuberculosis (Tuberculosis Congenita)

This is an infectious disease caused by tubercle bacilli (Mycobacterium tuberculosis). Infection usually occurs at the end of pregnancy (third trimester).

Etiopathogenesis

Routes of Infection

- Blood-borne – via umbilical vein

- Aspiration – swallowing amniotic fluid during delivery

Memory Aid

- Listeriosis Mnemonics:

- Jaundice, Acute respiratory distress, Hepatosplenomegaly, Cerebrospinal meningitis (J-A-H-C)

- Treatment: Ampicillin + Aminoglycosides (A+A)

Congenital Tuberculosis Clinical Pearl:

Infection occurs in the third trimester via blood-borne (umbilical vein) or aspiration (amniotic fluid).

Comparison Table:

| Disease | Common Route of Infection in Newborns | Key Diagnostic Test | Primary Treatment |

|-----------------------|----------------------------------------|-----------------------------|----------------------------------|

| Listeriosis | Blood-borne, umbilical cord | Blood culture, PCR | Ampicillin + aminoglycosides |

| Congenital Tuberculosis | Blood-borne (umbilical vein), aspiration | Blood culture, PCR | Isoniazid, rifampin (standard TB regimen) |

2 secSelected Problems of Child Neurology

9 secKey Statistics

50% of cases concern premature infants and newborns with low birth weight.

Recognition of DMPC by 9 months of age.

25 secNeurological Examination Findings

Lower limb hypotonia (more pronounced than upper limbs).

Preserved plantar reflexes and postural reflexes.

Delayed development of flexor reflexes.

Hyperactive deep tendon reflexes.

Straightening of lower limbs, inversion of the feet, equinovarus position when standing, walking on toes.

Hearing and speech disorders (dysarthria), visual impairment (amblyopia).

Rare MDP (16–27% of cases).

Intellectual development within normal limits, rarely deep mental retardation.

28 sec4. Ataxic Form

Etiology

Prenatal factors and genetic conditions.

OUN defects (partial or complete dysgenesis of the cerebellum).

Clinical Features

Most common form: MDP (5–10% of cases).

Neurological examination:

Generalized hypotonia in newborns.

Significant delay in flexor reflexes.

Absent postural and locomotor reflexes.

Tremors.

Unsteady gait, normal gait pattern develops between 2nd and 5th year of life.

Coordination disorders, unstable ocular movements.

Speech disorders: slow speech, staccato.

Normal intellectual development.

3 min5. Post-Pyramidal Form (Dyskinesia)

Etiology

Hypokalemia, hyperbilirubinemia, neonatal hemolytic disease.

Clinical Features

Rare MDP form.

Neurological examination:

Variable post-pyramidal movements (e.g., dystonic movements, atetotic, flaccid).

Progressive hypotonia.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Forms of DMPC

Ataxic → Attention to cerebellar defects.

Post-Pyramidal → Pay attention to dystonia and hypotonia.

🧠Clinical Pearl

DMPC (Dysmyelination of the posterior columns) is most commonly recognized by 9 months of age and often presents with lower limb

hypotonia and equinovarus deformity.

156 PEDIATRIA

Key Features of MPD (Movement Disorders in Pediatrics)

Muscle tension increases after 2 years of life with deep reflexes dominance.

Common sensory deficiencies:

Hearing loss

Visual impairment

Gaze deviation

Speech disorders

Intellectual development: Usually normal.

Mixed Forms of MPD (Neurological Disorders in Two or More MPD Forms)

Most common group of MPDs. Examples include:

1. Severe MPD with rigidity (rigiditas):

2. Bilateral hemiplegia and spastic diplegia

3. Characteristic head posture

4. Facial asymmetry

5. Intellectual impairment

6. Spastic diplegia with dystonia:

7. Spastic diplegia + dystonia

8. Dyskinetic forms:

9. Hyperkinetic or hypokinetic with ataxia and/or choreoatetosis

10. Hypotonic-atatic form:

11. Hypotonic type with tremors

12. Ball-like movements

13. Involuntary tremors

14. Torsional dystonia and others.

Common Symptoms Accompanying MPD

Intellectual development delay: 60%

Behavioral disorders: >50%

Speech disorders: 50%

Visual impairment: 50%

Seizures: 20–35%

Hearing loss: 25%

Psychological Symptoms in MPD

Emotional disturbances

Irritability

Explosiveness

Behavioral disorders: 50%

Adaptive disorders

Neurological syndromes

Psychotrophic syndromes:

Reactive syndromes

Depressive syndromes

Early childhood autism

Additional Symptoms Accompanying MPD

Polykinesis

Esophageal reflux

Swallowing difficulties

Increased standing reflex

Paraplegia

Orthopedic deformities:

Clubfoot

Scoliosis

Diagnosis of MPD

Based on:

1. Prenatal and perinatal history (assessment of MPD risk factors)

2. Assessment of psychomotor development in infants:

- Reaching developmental milestones

- Testing postural reflexes

- Evaluating standing posture and balance reactions

3. Comprehensive neurological examination of the child

🧠Memory Aid

MPD Acronym: Movement Problems in Development (helps recall focus on motor and developmental issues).

Symptoms Table:

| Symptom | Frequency |

|-------------------------|-----------|

| Intellectual delay | 60% |

| Behavioral disorders | >50% |

| Speech disorders | 50% |

| Visual impairment | 50% |

| Seizures | 20–35% |

| Hearing loss | 25% |

Mixed Forms Mnemonic: Rigiditas, Dystonia, Dyskinesia, Hypotonia, Torsion → R.D.D.H.T. (remember key forms).

Key Diagnostic Steps: History → Developmental Milestones → Neurological Exam (use H-D-N as a sequence).

6 minTreatment (Leczenie)

Multidisciplinary Team Approach

Individually tailored for each child, with collaboration among:

Pediatricians

Neurologists

Orthopedists

Physiotherapists

Speech therapists

Pedagogues

Psychologists

Audiologists

Ophthalmologists

Parent and Child Engagement

Support spontaneous activity:

Encourage contact between child and mother

Use tactile stimulation

Active parental participation in skill-improvement programs:

Proper handling of the child:

Correct lifting and carrying techniques

Reduce inappropriate postural reflexes

Promote correct control posture in movement patterns

Feeding:

Use positions that optimize tongue and lip activity

Dressing/undressing:

Gradually transition to independent execution

Positioning:

Monitor proper posture during rest, play, and learning

Rehabilitation Exercises

Method selection depends on:

Complexity of the child’s problems

Caregiver acceptance and cooperation

Specific techniques:

J. Ayres’ Method (Sensory Integration):

Corrects inappropriate posture, motor coordination, speech, visual perception, auditory function, and cognitive abilities

NDT (Bobath Method):

Reduces inappropriate postural reflexes

Normalizes muscle tone

Develops voluntary movements

Exercises must avoid spastic reflexes and align with natural motor development

V. Vojta’s Method:

Improves motor skills in central coordination disorders via repeated practice

Focuses on developing correct movement patterns

Challenging for caregivers to master

Memory Aid

Mnemonic for rehabilitation methods:

Ayres → Sensory Integration

Bobath → Normalizing Motor Development

Vojta → Voluntary Movement Patterns

Comparison Table:

Method

Key Focus

Primary Goal

Ayres

Sensory integration

Correct posture, coordination, speech

Bobath

Postural reflex normalization

Voluntary movement, muscle tone

Vojta

Repeated practice of movement patterns

Central coordination improvement

Clinical Pearl:

Bobath and Vojta methods both target motor development but differ in approach: Bobath emphasizes normalization, while Vojta relies on

repetition of specific patterns.

Therapeutic Methods in Pediatric Neurology

Castillo-Morales method

Targets regulation of facial and oral muscles (respiratory muscles, speech-related muscles, facial muscles, tongue, throat, larynx, and diaphragm).

Petö method

Guided teaching approach requiring specialized center care.

Focus: Improving functional skills and independence.

Not suitable for infants or children with severe psychomotor disabilities.

Pharmacological treatment

Primary use: Muscle relaxants to facilitate improvement.

Specific therapies:

Botulinum toxin for spasticity management.

Baclofen infusions via pump devices.

Electrical stimulation (Huffschmidt method – electrical toning).

Chemical toning.

Adjunct therapies

Musicotherapy.

Hydrotherapy.

Occupational therapy.

Orthopedic care.

DRYHEARTS in Children

Definition

- A single, time-limited episode of dystonia with neurological activity linked to abnormal electrical discharges in OUN (central nervous system)

neurons.

Evaluation of Clinically Manifest Dystonic Attack

1. Reconstruct the sequence of events

- Obtain detailed report (from witnesses and the child).

2. Identify triggering factors

- Determine precipitating causes.

3. Implement treatment

- Immediate intervention required.

Key Considerations

- Distinguish between acute dystonia attack and benign incident.

- Rule out life-threatening conditions.

- Assess whether this is the first episode, which may indicate a seizure disorder.

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

- Castillo-Morales vs. Petö:

- Castillo-Morales = Muscle-focused (oral/respiratory muscles).

- Petö = Center-based (requires specialized care).

- DRYHEARTS:

- Distinguish acute vs. benign.

- Reconstruct events.

- Yes/no: Is this a first episode?

- High suspicion for seizure disorder if first episode.

- Evaluate triggers.

- Active treatment needed.

- Rule out life-threatening causes.

- Therapeutic interventions (botulinum, baclofen).

- Specialized therapies (musicotherapy, hydrotherapy).

Comparison Table: Therapeutic Methods

| Method | Focus Area | Requires Specialized Care | Suitable for Severe Disabilities? |

|---------------------|--------------------------|---------------------------|-----------------------------------|

| Castillo-Morales | Facial/oral muscles | No | Yes |

| Petö | Functional independence | Yes | No |

| Pharmacological | Spasticity management | No | N/A |

| Musicotherapy | Holistic development | No | Yes |

Selected Problems of Neonatal Neurology

Most Common Causes and Diagnosis of Seizures in the Neonatal Period

Metabolic Causes:

Hypoglycemia, hypocalcemia → measurement of glucose levels and comprehensive ionogram in blood, Di George syndrome,

Renal cycle disorders with hyperammonemia, organic acids, nonketotic hyperglycemia, maple syrup urine disease → amino acid test, ammonia

level, GC/MS analysis of urine, evaluation of acidity, ketosis,

Vitamin B6 deficiency → therapeutic trial with vitamin B6.

Intrauterine and Perinatal Infections:

Cerebrospinal fluid examination (general, bacteriological, and PCR), urine examination (general and bacteriological), TORCH test.

Congenital Malformations:

USG, TK, MRI.

Perinatal Encephalopathy Due to Hypoxia-Ischemia:

Fetal distress, umbilical cord compression, meconium staining.

Perinatal Trauma:

Perinatal trauma, intracranial bleeding and hydrocephalus → obstetric history, perinatal examination, USG imaging.

Most Common Causes and Diagnosis of Seizures in Infants and Young Children

Infants and young children with normal development and neurological examination require only blood glucose and calcium levels. Further diagnostic

procedures are required for infants with recurrent seizures or neurological symptoms.

Fever-related seizures:

The most common type of seizure in childhood; occurs between the 6th month to the 6th year (peaks at age 2) and is associated with a fever

>38.5°C:

Simple – brief (3–5 minutes), generalized seizure occurring once during the course of the illness;

Complex – lasting more than 15 minutes or multiple seizures within the same illness, accompanied by neurological symptoms.

Memory Aid

Mnemonic for Fever-Related Seizures:

Simple = Short, Single, Symptom-free.

Complex = Continuous, Complex, Clinical signs.

TORCH Test Acronym:

Toxoplasmosis, Other (syphilis, varicella-zoster), Rubella, Cytomegalovirus, Herpes simplex.

Comparison Table: Neonatal vs. Infant Seizures

Feature

Neonatal Seizures

Infant/Child Seizures

Common Causes

Metabolic, hypoxia-ischemia, infections

Fever, structural, genetic

Diagnosis

Blood tests, imaging, CSF analysis

Blood glucose, EEG, MRI

Fever Association

Rare

Common (especially simple febrile seizures)

Clinical Pearl:

Recurrent seizures or neurological symptoms in infants necessitate urgent further testing (e.g., EEG, MRI, metabolic screening).

Anti-inflammatory drugs:

Ibuprofen

Immediate interventions during seizure:

Proper positioning of the child

Oral administration of diazepam

Mandatory in all cases: Exclude meningitis or encephalitis.

Children under 12 months: Always require diagnostic evaluation.

8 secEpilepsy (Seizure Disorders)

Clinical Pearl

All children under 12 months with feverish seizures require immediate diagnostic evaluation due to higher risk of underlying pathology.

Diazepam is the first-line medication for acute seizure management in children.

2 secSelected Issues in Pediatric Neurology

11 secScope of Diagnostic Studies

The scope of diagnostic studies depends on the age at which the seizure occurred. The most important element of differential diagnosis is the history.

6 minDifferential Diagnosis of Seizures (Conditions to Exclude)

Mood-dependent apneic seizures

Occur especially in children with a "temperamental" temperament (most commonly in toddlers) as a reaction to dissatisfaction

Child cries, stops breathing, turns blue, and briefly loses consciousness

No treatment required

Reflex anoxic seizures

Occur up to 2 years of age

Triggers: fear, fever, cold, hunger, and head injury

Child becomes pale, falls down, and briefly loses consciousness (effect of bradycardia and nerve compression)

Vegetative seizures

Occur in a warm and humid environment or after long periods spent standing up

Migraines

Aura preceding the migraine may include ataxia, visual and speech disturbances, altered consciousness, and other neurological symptoms

QT prolongation syndrome with cardiac causes

Requires consultation with a cardiologist and EKG examination

Tic disorders

Complex tics, especially localized ones, can resemble seizures of loss of consciousness

Seizures of the neck

Paroxysmal tilting to one side lasting several minutes in children up to 2 years old

Recurring several times per month without other symptoms

Spontaneously resolve

Early infantile myoclonic epilepsy

"Shaking" attacks persisting without altered consciousness

Appears between ages 4–6

Usually subsides by the age of 2–3

Sandifer syndrome

Changes in posture of limbs or dystonic body position during signs of gastroesophageal reflux (mainly in infants)

Spasmus nutans

Head shaking, tilting to one side, and oculopalsmus in infants

Symptoms spontaneously resolve after a few years

Requires neuroimaging due to the possibility of II nerve or brainstem lesion

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Differential Diagnosis Conditions

Mood-dependent apneic seizures

Reflex anoxic seizures

Vegetative seizures

Migraines

QT prolongation syndrome

Tic disorders

Neck seizures

Early infantile myoclonic epilepsy

Sandifer syndrome

Spasmus nutans

🧠Clinical Pearls

Spasmus nutans is the only condition in this list requiring neuroimaging.

Mood-dependent apneic seizures and seizures of the neck are non-epileptic and do not require treatment.

Early infantile myoclonic epilepsy typically resolves by age 2–3, but Sandifer syndrome is linked to gastroesophageal reflux.

162 PEDIATRIA

Hyperventilation Syndrome

Restlessness, rapid breathing, dizziness, palpitations, and occasionally loss of consciousness.

Occurs during emotional states.

Psychogenic Attacks

Pretending to have seizures or fainting as a way to manipulate the environment.

Munchausen's Syndrome

Giving false information in interviews about children having seizures or fainting due to incidents caused by parents.

ALTERATIONS OF CONSCIOUSNESS IN CHILDREN

Functional Consciousness Alterations:

Somnolence (praesomnolentia)

Preserved consciousness.

Can communicate verbally.

Responds with slowness and delay.

Slowing of thinking processes.

Pathological Somnolence (somnolentia)

Chronically sleepy.

Awakened by strong stimuli (shaking, loud noise).

Verbal contact limited to single words.

Physiological reflexes (coughing, gagging) preserved.

Pre-sleep State (praecoma, sopor)

Further deepening of alterations.

Impossible to establish verbal contact.

Reacts with grimacing, yawning, and movements on strong stimuli.

Swallowing and gagging reflexes lost.

Coma (coma)

Complete loss of consciousness.

No response to painful stimuli.

Absent deep and superficial reflexes.

Lost swallowing reflex.

Incontinence of urine and feces.

Lies motionless.

Severe Consciousness Alterations Characterized by Disorientation:

The child is disoriented about time, place, and situation.

Includes various symptoms forming syndromes:

Confusion State

Impairment of cognitive abilities and decision-making.

Disorientation often accompanies confusion.

First disturbance of orientation in time, later...

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonics for Consciousness States:

P.S.P.C. = Somnolence → Pathological Somnolence → Pre-sleep State → Coma (progressive depth).

Key Differentiators:

| State | Verbal Contact | Reflexes | Response to Stimuli |

|-------------------------|--------------------|----------------------|-------------------------------|

| Somnolence | Yes (slow) | Preserved | Slowness, delayed response |

| Pathological Somnolence | Limited (single words) | Preserved | Awakened by strong stimuli |

| Pre-sleep State | No | Partially lost | Grimacing, yawning on stimuli |

| Coma | No | Absent | No response |

Clinical Pearl:

Munchausen's Syndrome involves fabricated symptoms by parents; always consider psychosocial factors in unexplained pediatric cases.

Wybrane problemy neurologii wieku dziecięcego

1. Disorders of spatial recognition and subsequently short-term memory loss

Delirium Syndrome

In children: Strong psychomotor agitation, disorientation, fear, irritability, aggression, and agitation.

Coma Syndrome

Loss of consciousness, characterized by inability to think clearly or quickly and difficulty in contact with the environment.

May be accompanied by delusions, illusions, agitation, and aggression.

2. Epidemiology

Disorders of consciousness not caused by trauma most commonly occur during:

Infancy

Adolescence

Most common causes (30% of non-traumatic cases):

Meningitis

Meningo-occlusive inflammation

Most frequent metabolic cause of loss of consciousness:

Ketonemia acidosis (common in adolescents).

3. Causes leading to loss of consciousness

Substance-related:

Alcohol, drugs, medication overdose, poisoning

Neurological/metabolic:

Seizures, encephalopathy, electrolyte disorders

Cardiologic:

Endocrine:

Glucose disorders

Trauma-related:

Penetration, impact, tumor, conditions reducing intracranial space

Renal:

Kidney disease

Other:

Hypoxia, incorrect body temperature, infections, psychiatric diseases

4. Most commonly used substances causing loss of consciousness

Amphetamine

Cocaine

Barbiturates

Benzodiazepines

🧠Memory Aid

Delirium vs. Coma in Children:

Delirium: Agitation, disorientation, fear, aggression (active state).

Coma: Unresponsive, impaired cognition, may have delusions/illusions (passive state).

Mnemonic for Common Causes:

Alcohol, Drugs, Overdose, Poisoning → ADOSE

Seizures, Encephalopathy, Electrolytes → SEE

Cardiologic, Endocrine, Kidney → CEK

Trauma, Infections, Psychiatric → TIP

Ketonemia Acidosis: Remember it’s a metabolic cause most common in adolescents (link to diabetes).

2 secSelected Issues in Pediatric Neurology

28 secClinical Presentations and Differential Diagnoses

Fever, shivering (possibly positional headache) → Meningitis and/or brain inflammation, heat stroke

Neurological symptoms of focal origin, shivering, anisocoria → Localized intracranial pathology (tumor, bleeding)

Bruises, nosebleed → Trauma, shaken baby syndrome

Dehydration, shivering → Salicylate poisoning

Sweating, skin rash, shivering → Hypoglycemia

Dry skin, hyperpnea, sweet breath from the mouth → Hyperglycemia, ketosis

Hyperpnea, muscle cramps, spasm of the iris → Barbiturate or opioid poisoning

29 secAssessment of Altered Consciousness in Children

Collection of information about a child's health problems to assess causes of altered consciousness:

Diabetes → Hypoglycemia, hyperglycemia

Fainting → Post-shivering state, overdose of drugs

Laryngospasm → Vocal cord obstruction

Heart diseases → Cardiogenic shock

Coagulopathy → Bleeding into the brain

Kidney diseases → Hematuria, fracture of the lower abdomen

Inadequate history-taking, lack of cooperation with the child's condition → Trauma OUN, shaken baby syndrome, abuse syndrome

3 minAdditional Diagnostic Tests

Arterial blood gas analysis

Glucose level determination

Blood morphology with platelet count, coagulogram

Liver enzyme activity determination, bilirubin, ammonia, creatinine, and uric acid levels

Urine and blood culture

Cerebrospinal fluid examination (general and bacteriological)

EKG recording

Radiographic examinations (chest X-ray, CT OUN)

Toxicological tests and potential metabolic disease diagnostics

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics for Symptom-Condition Associations

Fever + Shivering + Headache → Meningitis (F-S-H = M)

Shivering + Bruises + Nosebleed → Shaken Baby Syndrome (S-B-N = SBS)

Hyperpnea + Sweet Breath → Diabetic Ketoacidosis (H-SB = DKA)

🧠Clinical Pearl

Anisocoria + Focal Neurological Symptoms = High suspicion for intracranial pathology (e.g., tumor, hemorrhage). Always prioritize

imaging (CT OUN) in such cases.

Key Considerations in Altered Consciousness

Ensure biological material (blood and urine) is secured for further tests.

Implementation of treatment after identifying causes of altered consciousness.

Assessment of Altered Consciousness: Glasgow Coma Scale

Frequent evaluation is critical to monitor changes in consciousness:

- ≥ 9 points – Normal consciousness

- 8 points – Mild alteration of consciousness

- 7 points – Deep alteration of consciousness; coma state

1 minBrain and Spinal Cord Meningeal Infections (ZOMR)

Key Principles of Treatment

Priority: Appropriate drug selection based on clinical presentation (symptomatic drugs, antiviral drugs, anti-inflammatory drugs, chemotherapy,

antibiotics).

Antibiotic selection: Must consider:

Current medical knowledge (EBM principles),

Local epidemiology,

Bacterial susceptibility/antibiotic resistance.

Pathogenesis

Transmission routes:

- Blood-borne,

- Contact with cerebrospinal fluid from external environment (e.g., lumbar puncture, neurosurgical procedures),

- Spread of infection to surrounding tissues (nasal sinuses, middle ear, mastoid process).

Etiology of Bacterial ZOMR

Newborns:

- Streptococcus gr. B (33%),

- Escherichia coli (30%),

- Gram-negative bacteria (27%),

- Listeria monocytogenes (10%),

- Pseudomonas spp., Proteus spp., Klebsiella spp.

Infants up to 3 months:

- Neisseria meningitidis,

- Streptococcus sp.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for "Opening Eye" Scoring

Spontaneous → 4 points

Reaction to sound → 3 points

Reaction to pain → 2 points

No reaction → 1 point

🧠ZOMR Transmission Routes (B, C, S)

Blood-borne

Contact with CSF

Spread from adjacent tissues

🧠Bacterial Etiology Comparison

Age Group

Common Pathogens

Newborns

Streptococcus gr. B, E. coli, Listeria

Infants <3 months

Neisseria meningitidis, Streptococcus sp.

🧠Clinical Pearl

For ZOMR, empirical antibiotic choice must balance local resistance patterns and patient age (e.g., newborns vs. older infants).

3 secClinical Symptoms of ZOMR in Children (Own Compilation)

44 secVerbal and Nonverbal Contact

Newborns and Infants

Fever (about 50% of cases) or body temperature elevation

Somnolence, restlessness (about 30% of children) to deep changes in consciousness

Seizures (about 40% of infants)

Muscle stiffness

Facial paralysis

Tightness and constriction of the throat (20-30% of cases)

Respiratory distress

Focal symptoms: seizures, eye inflammation

Flatau sign (ocular-chorioretinal); other focal symptoms are rarely present

Non-specific symptoms: rash, lack of coughing or wheezing

Older Children

Persistent fever, headache, nausea, vomiting, photophobia

Change in normal activity and mild changes in consciousness to delirium and coma

Changes in the nervous system: reduced reflexes, reduced facial nerve function

Positive opsonic symptoms

Often skin changes: eczematous and purulent

18 secAseptic ZOMR Clinical Symptoms

Gradual development of clinical signs (a few days to a week)

Initial grypoid-like symptoms: fever, throat pain, muscle pain, lack of coughing

Later stage: somnolence, vomiting, severe headache with nasal congestion, stiffness in the neck

Physical examination: opsonic symptoms are commonly present

1 minFungal ZOMR Symptoms

Fatigue, fever, headaches, nausea, vomiting, loss of body mass

Vision changes: double vision and others

Seizures

Facial nerve dysfunction symptoms

Opsonic symptoms

Physical examination: skin damage around the nose-lip area in cases of Coccidioides immitis

Progression: commonly with generalized fungal infection

Progression: sometimes with periods of relapse and remission of symptoms

3 minObjawy oponowe

Neck stiffness

Lack of physiological possibility of bringing the head to the chest cavity so that the chin touches the skull

Degree of stiffness measured by the number of fingers placed between the skull and the chin

Significant neck stiffness causes forced extension of the head (opisthotonus)

Flattau sign

Cervico-midriatic: With stiff neck, the pupils are dilated

Erective: Involves the release of the small boy's testicles after repeated passive flexion of their tails forward (typical for tuberculous ZOMR)

Brudziński's polliculus sign

Reflexive lifting of the forearms and their bending in response to pressing the lower ends of the ulnar bones

Brudziński's cervical sign

With stiff neck, spontaneous flexion of the lower limbs in the hip and knee joints

Sometimes observed simultaneously with flexion of the upper limb joints at the elbow

Brudziński's leg sign

Applied by the examiner to press on the child's joint causing its flexion

Memory Aid

Mnemonic for CSF glucose levels:

Bacterial → Low glucose (due to bacterial consumption).

Tuberculous → Very low glucose (similar to bacterial, but more severe).

Fungal → Normal glucose (distinct from bacterial/tuberculous).

Key differences in CSF appearance:

Bacterial: Turbid, yellow-tinged.

Fungal: Clear or opalescent.

Syphilis: Clear.

Chloride levels:

Tuberculous and Syphilis → Significantly reduced.

Bacterial → Reduced (but not as severe as tuberculous).

WBC patterns:

Bacterial: Polymorphonuclear predominance.

Tuberculous: Lymphocyte predominance.

Syphilis: Univalent or plasma cells.

Clinical pearl:

Tuberculous meningitis is the only type with both glucose and chloride <45 in ~80% of cases.

2 secMeningeal Signs in Pediatrics

1 minClinical Signs and Assessment

Kernig's Sign

Upper Kernig's sign:

Patient's trunk is passively bent forward.

Positive: Reflexive flexion of lower limbs at hip and knee joints.

Lower Kernig's sign:

Patient lies supine; flex hip and knee joints at 90°.

Positive: Reflexive resistance from thigh flexors when attempting to straighten the limb.

Alternative method: Raise straight limb at knee joint.

Positive: Flexion occurs at the knee joint.

Note: Clinically valuable in young children.

Amos's Sign (Tripod Sign)

Assessment: Sitting child.

Positive: Requires upper limb support to maintain sitting position (normal: no support needed).

Brdlik's Sign ("Kissing the Knees")

Assessment: Child sits on flat surface with limbs straight and flat against the ground.

Positive: Inability to bend forward deeply without flexing lower limbs at hip and knee joints (normal in children <5–6 years).

Weil-Edelman's Sign

Assessment: During lower Kernig's sign examination.

Positive: Dorsal flexion of the hallux (similar to Babinski's sign).

Clinical significance: Suggestive of meningitis or cerebral edema.

8 secPhysiological vs. Pathological Conditions

Physiological: Meningeal signs do not occur.

Pathological: Presence indicates meningeal irritation, inflammation, or subarachnoid hemorrhage.

1 minSpecial Considerations in Newborns and Infants

Interpretation challenges: Meningeal signs are difficult to assess in newborns.

Additional signs in advanced meningitis:

Excessive lordosis of the spine.

Retracted abdomen.

Lateral position with flexed lower limbs.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Meningeal Signs

Kernig's (hip/knee flexion), Amos's (tripod support), Brdlik's (knees not touched), Weil-Edelman's (hallux flexion).

🧠Comparison Table

🧠Clinical Pearl

Brdlik's sign: Normal in children under 5–6 years; abnormal in older children suggests meningeal irritation.

Weil-Edelman's sign: A key indicator of meningitis, similar to Babinski's sign but assessed during Kernig's test.

49 secPhysical and Biochemical Characteristics of Proper PMR

pH: 7.31–7.34

Pressure:

Lying down: 6–20 cm H₂O

Sitting: 15–24 cm H₂O

Newborn: 1–8 cm H₂O

Color: Water-like

Transparency: Full transparency

Volume: 100–160 ml

Pleocytosis: < 6/mm³; cell type – lymphocytes/monocytes 7/3

Premature: Average 27 (maximum 110)/mm³

Newborn: Average 5–10/mm³

Glucose: 2.2–3.9 mmol/l (50–80% concentration in serum)

Premature: 50–250% (average 81%) concentration in serum

Newborn: 50–105% concentration in serum

Protein:

Premature: Average 50–290 mg/dl (average 100 mg/dl)

Newborn: 45–120 (240) mg/dl (average 60 mg/dl)

After second month of life: 20–45 mg/dl

Albumin: 56–75%

Protein ratio (globulin to albumin): 0.1–0.45

IgG: 0.01–0.04 g/l

IgG index (Linka-Tiebling's index): < 0.8

Globulin reaction (Pandy's and Noone-Apel's reactions): Present

Electrolytes:

Na: 137–145 mmol/l

K: 2.7–3.9 mmol/l

Cl: 116–132 mmol/l

Blood

Urine

General analysis

Gram stain preparation

Ziehl-Neelsen staining

Bacteriological test

PCR

Imaging Studies

USG, TK or MR

Indicated in cases of:

Worsening general condition

Appearance of focal symptoms

6 minTreatment

Empirical treatment (should not last longer than 48 hours) until information about the etiology of ZOMR is obtained

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics for PMR Characteristics

PH: 7.31–7.34 (slightly acidic)

Pressure: 6–20 cm H₂O (lying down)

Color: Water-like (clear)

Transparency: Full (no cloudiness)

Volume: 100–160 ml (moderate)

Pleocytosis: <6/mm³ (lymphocytes/monocytes 7/3)

Glucose: 2.2–3.9 mmol/l (50–80% of serum)

Protein: Premature 50–290 mg/dl, newborn 45–120 mg/dl

IgG index: <0.8 (Linka-Tiebling’s)

Globulin reaction: Present (Pandy/Noone-Apel)

Parameter

🧠Clinical Pearl

ZOMR (etiology unknown in empirical treatment) – always confirm etiology within 48 hours to avoid prolonged antibiotic use.

174 PEDIATRIA

Empirical Treatment for Bacterial Meningitis in Children

Children up to 3 months old:

- Cefalosporins III generation + aminoglycosides

- Example: Cefotaxime 200 mg/kg mc./24 hours i.v. every 6 hours + netromycin 7.5 mg/kg mc./24 hours i.v. every 12 hours

- Cefalosporins III generation + ampicillin

- Dose: Ampicillin 100–200 mg/kg mc./24 hours i.v.

Children over 3 months old:

- Cefalosporins III generation + penicillins

- Example: Cefotaxime 200 mg/kg mc./24 hours i.v. every 6 hours or ceftiraxone 100 mg/kg mc./24 hours i.v. every 24 hours + penicillin G 300–400

thousand units/kg mc./24 hours, every 4–6 hours

- Cefalosporins III generation + vancomycin + acyclovir

- Dose: Vancomycin 60 mg/kg mc./24 hours i.v. every 6 hours + acyclovir 15 mg/kg mc./24 hours every 8 hours

Targeted Treatment (Based on Confirmed Etiology)

N. meningitidis

Penicillin + cefalosporins III generation

S. pneumoniae

Penicillin + cefalosporins III generation

If vancomycin-resistant: Adjust accordingly

H. influenzae

Cefalosporins III generation

If resistant to vancomycin and rifampicin: Adjust accordingly

S. aureus

Nafcillin (200–300 mg/kg mc./24 hours) or oxacillin (200–300 mg/kg mc./24 hours)

If vancomycin-resistant: Adjust accordingly

Gram-negative bacteria (-)

Cefalosporins III generation

If resistant to ticarcillin with aminoglycosides: Adjust accordingly

L. monocytogenes

Ampicillin 200–300 mg/kg mc./24 hours every 4–6 hours for 21 days + aminoglycosides

If resistant to imipenem, cotrimoxazole, or ciprofloxacin: Adjust accordingly

🧠Memory Aid

Key Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

- Age-Based Empirical Regimens:

- <3 months: Cefotaxime + aminoglycosides or ampicillin

- >3 months: Cefotaxime/ceftiraxone + penicillin G or vancomycin + acyclovir

Pathogen-Specific Treatments:

| Pathogen | Empirical Agent | Targeted Agent (if resistant) |

|------------------|------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------|

| N. meningitidis | Cefalosporins + penicillin | N/A |

| S. pneumoniae | Cefalosporins + penicillin | Vancomycin (if resistant) |

| H. influenzae | Cefalosporins | N/A (rare resistance) |

| S. aureus | Cefalosporins + vancomycin | Nafcillin/oxacillin (if vancomycin-resistant) |

| Gram-negative | Cefalosporins | Aminoglycosides (if ticarcillin-resistant) |

| L. monocytogenes| Ampicillin + aminoglycosides | Cotrimoxazole/ciprofloxacin (if imipenem-resistant) |

Dosage Tip:

- Vancomycin: 60 mg/kg every 6 hours (for >3 months)

- Ampicillin: 100–200 mg/kg for <3 months; 200–300 mg/kg for L. monocytogenes (21 days)

CONGENITAL AND ACQUIRED INFECTIONS

21. Diagnostics

Examination of respiratory tract or cerebrospinal fluid aspirates.

Determination of the titer of specific IgM and IgG antibodies.

Treatment

- Erythromycin and other macrolide antibiotics.

2. INFECTIONS THAT MAY BE CONGENITAL OR ACQUIRED

2.1. Inflammation of the meninges (meningitis)

Epidemiology

- Incidence of purulent meningitis: 0.1–1%.

- Most common in premature infants.

Etiopathogenesis

- Infectious disease caused by:

- Bacteria: group B streptococci, Escherichia coli, Listeria monocytogenes, Neisseria meningitidis

- Viruses

- Fungi

- Rarely: protozoa and parasites

Clinical symptoms

- No classic meningeal symptoms!

- Respiratory and circulatory failure

- Tachycardia > 180/min

- Skin symptoms

- Jaundice

- Convulsions

Diagnostics (continued)

- Lumbar puncture (basic diagnostic test). Bacterial infection indicated by:

- Cloudy cerebrospinal fluid

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for bacterial causes of meningitis:

Brain → Streptococcus agalactiae (group B strep)

E. coli → Escherichia coli

Listening → Listeria monocytogenes

Neisseria → Neisseria meningitidis

Clinical Pearl:

In infants, absence of classic meningeal signs (e.g., neck stiffness) is common in meningitis. Look for non-specific symptoms like

tachycardia, jaundice, and skin changes.

Comparison Table:

| Congenital Infections | Acquired Infections |

|-------------------------------|-------------------------------|

| Transmitted in utero | Acquired after birth |

| Common pathogens: Toxoplasma, CMV | Common pathogens: Bacteria, viruses |

| Often asymptomatic at birth | Symptoms appear after exposure |

Selected Problems of Childhood Neurology

Treatment of Meningitis

Antifungal therapy for C. neoformans or C. albicans:

Amphotericin B: 0.25 mg/kg BW/24 h, increasing to 1.5 mg/kg BW/24 h

Fluconazole: 6–12 mg/kg BW/24 h in one dose

Steroid therapy (e.g., dexamethasone):

Administer before antibiotic administration to inhibit inflammatory cytokine synthesis

Intravenous fluid management:

Correct water-electrolyte and acid-base disturbances

Monitor diuresis

Anti-inflammatory drugs:

20% mannitol: 2 g/kg BW/24 h every 4–6 h for 3–5 days

Furosemide: 1 mg/kg BW/dose every 8 h

Anticonvulsants for convulsions:

Relanium (midazolam) PR or IV

Phenobarbital IV infusion

Immunotherapy for severe cases:

Intravenous immunoglobulins for:

Severe meningitis with coagulation disorders (DIC)

Immunodeficiency disorders

Meningococcal disease (Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome)

Fresh frozen plasma, cryoprecipitate, platelet concentrate for meningococcal etiology

Early rehabilitation of the patient

Chemoprophylaxis and immunoprophylaxis for close contacts

Complications

Early Complications

Inflammation of the ventricular lining → hydrocephalus

Brain abscess

Subarachnoid space dilatation

Subdural hydrops

Stroke

Venous sinus thrombosis

Seizures

Late Complications

Delayed psychomotor development

Mental retardation

Cerebral palsy

Epilepsy

Analyzer function disorders (e.g., hypoacusis)

Cranial nerve damage (III, IV, VI, VII)

Limb paresis/paralysis

Memory Aid

MENINGITIS Treatment Mnemonic:

Mannitol, Enter steroids early, No delay in fluids, Imune support (IGIV), Neonatal antifungals, Glucocorticoids, Itis (anticonvulsants), Timely

rehab, Stay prophylactic

Early vs. Late Complications Table:

Early Complications

Late Complications

Hydrocephalus

Cerebral palsy

Brain abscess

Epilepsy

Venous sinus thrombosis

Mental retardation

Seizures

Psychomotor delay

Subdural hydrops

Hypoacusis

Key Tip: For meningococcal disease, Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome requires immunoglobulins + plasma/platelets.

2 secBÓLE GŁOWY U DZIECI

7 secPrevalence

30–40% of children experience recurring headaches (peak: ages 7–14).

Preschool age: ~20% of children.

Older children/adolescents: ~50%.

3 minDivision of Headaches

Primary Headache

Includes:

Migraine

Tension-type headache

Cluster headache

Secondary Headache

Pain caused by non-neurological diseases (e.g., infections, sinusitis, trauma).

Acute Headache

Definition: First-time occurrence or significantly different from prior headaches.

Common causes:

Frequent in children.

Associated with infectious diseases (mainly viral; 57% due to upper respiratory tract infections).

Red flags requiring exclusion:

Meningitis/encephalitis:

Pain with fever.

Altered consciousness.

General symptoms: joint pain, neck swelling.

Acute onset with:

Temporal pain.

Dizziness.

Vomiting.

Double vision.

Neurological signs:

Brainstem damage (e.g., aphasia, dysarthria).

Cerebellar nerve damage.

Visual field loss.

Abnormal motor response.

Vascular malformations.

Localized headache linked to:

Sinusitis.

Middle ear infection.

Stomatological diseases.

Head injury (assess for:

Subdural hematoma.

Epidural hematoma.

Fractures.

Increased intracranial pressure in children <3 years old with recent head injury (check for pulsating bruise sign).

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Primary Headache Types

Migraine

Tension-type

Cluster → MTC

🧠Red Flags in Acute Headache (Meningitis/Encephalitis)

Fever

Encephalitis

Vascular malformations

Eye symptoms (double vision, visual field loss)

Rapid onset → FEVER mnemonic

🧠Key Clinical Pearls

Children <3 years with head injury: Always check for pulsating bruise sign (sign of increased intracranial pressure).

Sinusitis and middle ear infections are common causes of localized headaches in children.

Viral infections (e.g., upper respiratory tract infections) account for 57% of infectious-related headaches in children.

Wybrane problemy neurologii wieku dziecięcego

1. Temporomandibular Joint Dysfunction

Clinical presentation:

Unilateral pain localized to the ear, jaw, or mouth.

Other causes of acute headache:

Astigmatism, refractive errors, eye strain, alcohol, drugs, poisoning.

2. Migraine in Children

Key features:

Repeated sharp pain – cyclic episodes separated by asymptomatic periods.

Common type: Migraine without aura (common migraine).

Associated symptoms: Nausea, vomiting, photophobia, phonophobia.

Subtypes in children:

Abdominal migraine:

Recurrent abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, recurring headaches.

Mild episodic vertigo:

Sudden vertigo, dizziness, nystagmus; occurs in children aged 2–3 years (preceding migraines).

Episodic torticollis:

Recurring lateral head tilt, vomiting, headache.

3. Persistent Dull Pain (Serious Conditions)

Characteristics:

Worsening over time; duration: hours to months.

Common causes:

1. Intracranial hypertension:

2. Often due to OUN tumor (most frequent symptom: headache; 50% of cases have: nausea, vomiting, visual disturbances, gait disorders,

speech difficulties, personality changes, academic decline).

3. Cerebral pseudotumor:

4. Cerebral edema → optic nerve compression, VI nerve damage, visual field loss.

5. Hydrocephalus.

6. OUN abscess:

7. Brain swelling, middle ear infection, sinusitis with nasal congestion, bleeding.

Memory Aid

Migraine subtypes in children:

Abdominal migraine → Abdominal pain + headache.

Vertigo → Vertigo in 2–3-year-olds.

Torticollis → Tilted head + headache.

Intracranial hypertension red flags:

Headache + Nausea/vomiting + Visual changes + Gait issues + School performance decline (HNVGS mnemonic).

OUN tumor symptoms:

Headache (most common) + Nausea/vomiting + Visual disturbances + Gait disorders + Speech/school issues.

Persistent dull pain vs. acute headache:

Persistent → Serious conditions (tumor, pseudotumor, hydrocephalus, abscess).

Acute → Temporomandibular dysfunction, eye strain, drugs/alcohol.

4 secPEDIATRIA

178 | Intracranial Head Injury and Headache Management

17 secIntracranial Headache (Head Injury)

Classic sign of intracranial hypertension:

Headache that wakes the patient from sleep or occurs immediately after waking, accompanied by vomiting.

Can be a symptom of:

Vascular diseases (hypertension, collagen disease).

Optic nerve atrophy.

Lyme disease.

Infectious mononucleosis.

Metabolic errors.

20 secPersistent Headaches (Non-Worsening)

Characteristics:

Most common type of headache in young people.

Typical for:

Tension-type headaches.

Muscle-contracting headaches.

Depression equivalents.

Psychogenic headaches during conversational reactions.

Pain features:

Mild or moderate, non-pulsating, with a local pressure character.

Occurs intermittently around the forehead.

No nausea, vomiting, or aura.

Chronic pain lasting several years.

6 secMixed Headache

Definition:

Combination of:

Acute recurring headache (migraine).

Persistent non-worsening headache occurring daily.

23 secWarning Signs of Serious Headache

Red flags requiring urgent evaluation:

First-time headache in life.

Headache during physical exertion or sports activities.

Associated neurological symptoms (e.g., focal weakness, vision changes).

Change in pain character (e.g., new pattern, intensity).

Headache that interrupts sleep.

Intermittent occipital region pain.

History data:

Previous head injury.

Neurological or psychiatric disorders.

Hospitalization.

Medication use.

13 secDiagnosis Approach

1. History:

2. Detailed patient history (onset, duration, associated symptoms).

3. Physical Examination:

4. Measure:

Height.

Body mass index (BMI).

Head circumference (using centimeter grids).

5. Reanalysis of measurements for trends.

32 secMemory Aid

Mnemonic for Warning Signs:

First-time headache

Exertion-related pain

Neurological symptoms

Change in pain character

Sleep disruption

Occipital pain

History of injury/illness → FENCSOH

Clinical Pearl

Persistent daily headaches in children are often tension-type unless red flags are present.

Occipital headaches with exertion may suggest intracranial pathology (e.g., tumor, aneurysm).

Assessment of current developmental status

Detailed skin examination at the angle to detect typical changes associated with neurocutaneous syndromes

8 secImaging Diagnostics

Recommended modalities: USG (ultrasonography), TK (computed tomography), MR (magnetic resonance imaging)

Limited usefulness: RTG (radiography), EEG (electroencephalography)

1 minIndications for Brain Imaging in Patients with Headaches

1. Neurological examination abnormalities

2. Signs of increased intracranial pressure

3. Progressive or newly appearing neurological symptoms or changes in mental state

4. Significant head injury

5. Intense headache waking up from sleep or present immediately after awakening

6. Intense headache (most severe during life) occurring more frequently and lasting longer

7. Nasal drainage

8. Persistent headaches

9. Imaging diagnostics can also be considered in cases of persistent headache localization

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Imaging Modalities

USG, TK, MR – Remember as "U-T-M" (Ultrasonography, Tomography, Magnetic Resonance).

🧠Clinical Pearl

Persistent headaches in children always warrant imaging if associated with neurological abnormalities, increased intracranial pressure, or

changes in mental state.

🧠Comparison Table: Imaging Modalities

Modality

Usefulness

Notes

High

Non-invasive, ideal for neonates

TK

High

Rapid, useful for acute trauma

MR

High

Best for soft tissue detail

RTG

Limited

Low sensitivity for brain pathology

Evaluates electrical activity, not structural abnormalities

🧠Key Indications for Brain Imaging (Headaches)

Progressive symptoms

Increased intracranial pressure

Nasal drainage

Trauma

Extreme intensity or frequency

Sleep disturbance

Imaging for persistent localization

Consider in all persistent cases

2 secPEDIATRIA: Headache in Children

14 secClinical Indications for Evaluation

Localized headache (e.g., around the temple)

Recurrent headaches with no family history of migraine

Presence of nerve-skin syndromes or pain sensitivity before 3 years of age (due to limited verbal communication)

49 secTreatment Approach

1. Non-Pharmacological Interventions

Calm the patient and remove potential symptom-exacerbating factors

Introduce a headache calendar for tracking patterns

Behavioral program:

Establish appropriate sleep and rest schedules

Dietary modifications:

Limit caffeine, chocolate, alcohol, glutamic acid sodium, and aspartame

Ensure adequate hydration

Encourage physical exercise

2. Pharmacological Management

Analgesics:

Paracetamol:

10–15 mg/kg per dose (children <12 years)

650–1000 mg per dose (children ≥12 years)

Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs):

Acetylsalicylic acid:

10–15 mg/kg per dose (children <12 years)

650–1000 mg per dose (children ≥12 years)

Ibuprofen:

5–10 mg/kg per dose (children <12 years)

400 mg per dose (children ≥12 years)

Naproxen:

2.5–5 mg/kg per dose (children <12 years)

250–300 mg per dose (children ≥12 years)

Symptomatic treatment: Anti-inflammatory agents and other supportive therapies

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Drug Dosage Mnemonic (for NSAIDs):

Paracetamol: 10–15 mg/kg (children) / 650–1000 mg (≥12 years)

Ibuprofen: 5–10 mg/kg (children) / 400 mg (≥12 years)

Naproxen: 2.5–5 mg/kg (children) / 250–300 mg (≥12 years)

Acronym: PIN (Paracetamol, Ibuprofen, Naproxen)

Behavioral Program: S.D.H.E.

Sleep: Regular schedule

Diet: Avoid triggers (caffeine, chocolate, etc.)

Hydration: Maintain adequate fluid intake

Exercise: Encourage physical activity

Key Red Flags:

Headache before age 3 with no family migraine history

Nerve-skin syndromes or pain sensitivity in non-verbal children

Headache Calendar: Essential for identifying triggers and patterns in recurrent headaches.

2 secSelected Problems of Pediatric Oncology

22 secAcute Lymphoblastic Leukemia (ALL)

Prevalence: Most common childhood leukemia, accounting for 80–85% of all cases.

B-cell ALL:

86% of ALL cases.

Higher incidence in children vs. adults.

Affects B-cell lineage lymphocytes (B-ALL).

T-cell ALL:

14% of ALL cases.

Acute Non-Lymphoblastic Leukemia (ANLL):

10–15% of childhood leukemia cases.

Affects myeloid cells.

Characterized by non-lymphocytic morphology blasts.

8 secChronic Myelogenous Leukemia (CML)

5% of all childhood leukemia cases.

Chronic lymphocytic leukemia (CLL):

Does not occur in children.

13 secNon-Hodgkin Lymphoma (NHL)

Third most common childhood cancer (after leukemia and brain tumors).

Types:

B-cell NHL: 50% of cases.

T-cell NHL: 35% of cases.

Mixed Cell Lineage Lymphoma (MCL): 15% of cases.

11 secHodgkin’s Lymphoma

5–7% of all childhood cancers.

Characteristics:

Presence of Reed-Sternberg cells.

Involves superficial and deep lymph nodes.

Age: Rarely occurs in children under 4 years old.

45 secCentral Nervous System Tumors

Most common solid tumors in children.

Prevalence: 20–25% of all childhood cancers.

Features:

Diverse group of brain tumors.

Varying histological and clinical characteristics.

🧠Memory Aid

ALL Subtypes Mnemonic:

B-T 86-14 (B-cell ALL = 86%, T-cell ALL = 14%).

NHL Types Table:

Type

Percentage

Key Feature

B-cell NHL

50%

Most common

T-cell NHL

35%

Second most common

Mixed Cell Lineage

15%

Combination of cell types

- CML vs. CLL in Children:

- CML occurs in 5% of childhood leukemia.

- CLL is absent in children.

- Hodgkin’s Lymphoma:

- Remember Reed-Sternberg cells and rare under 4 years.

- CNS Tumors:

- 20–25% of childhood cancers; focus on brain tumor diversity.

1 secPEDIATRIA

29 secBrain Tumors in Children

Location and Growth Dynamics

80% of primary brain tumors in children originate from neuroepithelial tissue.

Non-neuroepithelial tumors include:

Craniopharyngioma

Germ cell-derived tumors

Infants more frequently have tumors in the suprasellar region.

Children over 1 year more commonly have infratentorial tumors.

Typical locations in children include:

Around the sphenoid bone

Ventricles

Brainstem structures

Histological Tumor Characteristics

Excluded in children:

Glioblastoma multiforme

Primitive neuroectodermal tumor (PNET)

Medulloblastoma

Choroid plexus papilloma

1 minEtiopathogenesis

Environmental Factors

Pesticides

Nitrosamines exposure

Ionizing radiation (e.g., secondary brain tumors after radiotherapy)

Electromagnetic radiation (suspected factor)

Viral infections

Immunodeficiency Disorders

Primary/secondary immunodeficiency (lymphatic origin tumors)

Genetic Syndromes and Hereditary Diseases

1. Optic nerve glioma type I →

2. Optic pathway glioma

3. Optic nerve glioma

4. Optic nerve glioma type II →

5. Schwannoma (most common around the tentorium cerebelli)

6. Ependymomas

7. Many optic gliomas

8. Tuberous sclerosis →

9. Subependymal giant cell astrocytoma

10. Von Hippel-Lindau disease →

11. Placental hemangioblastoma (hemangioblastoma)

12. Vertebral artery aneurysm

13. Li-Fraumeni syndrome →

14. Diverse brain tumors

15. Gardner syndrome →

16. Medulloblastoma

17. Craniopharyngioma

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics for Genetic Syndromes

VHL: Von Hippel-Lindau → Hemangioblastoma + Aneurysms

Li-Fraumeni: Li-Fraumeni → Lymphatic/brain tumors

Gardner: Gardner → Germ cell tumors (medulloblastoma, craniopharyngioma)

🧠Clinical Pearls

Infants = suprasellar tumors (e.g., craniopharyngioma).

Children >1 year = infratentorial tumors (e.g., medulloblastoma).

Optic nerve glioma type II = schwannoma + tentorium cerebelli location.

Tuberous sclerosis = subependymal giant cell astrocytoma.

10 secSyndromy związane z nowotworami mózgu u dzieci

23 secNajczęstsze typy nowotworów mózgu u dzieci

PNET (3%)

Papillomy pęcherzyka choroidowego (3%) – najczęściej lokalizowane w czwartym komórce mózgowej

Nowotwory rdzenia kręgowego (8%)

1 minObjawy kliniczne

Nowotwory mózgu powodują objawy wynikające z zwiększonych ciśnień w jamie czaszki oraz deficytach neurologicznych zależnych od lokalizacji

nowotworu.

Nowotwory w fossie tylniej: objawy zwiększonych ciśnień w jamie czaszki, wymioty, ból głowy, nudności, zawroty głowy

Nowotwory w półkulach mózgu: dwupłonność, zaburzenia mowy, trudności w połykaniu, ataksja, spastyczność kończyn, zaburzenia czucia,

napady padaczkowe

Nowotwory w półkuli i czwartym komórce mózgowej: częściowe paraliże, drżenie, objawy irytacji

Nowotwory w linii środkowej: zaburzenia endokrynne (spowolnienie wzrostu, niska wzrostowa), pogorszenie ostrości wzroku, utrata pola

wzroku

Nowotwory w regionie pinealnym i cisternie suprasellary: wodowstępy, anizokoria, brak refleksu światłowego, znak Parinauda – "znak

patologicznego patrzenia w dół" (zawroty oczu z powodu bólu i irytacji), pojawienie się białego pierścienia nad dyskiem optycznym; inne objawy

zwiększonych ciśnień w jamie czaszki

Nowotwory w regionie przysadki: zaburzenia pola wzroku (częściowa utrata wzroku)

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonik dla najczęstszych nowotworów mózgu u dzieci

Intraventricular: Astrocytomy, Medulloblastomy, Ependymomy

Extraventricular: Astrocytomy, Medulloblastomy, Germinomy, PNET, Choroid plexus papilloma

1 secPEDIATRIA

37 secClinical Features by Brain Lobe

Temporal Lobe

Disturbances of taste

Visual field disturbances (lower quadrantanopia unilaterally)

Finger agnosia

Dyslexia (when the tumor is located in the dominant hemisphere)

Apraxia

Anosognosia (when the tumor is located in the non-dominant hemisphere)

Parietal Lobe

Sensory aphasia (when the tumor is located in the dominant hemisphere)

Visual field disturbances (upper quadrantanopia unilaterally)

Frontal Lobe

Damage to the facial nerve

Contralateral upper and/or lower extremity weakness

Motor aphasia (when the tumor is located in the dominant hemisphere)

Behavioral and personality changes

Medullary Cavity

Dissociation syndromes

Apraxia

Alexia

14 secSymptoms of Increased Intracranial Pressure

In Infants

Pulsating headache

Feeding disturbances

Vomiting

Enlargement of head circumference

Protrusion of fontanelles in the back

In Older Children

Double vision

Headaches

Behavioral changes

Irritability

Vomiting

Optic nerve papilledema

3 minDiagnostic Methods

Imaging

CT, MR:

Accurate assessment of tumor size, location, and relationship to brain structures

Postoperative evaluation of surgical radicality

Control studies for recurrent tumors

PET (positron emission tomography):

Assessment of metabolic activity and chemical compound metabolism within the brain

Biopsy

Stereotactic biopsy of the tumor and histopathological examination of the biopsy material

Additional Examinations

Examination of the eye fundus: Cerebrospinal fluid analysis

Biopsy of bone marrow and scintigraphy of bones in case of leukemia or high risk of recurrence

🧠Diagnostic Imaging Comparison

Imaging Modality

Key Use

CT, MR

Tumor size, location, surgical evaluation, recurrence monitoring

PET

Metabolic activity assessment

Clinical Features of Meningitis

Increased pleocytosis (number of cells)

Decreased glucose level in cerebrospinal fluid

Increased protein content in cerebrospinal fluid

Assistive Tests for Diagnosis

Blood smear morphology

CRP concentration in serum

Coagulation profile (assessment of hemostasis parameters)

Evaluation of kidney function (creatinine, urea) and liver function (AspAT, ALAT, bilirubin)

Glucose level, electrolyte levels (Na, K, Ca)

Blood gas analysis, serum protein concentration

Quick latex tests for detection of streptococci, pneumococci, Haemophilus influenzae, meningococci

Chest X-ray and abdominal ultrasound

Differential Diagnosis

Pneumonia

Subdural hemorrhage

Acute hypoxia

Brain edema

Metabolic disorders

Congenital heart defects

Treatment

Empirical antibiotic therapy initially with the use of 2 broad-spectrum antibiotics:

Combination of cephalosporins (cefotaxime, ceftriaxone) and penicillins (ampicillin, piperacillin)

Aminoglycosides (netilmicin, gentamicin, amikacin) with penicillin (amoxicillin with clavulanate)

Dosage guidelines:

Ampicillin:

200 mg/kg/24 hours for premature infants and newborns

300 mg/kg/24 hours for infants

Cefotaxime:

100 mg/kg/24 hours for premature infants

150–200 mg/kg/24 hours for newborns and infants

Memory Aid

Mnemonic for cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) changes in meningitis:

"PLGHP" → Pleocytosis, Low glucose, Glycine (glucose), High protein.

Differential diagnosis mnemonic:

Pneumonia, Subdural hemorrhage, Acute hypoxia, Brain edema, Metabolic disorders, Congenital heart defects → PSABMC.

Key clinical pearl:

Empirical antibiotics must cover common pathogens (e.g., Streptococcus pneumoniae, Neisseria meningitidis, Haemophilus influenzae) and adjust

based on age-specific susceptibility and local resistance patterns.

Dosage comparison table:

| Drug | Premature Infants/Newborns | Infants |

|--------------|----------------------------|---------|

| Ampicillin | 200 mg/kg/24h | 300 mg/kg/24h |

| Cefotaxime | 100 mg/kg/24h | 150–200 mg/kg/24h |

2 secSelected Problems in Pediatric Oncology

32 secEvaluation of Tumor Markers in Hormonally Active Tumors

Tumor Markers: - AFP (Alpha-fetoprotein) - βhCG (Beta-human chorionic gonadotropin) - Alkaline phosphatase

Treatment: 1. Neurosurgical (primary method in most cases) 2. Radiotherapy (contraindicated in children <3 years old) 3. Chemotherapy (primary

method for secreting tumors)

Prognosis: - Depends on: - Histological and biological malignancy degree - Tumor localization - Surgical resection possibilities - Collins' Law: Suggests

cure if no new tumor appears after the child's age at diagnosis, increased by 9 months

2 minSoft Tissue Sarcomas

Incidence: 8% of all childhood tumors

Most common: Rhabdomyosarcoma (RMS) (50% of cases)

Rhabdomyosarcoma (RMS)

Age-Related Peaks and Localization: 1. Before age 5: - Localizations: Head, neck, rectum, bladder, vagina 2. After age 14: - Localizations: Urinary

system (renal pelvis), soft tissues of limbs and extremities

Histological Subtypes (4 types): 1. Embryonal type (60% of RMS cases) 2. Sarcoma botryoides: - Most common in: Vagina, bladder, nasopharynx,

middle ear 3. Alveolar type (20% of RMS cases): - Most common in: Limbs and trunk - Typically seen in older children

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Tumor Markers Mnemonic

AFP, βhCG, ALP

- Alpha-fetoprotein

- Beta-hCG

- Alkaline Phosphatase

🧠RMS Subtype Mnemonic

Embryonal, Botryoides, Alveolar (use "EBA" to recall first three subtypes)

🧠Collins' Law

"9-Month Rule": If no tumor recurs 9 months after diagnosis age, prognosis is favorable

🧠RMS Localization Comparison

Age Group

Common Localizations

<5 years

Head, neck, genitalia

>14 years

Limbs, urinary tract

page 151

Rhabdomyosarcoma (RMS) - Clinical Features

Pleomorphic type: 1% of RMS cases.

Undifferentiated forms: 20% of all RMS cases.

Clinical Symptoms

Location-dependent symptoms:

Orbital involvement:

Loss of eyelid, protrusion of the eyeball, vision disturbances.

Differential diagnoses: Orbital mass, retinoblastoma, neuroblastoma metastasis.

Cranial nerve involvement:

Swelling/leakage from the ear canal, facial nerve paralysis, auditory nerve dysfunction, other cranial nerve signs.

Blindness, increased intracranial pressure on the tumor side.

Nasopharyngeal involvement:

Nasal congestion, mouth breathing, nosebleeds, dysphagia.

Bladder/bowel involvement:

Urinary retention, constipation, incontinence, palpable abdominal mass (on palpation or per rectum exam).

Genitourinary involvement:

Prostatic enlargement, pelvic masses, genital tract bleeding.

Soft tissue/bone involvement:

Soft tissue tumor (often post-trauma), bone pain, respiratory tract infection.

Metastatic features:

Hypercalcemia (in metastatic cases).

Diagnostic Methods

Imaging:

CT scan of the head and neck.

MRI.

Abdominal CT and ultrasound (USG).

Cystoureterography.

Bone metastasis imaging:

Chest X-ray (RTG) and CT of the chest wall.

Skull scintigraphy.

Biopsy.

Histopathology:

Gold standard: Histopathological examination of tumor tissue from the mass or omental fat (per IRS criteria).

Staging based on histopathological assessment of surgical radicality.

Memory Aid

Mnemonic for RMS differential diagnoses in orbital involvement: ORN (Orbital mass, Retinoblastoma, Neuroblastoma).

Key diagnostic steps:

Imaging first: CT/MRI for localization, then confirm with histopathology.

Clinical pearl: Hypercalcemia is a red flag for metastatic RMS.

IRS criteria: Focus on histopathological grading to determine surgical staging.

Rarely occur in children

Around the trunk and limbs

Most common among nRMS diagnosed before 1 year of age

Hyperglycemia

Can be a congenital tumor

Occurs mainly in the neonatal period

Most common localization:

Tongue and sublingual area (in neonates)

Head, neck, lower limbs, and abdominal cavity (in older children)

Associated features:

Hypoglycemia

Hypokalemia

12 secLeiomyosarcoma

Most frequent localization:

Esophageal conduit near the stomach

Associated conditions:

May accompany HIV, EBV infection

Seen in children with immunodeficiencies or after immunosuppressive treatment (Tendoksan)

Secondary tumor arising from retinoblastoma

11 secLiposarcoma

Etiology:

Genetic factors play a role

Localization:

Most frequent: limbs and trunk

In newborns and infants: benign course

Epidemiology:

Most common soft tissue sarcoma in adults

1. Surgery

2. Chemotherapy

3. Radiotherapy

4. Choice depends on:

5. Site of primary tumor

6. Histological type

7. Stage of tumor progression
