🧠Clinical Pearl

Liposarcoma is the most common soft tissue sarcoma in adults but has a benign course in infants.

Leiomyosarcoma is often linked to immunodeficiency states and retinoblastoma.

2 secNeuroblastoma (Nerviac Płodowy Współczulny)

30 secOverview

Second most common solid tumor after OUN tumors in children, accounting for 8% of all childhood cancers.

Most commonly occurs in infants and toddlers.

Most common aggressive tumor during the neonatal period and most common abdominal mass up to 3 years of age.

Most frequent localization: 75% abdominal cavity (retroperitoneal space and adrenal glands).

Origin: Develops from nerve cells that give rise to sympathetic nerves and adrenal glands.

Spontaneous regression tendency in the neonatal period.

3 minClinical Symptoms

Depend on location and stage of primary tumor spread.

Homer sign: Drooping eyelid, sunken eyeball, constricted pupil, iris discoloration on the affected side.

Respiratory symptoms: Dyspnea, respiratory tract infections, swallowing difficulties.

Gastrointestinal symptoms: Loss of appetite, nausea, abdominal pain.

Abdominal findings: Enlarged abdominal mass (often with kidney involvement).

Rectal findings: Rectal palpable mass, rectal discharge.

Neurological symptoms:

Localized back pain, weakened lower extremities, muscle weakness.

Skin discoloration, rash, increased reflexes, scoliosis.

Urinary incontinence, external urethral obstruction, lack of sweating.

Encephalopathy: Ataxia, myoclonus, opsomyoclonus (chaotic, variable oculomotor).

Gastrointestinal effects: Diarrhea due to intestinal atony (VIP secretion increase).

Autonomic symptoms:

Sudden flushing with excessive sweating (catecholamine secretion increase).

Tachycardia.

Metastatic symptoms:

Protruding eyeball, ocular discharge, swelling of eyelids and eyebrows.

Bone pain, oozing.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Key Features

"HARMED"

- Homer sign (autonomic dysfunction)

- Abdominal mass (most common localization)

- Rapid progression (aggressive tumor)

- Metastatic spread (bone, skin, CNS)

- Early regression (neonatal period)

- Diarrhea (VIP secretion)

🧠Clinical Pearls

VIP (vasoactive intestinal peptide) secretion causes diarrhea and intestinal atony.

Catecholamine excess leads to flushing, sweating, and tachycardia.

Homer sign is a classic indicator of adrenal or retroperitoneal neuroblastoma.

Neuroblastoma is the most common abdominal tumor in children under 3 years.

Selected Problems in Paediatric Oncology

189

Metastases

- Via lymphatic vessels: regional and distant lymph nodes

- Via blood vessels: bone, liver, spleen, skin; rarely lung and OUN

Diagnostic Methods

Imaging and Recognition

Chest X-ray (lung mass localized retrocardially in the mediastinum)

Ultrasound of the abdomen

CT scan of the abdomen with contrast

MRI (syndrome of vertebral compression)

MIBG scintigraphy

Laboratory Tests

Bone marrow examination

Marker tests:

Urine: dopamine and vanillylmandelic acid

Serum: ferritin and neurospecific enolase

Cerebrospinal fluid: lactate dehydrogenase

Diagnostic Procedures

Histopathological evaluation of the mass according to Shimada criteria

INSS staging assessment in modified Ewensa, D'Angio, and Randolph's criteria

Immunohistochemical examination (NSE, S100 protein)

Electron microscopy – presence of secretory vesicles

N-myc gene amplification and expression evaluation

Treatment

Depends on prognostic factors and disease stage

Modalities:

Surgical treatment

Chemotherapy

Radiotherapy

Autologous stem cell transplantation

Bone Tumours

Prevalence: Approximately 5.5% of all tumours in children and adolescents

Most common: Osteosarcoma and Ewing's sarcoma

Osteosarcoma (osteosarcoma)

Epidemiology:

Most common bone tumour in children

Male:female ratio: 2:1

Peak incidence: 15–19 years of age

Origin: Derived from mesenchyme

Ewing's Sarcoma

Epidemiology:

Second most common bone tumour in children

Male:female ratio: 1:1

Peak incidence: 10–19 years of age

Features:

Characteristic "onion-skin" periosteal reaction on imaging

High expression of CD99 (immunohistochemistry)

Often associated with FLI1 gene rearrangement

🧠Memory Aid

1. Mnemonic for Urinary Markers:

Dopamine, Vanillylmandelic acid → DOPA (remember as "DOPA" for urinary markers)

3. Clinical Pearl:

- Osteosarcoma is more common in long bones (femur, tibia), while Ewing's sarcoma frequently affects pelvis and skull.

4. Staging Acronym (INSS):

Intralesional, Nodal, Systemic, Stage – remember as INSS for staging in paediatric sarcomas.

2 secOsteosarcoma and Ewing Sarcoma

25 secClinical Symptoms

Progressive pain and swelling

Rare general symptoms

In 50% of cases, the tumor develops near the knee; most common localization:

Distal femur metaphysis

Proximal tibia metaphysis

Proximal humerus metaphysis

Flat bones (scapula, sternum) – rare

Most commonly presents as a unifocal change; rarely is it symmetric or multifocal in the metaphyseal region

Most common sites for metastasis: lungs, OUN, other bones

10 secRisk Factors

Rapid bone growth

New tumor appearance in the family history

Retinoblastoma as primary tumor (risk of osteosarcoma development is 500 times higher)

Ionizing radiation

14 secDiagnostic Laboratory and Imaging. Recognition

X-ray of the bone – confirmation of a primary lesion with structural changes and an osseous reaction with bony spicules

CT scan of lungs and OUN, bone scintigraphy (metastasis localization)

8 secTreatment

1. Preoperative and postoperative chemotherapy

2. Surgical (limb-sparing) removal of the primary tumor and metastatic lesions

3. Endoprosthetics

4. Amputations

50 secEwing Sarcoma (Sarcoma Ewing)

Most common in adolescents; male : female ratio 1.5:1

Unclear histogenesis of the tumor; neural origin?

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Common Tumor Localizations

DFTP = Distal femur, Proximal tibia, Proximal humerus, Flat bones

🧠Risk Factor Acronym

R.I.N.K.

- Rapid bone growth

- Ionizing radiation

- New tumor in family history

- Ketogenic (Retinoblastoma → 500x higher osteosarcoma risk)

🧠Clinical Pearl

Retinoblastoma history is a red flag for osteosarcoma – always check for bone lesions in these patients.

2 secSelected Problems of Paediatric Oncology

21 secClinical Symptoms

Progressive pain and swelling.

Common general symptoms: fever, weight loss, increase in inflammatory parameters.

Tumor can develop in any bone; most common localizations:

Lower extremities (femur, tibia, fibula)

Pelvic bones (iliac bone, sacrum, ischium)

Ribs, sternum, vertebrae

Most frequent sites of metastasis: lungs, other bones, spine, OUN (ocular and nasal regions).

11 secDiagnostic Laboratory and Imaging. Diagnosis

Bone X-ray: Confirmation of osteolytic changes and bony swelling ("cabbage leaf sign"), bone fracture, soft tissue effusion.

Imaging diagnosis: Detection of metastatic changes.

11 secTreatment

Chemotherapy: Reduction of the primary lesion site, prevention of metastasis.

Radiotherapy.

Surgical removal: Bones that do not cause disability (e.g., rib, sternum).

Surgery with radiotherapy and/or chemotherapy.

3 minTumors of the Eye

Retinoblastoma

Most common malignant tumor of the eye in children.

Unilateral, multifocal, unilateral and bilateral cases:

60% sporadic unilateral case

25% hereditary bilateral case

15% hereditary unilateral case

80% of cases diagnosed by age 4.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics and Clinical Pearls

Metastasis sites: Remember LBSO (Lungs, Bones, Spine, OUN).

Retinoblastoma prevalence: Use 60-25-15 (60% sporadic unilateral, 25% hereditary bilateral, 15% hereditary unilateral).

X-ray sign: "Cabbage leaf sign" = osteolytic lesion with bony expansion (key for diagnosing osteolytic bone tumors).

Treatment priorities: Chemotherapy first (to shrink tumors before surgery/radiotherapy).

Clinical Symptoms

White pupillary reflex ("cat's eye") or strabismus - early symptoms.

Every child, even in the neonatal period, with symptoms of strabismus should be examined ophthalmologically under the fundus.

Symptoms related to:

Intracranial tumor growth during optic nerve compression.

Intraorbital tumor growth during nuchal cord compression.

Local tumor growth within the orbit.

Diagnostic Laboratory and Imaging. Diagnosis

Ophthalmological examination (confirmation of mass).

Orbital CT - confirmation of bone involvement in the orbit; evaluation of:

Intracranial tumor spread.

Orbital edema.

Intraorbital tumor size.

Imaging studies - searching for metastatic foci.

Treatment

1. Enucleation - either unilateral or bilateral, depending on the advanced disease process.

2. Radiotherapy, cryotherapy, laser hyperthermia, brachytherapy.

3. Chemotherapy - initial treatment in every case.

Orbital Hemangioma (haemangiom orbitae)

Most common orbital tumor in children.

Most frequent location - superior quadrant.

Benign tumor, appearing in the first week of life, tends to increase over several months and has a tendency for spontaneous regression and

disappearance by 7 years of age.

Only large tumors causing optic nerve compression are treated.

Rhabdomyosarcoma

Most common malignant orbital tumor in children.

The first symptom is rapidly increasing unilateral exophthalmos with accompanying bleeding.

🧠Memory Aid

Orbital Hemangioma:

Mnemonic: "Super 7" (superior quadrant, regression by 7 years).

Key: Benign, spontaneous regression; treat only if causing optic nerve compression.

Rhabdomyosarcoma:

Mnemonic: "Rapid Bleed" (rapid exophthalmos + bleeding).

Key: Most common malignant orbital tumor; early presentation with unilateral exophthalmos.

Comparison Table:

| Feature | Orbital Hemangioma | Rhabdomyosarcoma |

|-----------------------|--------------------------|--------------------------|

| Benign/Malignant | Benign | Malignant |

| Age of onset | Neonatal | Any age (common in children) |

| Symptoms | Strabismus, regression | Rapid exophthalmos, bleeding |

| Treatment | Surgery if optic nerve compression | Chemotherapy, radiotherapy |

2 secSelected Problems in Pediatric Oncology

9 secTesticular Tumors: Recognition and Treatment

Diagnosis: Based on CT scans of testicular tumors and biopsy of the mass.

Treatment: Chemotherapy with radiotherapy.

32 secPrimary Tumors from Germinal Cells

Prevalence:

3% of all tumors in children up to 15 years old are germinal cell tumors or other localized tumors.

Common Tumor in Newborns:

Testicular teratoma (1:40,000 live births).

Genetic Marker:

80–100% of cases confirm deletion at del 1p36.

Localization of Testicular Teratomas:

Inguinal canal, midline, retroperitoneal area, and neck region.

Benign vs. Malignant:

95% of testicular germ cell tumors are benign teratomas.

Most Common Germ Cell Tumor in Children:

Dysgerminoma (primary localization in the testis).

26 secClinical Symptoms

Dependent on Primary Lesion Location:

Teratomas:

Guillotined structure around the inguinal canal and pubic area.

No pressure on the inguinal canal or urinary tract → hematuria.

Prepubertal intraperitoneal localization → anuria.

Testicular Tumors:

Swelling of the testis (differential diagnosis: hydrocele, spermatic cord inflammation).

Abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, feeling of fullness (differential diagnosis: appendicitis).

Associated Findings:

Hydronephrosis, ureteral obstruction, bladder outlet obstruction (accompany teratomas).

40 secDiagnostic Laboratory and Imaging

Laboratory Markers:

AFP, hCG, CEA → used for recognition and monitoring of treatment.

Imaging Studies:

Evaluate primary tumor stage and presence of metastases.

Definitive Diagnosis:

Histopathological examination after surgical intervention.

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for Teratoma Localization: IMRN (Inguinal, Midline, Retroperitoneal, Neck).

Key Statistic:

95% of testicular germ cell tumors are benign teratomas.

Comparison Table:

| Tumor Type | Benign/Malignant | Common Location | Marker |

|------------------|----------------------|-----------------------------|------------|

| Teratoma | Benign | Inguinal, midline, neck | AFP, CEA |

| Dysgerminoma | Malignant | Testis | hCG |

Clinical Pearl:

Hydronephrosis and ureteral obstruction are pathognomonic for teratomas.

4 secSacrococcygeal Teratomas & Wilms' Tumor (Nephroblastoma) – Treatment & Key Features

10 secSacrococcygeal Teratomas

Newborns

Treatment: Surgery + possible chemotherapy if biomarkers are elevated.

Infants, Older Children, Extragonadal Teratomas

Treatment Protocol:

Induction chemotherapy

Surgery

Maintenance chemotherapy

7 secTesticular & Ovarian Cancer

Treatment: Depends on cancer stage.

Surgery (with or without induction chemotherapy)

Postoperative chemotherapy

42 secWilms' Tumor (Nephroblastoma)

Epidemiology

7–10% of all childhood cancers.

Age of onset: Most common in preschool children; 90% diagnosed by age 7.

Bilateral involvement: ~5–8% of cases (associated with nephroblastomatosis – incomplete maturation of nephrogenic cells).

Genetic Associations

Wilms' tumor is more common in children with:

- WAGR syndrome (Wilms' tumor – aniridia – genitourinary malformation – mental retardation)

- Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome (macroglossia, umbilical hernia, muscular hypertrophy, urinary tract infections)

- Denys-Drash syndrome (nephropathy, pseudohermaphroditism)

- Perlman syndrome (facial skeletal flattening, mandible retraction, genitourinary defects)

Associated Anomalies

Urinary/reproductive system: Hypopituitarism, cryptorchidism, renal hypoplasia, duplicated kidneys, horseshoe kidney

Musculoskeletal system: Defects

Circulatory system: Defects

Hemihypertrophy

41 secMemory Aid

Mnemonic for Genetic Syndromes

WAGR (Wilms, Aniridia, Genitourinary, Retardation)

Beckwith-Wiedemann (Big tongue, Belly button hernia, Big organs)

Denys-Drash (Disease, Developmental Disorders)

Perlman (Perfectly Plain Profile, Problems)

Clinical Pearl

Wilms' tumor is the most common childhood kidney cancer, often linked to genetic syndromes and congenital anomalies.

Bilateral tumors are strongly associated with nephroblastomatosis.

Treatment for sacrococcygeal teratomas in newborns depends on biomarker levels (chemotherapy may be needed).

Comparison Table: Teratoma Treatment

Patient Group

Treatment Protocol

Newborns

Surgery + chemotherapy (if biomarkers ↑)

Patient Group

Treatment Protocol

Infants/Older Children

Induction chemo → surgery → maintenance chemo

Extragonadal teratomas

Same as infants/older children

🧠Clinical Pearl

Key drugs for severe infections: Acyclovir (viral), amphotericin B (fungal), and dexamethasone (anti-inflammatory/diuretic).

28 secClinical Symptoms

Dependent on the location of the tumor within the kidney and stage of disease.

Most common initial symptoms:

Abdominal circumference increase

Hematuria

Proteinuria

Acute abdomen signs

Nausea, vomiting

Abdominal pain (effect of bleeding into the tumor)

Tenderness (effect of pressure from renal vessels or renin secretion by tumor cells).

Most common sites for metastasis:

Lungs

Liver

Bones

OUN (ocular nerves/ocular tumors).

Differential diagnosis:

Hydronephrosis

Pyelonephritis

Kidney cystic disease.

Physical examination.

Imaging studies:

USG and CT of OUN

CT of the chest wall

Bone scintigraphy in children with unfavorable histology tumor.

Assessment of disease stage:

Based on imaging studies

Intraoperative assessment

Tumor pathology structure (classification of tumor grade according to SIOP and classification of local extent of tumor spread according to National

Wilms Tumor Study modified SIOP).

2 secTreatment

Surgical treatment

Chemotherapy

Radiotherapy

50 secLiver Tumors

Prevalence: Comprise about 2% of all childhood tumors; more common in boys.

Fetal liver tumor (hepatoblastoma):

Etiology unknown

Similar to Wilms' tumor

Often associated with Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome and hemihypertrophy

Frequently observed in children with trisomy chromosomes 11, 20

Similar to rhabdomyosarcoma.

Hepatocellular carcinoma (hepatocarcinoma):

Often related to previous WZW A infection

Poorly responsive to chemotherapy.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics and Clinical Pearls

Metastasis sites (LLBO):

Lungs

Liver

Bones

OUN (ocular nerves/ocular tumors).

Hepatoblastoma associations:

Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome

Hemihypertrophy

Trisomy 11/20.

Hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) trigger:

WZW A infection (WZW = "wirusowe zapalenie wątroby" in Polish; likely hepatitis A).

Treatment triad (SCR):

Surgery

Chemotherapy

Radiotherapy.

Tumor staging classifications:

SIOP (International Society of Pediatric Oncology) for tumor grade

National Wilms Tumor Study modified SIOP for local tumor spread.

1 secPEDIATRIA

20 secClinical Symptoms

Asymptomatic growth of the primary lesion; 10% of cases are incidentally diagnosed.

In advanced cases:

Vomiting

Weight loss

Abdominal pain

Anorexia

Signs of acute abdomen

Very rarely jaundice

Osteopenia

Numerous fractures of long bones

Compressive bone fractures in case of hepatoblastoma

Most common site for metastases: lungs

28 secDiagnostic Laboratory and Imaging. Diagnosis

Normocytic anemia

Thrombocytosis (600 thousand or more)

↑ AFP (80% cases) – diagnostic evaluation and observation

TK – assessment of the primary lesion and metastatic lesions (enlarged hilar lymph nodes, intrahepatic and paraaortic lymph nodes)

USG of the abdominal cavity with Doppler method – assessment of tumor vascularity

CT scan of the abdomen – detection of solid masses in the right upper quadrant (6% cases)

3 secTreatment

Chemotherapy before surgery followed by surgical intervention

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

AFP (Alpha-fetoprotein): Key diagnostic marker in 80% of hepatoblastoma cases.

"AFO" (remember Asymptomatic growth, Fractures, Osteopenia) – hallmark features of hepatoblastoma.

"C before S": Chemotherapy precedes surgery in treatment protocol.

Metastases: Most common site is lungs (contrast with neuroblastoma, which often metastasizes to bones).

Imaging: CT scan detects solid masses in the right upper quadrant (6% of cases), while USG with Doppler evaluates tumor vascularity.

3 secSelected Problems of Pulmonology and Allergology in Childhood

21 secPriorities in Treating Infectious Diseases

The priority in treating infectious diseases is the appropriate selection of drugs (symptomatic medications, antiviral agents, antifungal agents,

chemotherapy, antibiotics) based on the observed clinical picture of the disease.

Antibiotic choice must always consider:

Current medical evidence (especially EBM-based clinical trials)

Local epidemiology

Changing drug sensitivity/pathogen susceptibility

1 minSinusitis

Definition: Inflammation of the nasal sinuses caused by obstruction of drainage/ventilation due to infection, allergy, and/or anatomical abnormalities

(anatomical paradox, bony protrusions, bulla).

Acute Sinusitis

Older children (≥5–6 years):

Mainly occurs as a complication of upper respiratory tract infections, childhood infectious diseases, or dental caries.

Acute frontal sinusitis:

More common in older children.

Frequently associated with acute maxillary/ethmoidal sinusitis due to upper respiratory tract infection.

Acute ethmoid sinusitis:

Predominantly affects younger age groups.

Disease course is often severe due to proximity to nasal cavity, orbits, and anterior cranial fossa.

Acute sphenoid sinusitis:

Rarely occurs in isolation.

Often accompanies other sinus infections.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Acute Sinusitis Types

F.E.S.M. (Frontal, Ethmoid, Sphenoid, Maxillary) – Age and Severity:

- Frontal: Older children, often secondary to maxillary/ethmoiditis.

- Ethmoid: Younger children, severe due to anatomical location.

- Sphenoid: Rare, usually coexists with other sinusitis.

- Maxillary: Common in older children (linked to dental caries).

🧠Clinical Pearl

EBM and local epidemiology are critical for antibiotic selection in infectious diseases.

Anatomical abnormalities (e.g., bulla, bony protrusions) are key causes of sinusitis in children.

2 secSinusitis: Etiology, Symptoms, Diagnosis, and Treatment

9 secEtiology

Bacterial infection – 90% of infections are caused by S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae, M. catarrhalis.

Viral infections – rhinoviruses, flu virus, parainfluenza.

25 secClinical Symptoms

Fever, loss of appetite, irritability, drowsiness.

Pain around the eye socket, nasal congestion, dripping or runny nose with thick mucus.

Nasal discharge, swelling of the nasal passages, presence of thick mucus in the nasal passages.

In acute sinusitis:

Thick mucus in the nasal passages.

Dripping mucus down the back of the throat.

Unpleasant smell from the mouth.

Watery and persistent cough.

5 secDiagnosis

History taking.

Laryngological examination.

Imaging studies: CT sinuses (primary), MRI sinuses (rarely).

22 secTreatment

Empirical Therapy

Antibiotic therapy (14–21 days):

1st generation amoxicillin or amoxicillin/penicillamine acid.

In case of penicillin allergy: clindamycin; second-generation cephalosporins.

Surgical Therapy

Ethmoidectomy in case of sinusitis complications.

Drainage of frontal sinuses (Beck’s drainage) in case of failure of treatment for frontal sinusitis.

Percutaneous puncture of the maxillary sinus in case of fluid presence.

44 secRecurrent Sinusitis

Risk Factors

Anatomical abnormalities of the lateral nasal wall (nasal septum deviation).

Enlarged adenoids.

Allergic rhinitis.

Prolonged foreign body in the nasal passages.

Nasal injuries.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics

Bacterial causes (90%): SHM – S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae, M. catarrhalis.

Viral causes: RFP – rhinovirus, flu, parainfluenza.

🧠Comparison Table: Empirical Antibiotics

Antibiotic

Use

1st-gen amoxicillin

First-line for bacterial sinusitis

Clindamycin

Alternative for penicillin allergy

2nd-gen cephalosporins

Alternative for penicillin allergy

🧠Clinical Pearls

Duration of antibiotic therapy: 14–21 days (critical for resolution).

Beck’s drainage is specific to frontal sinusitis complications.

Percutaneous puncture is indicated for maxillary sinus fluid.

Anatomical risk factors (e.g., nasal septum deviation) are common in recurrent cases.

Acute Laryngitis

Occurs in: Older children and adolescents during school age

Etiology:

Viral infection

Laryngeal inflammation

Clinical Symptoms:

Cough (dry)

Inspiratory stridor (especially in younger children)

Nasal congestion

Sometimes conjunctivitis

Treatment:

Symptomatic treatment

Acute Epiglottitis

Definition:

Inflammation of the epiglottis and laryngopharyngeal folds

Swelling, thickening, and edema of the laryngopharyngeal folds

Age distribution:

Mainly occurs in children aged 2–7 years (peaks at 3–4 years)

Etiology:

90% of cases caused by H. influenzae

Clinical Symptoms:

Fever

Severe throat pain (especially when swallowing)

Drooling

Barking cough

Stridor on inspiration or expiration (rapidly developing inspiratory stridor with wheezing quality)

Swollen, red, and edematous epiglottis

Treatment:

Immediate hospitalization

Ensure airway patency

Antibiotic therapy (third-generation cephalosporins)

Antipyretics

Hydration

Cool inhalations with physiological saline

Maintain high humidity and temperature at 18–20°C

Acute Subglottic Laryngitis

Definition:

Inflammation of the subglottic area

🧠Memory Aid

Comparison Table:

| Condition | Age Group | Pathogen | Key Symptom | Treatment Focus |

|------------------------|-------------------|------------------|--------------------------------------|-------------------------------|

| Acute Laryngitis | School-age | Viral | Dry cough, inspiratory stridor | Symptomatic |

| Acute Epiglottitis | 2–7 years (peak 3–4) | H. influenzae | Severe throat pain, drooling, stridor | Airway, antibiotics, hydration |

| Acute Subglottic Laryngitis | All ages | Viral | Stridor, respiratory distress | Symptomatic, humidified air |

Mnemonic for Epiglottitis Symptoms:

Fever, Throat pain, Drooling, Stridor → F-T-D-S

Clinical Pearl:

- Epiglottitis is a medical emergency requiring immediate airway management and antibiotic therapy.

- Laryngitis in younger children may present with inspiratory stridor, while epiglottitis is more severe and associated with H. influenzae.

3. Antibiotic therapy only if lower respiratory tract inflammation is confirmed

3 secSelected Problems of Pneumology and Allergology of Childhood

30 secDiagnostics. Recognition

Clinical findings:

Dullness of percussion sound, bronchial murmur, crackles, and fine rales over the affected lung.

Single-focal percussive changes (mainly in older children).

Multifocal, diffuse changes (in infants and younger children).

Laboratory findings:

Leukocytosis with a shift to the left in the leukogram.

High inflammatory markers: ESR, CRP.

Imaging:

Radiological examination of the lungs: shadowing of the lung lobe or segments.

Concomitant exudative pleurisy (approx. 20% of cases).

Bacteriological tests: Required for confirmation.

12 secTreatment

Setting:

Infants and younger children: Hospital-based administration.

Older children: Outpatient administration (if appropriate).

Antibiotic therapy:

First-line: Penicillin G, amoxicillin with clavulanic acid, cephalosporins.

In case of resistance: Macrolides.

20 secStaphylococcal Pneumonia

Clinical Symptoms

High fever.

Cough, angina.

Gastrointestinal complaints:

Abdominal distension.

Vomiting.

Diarrhea.

Diagnostics

Laboratory findings:

Significantly elevated leukocytosis and other inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP).

Anemia.

Imaging:

Early stage: Bronchopneumonia pattern.

Late stage: Cysts, abscesses, vesicles, pneumothorax, empyema.

Bacteriological tests:

Positive results in blood culture or pleural fluid culture.

6 minMemory Aid

Mnemonic for Antibiotic Choices

PACM

- P: Penicillin G

- A: Amoxicillin with clavulanic acid

- C: Cephalosporins

- M: Macrolides (for resistance)

Clinical Pearls

Staphylococcal pneumonia is more common in infants and often presents with gastrointestinal symptoms (abdominal distension, vomiting,

diarrhea).

Differential diagnosis for focal lung changes: older children (single-focal) vs. infants (multifocal, diffuse).

Radiological clues:

Early stage: Bronchopneumonia.

Late stage: Abscesses, empyema, pneumothorax.

Atypical Pneumonia

Clinical Symptoms

Moderate rise in body temperature

Very intense cough

Memory Aid

Complications of pneumonia: Use PEEP mnemonic:

Pleural effusion

Empyema

Pleural abscess

Ear infection (skin infection)

Atypical pneumonia treatment: MACE (Macrolides, Azithromycin, Clarithromycin, Erythromycin)

Diagnosis clues:

Leukocytosis + normal blood smear = atypical pneumonia

Radiological findings (middle/lower zones, pleural effusion, hilar lymphadenopathy) = key for atypical pneumonia

Clinical pearl: Atypical pneumonia often presents with inadequate auditory changes relative to radiological findings.

Pediatric Surgery: Key Points for LEK Exam

Topic: Common Pediatric Surgical Conditions and Management

1. Appendicitis in Children

Clinical Presentation:

Abdominal pain (often starts periumbilical, migrates to right lower quadrant).

Vomiting, fever, leukocytosis.

Red flags: Age <2 years, delayed presentation, perforation risk.

Diagnostic Criteria:

History and physical exam (McBurney’s point tenderness).

Imaging: Ultrasound preferred in children <5 years; CT in older patients.

Treatment:

Surgical: Laparoscopic appendectomy (gold standard).

Medical: Consider in stable patients with early diagnosis (antibiotics + close monitoring).

2. Intussusception

Clinical Presentation:

Classic triad: Abdominal pain (intermittent), vomiting, currant jelly stool.

Age: Most common in children <2 years.

Diagnostic Criteria:

Ultrasound (target sign).

Air or barium enema (diagnostic and therapeutic).

Treatment:

Non-surgical: Successful in 80% of cases with enema reduction.

Surgical: Required if enema fails, perforation, or obstruction.

3. Hypospadias

Clinical Presentation:

Abnormal urethral opening on ventral surface of penis.

Complications: Urinary incontinence, penile curvature.

Diagnostic Criteria:

Physical exam at birth.

Treatment:

Surgical correction: Preferred between 6–18 months of age.

Goals: Cosmetic appearance, normal urinary function.

4. Congenital Diaphragmatic Hernia (CDH)

Clinical Presentation:

Prenatal: Ultrasound detects herniation of abdominal organs into thorax.

Postnatal: Respiratory distress, cyanosis, scaphoid abdomen.

Diagnostic Criteria:

Chest X-ray (mediastinal shift, bowel loops in thorax).

Treatment:

Immediate: Stabilization (ventilation, surfactant).

Surgical: Repair via thoracotomy or laparotomy (postponed if unstable).

5. Inguinal Hernia in Children

Clinical Presentation:

Bulge in groin, reducible, may cause pain.

Risk of incarceration: 10% in infants, 20% in adults.

Diagnostic Criteria:

Physical exam (reducible swelling).

Treatment:

Surgical: Repair via herniotomy (preferred in infants) or hernioplasty (older children).

Memory Aid

Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

- Appendicitis in Children:

- "Pain migration" is a key sign.

- "Red flags" (age <2, delayed presentation) indicate higher perforation risk.

Intussusception:

"Currant jelly stool" is a hallmark but not always present.

Air enema is both diagnostic and therapeutic.

Hypospadias:

"Early surgery" (6–18 months) avoids complications like curvature.

CDH:

"Prenatal ultrasound" is the first-line diagnostic tool.

"Stabilize first, repair later" to avoid surgical risks.

Inguinal Hernia:

"Herniotomy" is preferred in infants to avoid recurrence.

Comparison Table: Appendicitis vs. Intussusception

| Feature | Appendicitis | Intussusception |

|----------------------|----------------------------------|----------------------------------|

| Age | Any age, but common in children| <2 years |

| Pain | Localized to RLQ | Intermittent, crampy |

| Diagnostic Test | Ultrasound/CT | Ultrasound (target sign) |

| Treatment | Appendectomy | Air/barium enema |

Wrodrzne zapalenia płuc

Najczęściej wywołane są przez paciorkowce grupy B oraz E. coli.

Clinical Symptoms

Dyspnea (tachypnoe, irregular breathing, retractions, grunting between breaths)

Cough

Over lung fields: wheezing and crackles

Fever

Rash, sweating

Diagnostic Tests

Chest X-ray

Blood culture, urine culture, sputum culture, cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) or pleural effusion

Serological test for TORCH pathogens

Treatment

1. Empirical antibiotic therapy: Penicillin with aminoglycosides

2. After obtaining bacterial test results: Adjust treatment according to the antibiogram

3. Symptomatic and supportive treatment as in case of generalized infection

2.3 Sepsis neonatorum (sepsis, septicaemia)

Definition: Severe systemic infection with changes in organ function and tissue-level disturbances, leading to organ dysfunction

Types of sepsis:

- Sepsis: Symptoms of generalized inflammation + single-organ dysfunction

- Severe sepsis: Septic shock or septic syndrome + multiorgan dysfunction

Memory Aid

Pathogens in wrodrzne zapalenia płuc:

Mnemonic: "B and E" = Streptococcus group B + E. coli

Sepsis classifications:

Sepsis → Severe sepsis → Septic shock (progression severity)

TORCH pathogens:

Toxoplasmosis, Other (syphilis, varicella), Rubella, Cytomegalovirus, Herpes simplex

Mnemonic: TORCH (acronym for pathogens)

Antibiotic therapy:

Penicillin + aminoglycosides (first-line empirical treatment)

Antibiogram = key for adjusting therapy post-culture results

3 secSelected Problems of Pediatric Pulmonology and Allergology

14 secFactors Predisposing to Acute Otitis Media

Short, wide, poorly positioned tympanic membrane

Frequent upper respiratory tract infections

Tendency to ear drainage and vomiting (gastroesophageal reflux)

Enlarged tonsils

Nasal congestion

Chronic rhinitis

Nasal polyps

Enlarged nasal turbinates

8 secEtiology

Primarily viral; bacterial:

Streptococcus pneumoniae

Haemophilus influenzae

Moraxella catarrhalis

Streptococcus groups A and B

Staphylococcus aureus

Pseudomonas aeruginosa

Gram-negative bacteria

35 secClinical Symptoms

Infants:

Fussy child, lack of appetite, refusal to feed, interrupted sucking during feeding, ear pulling and rubbing

Pronounced general symptoms: fever, irritability, coughing, upper respiratory tract infection

Older Children:

Ear pain

General symptoms of upper respiratory tract infection

Viral Phase of the Disease:

Mild ear pain

Sensation of fullness in the ear

Conductive hearing loss

Tinnitus

Fever

Mild tympanic membrane swelling

Bacterial Phase of the Disease:

Severe ear pain

Conductive hearing loss

High fever

Pulsating ear pain

Decreased ability to hear

Ear swelling and redness

17 secAcute Pyogenic Otitis Media

Acute middle ear inflammation in viral infections (influenza virus, measles, mumps, chickenpox, EBV)

Symptoms: severe ear pain without tinnitus

Otoscopic examination: tympanic membrane with purulent discharge or seropurulent discharge

Characteristic: disappearance of pain after spontaneous rupture of pus cells

2 minAcute Necrotizing Otitis Media

Rarely observed in children: acute middle ear inflammation during viral infections (diphtheria, measles)

Often progresses to complications in the form of mastoiditis

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Predisposing Factors:

Short tympanic membrane

Frequent infections

Tonsils and Gastroesophageal reflux

Nasal issues (congestion, polyps, turbinates)

🧠Viral vs. Bacterial Phase Comparison:

Feature

Viral Phase

Bacterial Phase

Pain

Mild

Severe

Tinnitus

Present

Absent

Fever

Mild

High

Tympanic Membrane

Mild swelling

Swelling + redness

🧠Clinical Pearl:

Acute pyogenic otitis media often resolves after spontaneous rupture of the tympanic membrane.

Acute necrotizing otitis media is a rare but serious complication of viral infections, requiring prompt evaluation for mastoiditis.

Pathophysiology

- Formation of necrosis

- Damage to facial nerve and inner ear

- Leads to sensorineural hearing loss

Treatment

1. First episode of acute middle ear infection

- Expectant management (observation 1–2 days)

- Follow-up visit within 24–48 hours

- Local treatment: anti-inflammatory, analgesic, antipyretic

1. Maintenance of local inflammatory state and symptoms

2. Antibiotic therapy:

I°: Amoxicillin

II°: Amoxicillin with aminoglycosides, cefuroxime, or clarithromycin

3. Acute necrotizing middle ear infection

4. Targeted antibiotic therapy

5. Paracentesis

6. Pressure on affected bony tympanic membrane

7. Discharge of serous or purulent material from the tympanic cavity

8. Prevention methods

9. Breastfeeding

10. Bottle feeding in upright position with averted head

11. Avoid exposure to tobacco smoke

12. Limit contact with individuals infected with viral respiratory infections

13. Avoid pacifier use >10 months old

14. Immunoprophylaxis (vaccines)

Complications of acute middle ear infection

Intracranial complications

- Mastoiditis

- Facial nerve damage or paralysis

- Inner ear inflammation

- Inflammation of the mastoid bone

Other complications

- Suppurative otitis media

- Otitis externa

- Acute sinusitis

- Meningitis

- Brain abscess

Memory Aid

- Antibiotic Mnemonic: "I° Amoxicillin, II° Amoxicillin + Aminoglycosides/Cefuroxime/Clarithromycin"

- Prevention Tips: "Bottle Upright, Smoke Avoid, Pacifier Limit, Vaccines Apply"

- Intracranial Complication Mnemonic: Mastoiditis, Facial nerve damage, Inner ear inflammation, Mastoid bone inflammation (M-F-I-M)

- Complication Comparison Table:

| Type | Intracranial | Other |

|------------------|------------------|-------------------|

| Examples | Mastoiditis | Otitis externa |

| | Facial paralysis | Meningitis |

| | Inner ear inflammation | Brain abscess |

| | Mastoid bone inflammation | Acute sinusitis |

2 secWysiękowe zapalenie ucha środkowego

20 secDefinicja

Obecność płynu w przewężeniu ucha środkowego z nienaruszonym błoną bębenkową.

Najczęściej występuje u dzieci do 10 lat po nawrotach infekcji dróg oddechowych i ucha środkowego, u dzieci z:

językiem wisiem (tonsillar uvula),

odwodnieniem przegrody nosowej,

zapaleniem sinusów twarzowych (hipertrofia przedsionków nosowych),

zaburzeniami immunologicznymi i endokrynologicznymi (np. niedobór odporności).

19 secFormy i objawy

Forma ostra

Objawy infekcji górnych dróg oddechowych: gorączka, ból ucha, szumy uszne, przewodnikiowe zaburzenia słuchu z perforacją błony bębenkowej.

Forma przewlekła

W badaniu otoskopowym:

Gruba, żółto-brązowa błona bębenkowa.

Czasem wzrosty w przewężeniu ucha środkowego (otitis media adhesiva).

W badaniu audiometrycznym: przewodnikiowe lub mieszane zaburzenia słuchu.

22 secLeczenie

1. Leki przeciwzapalne, przeciwbólowe, środki rozkurczowe błony śluzowej nosa, expectoranty (np. przy grudkowatym wydzieliny, u pacjentów

z przewlekłym zapaleniem sinusów).

2. Wyciąganie wydzieliny (evacuation of exudate).

3. Tympanostomia lub tympanocentez do usunięcia wydzieliny.

4. Drainage wentylacyjny do równoważenia ciśnienia między uchem środkowym a środowiskiem zewnętrznym oraz odprowadzania wydzieliny z

przewężenia przez przewód słuchowy.

2 secPrzewlekłe zapalenie ucha środkowego

3 minDiagnostyka

Diagnoza oparta na:

Trwały lub nawrotowy purpurowy wyciek z ucha,

Przewodnikiowe zaburzenia słuchu z perforacją błony bębenkowej.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonik dla objawów formy ostrej (FETTP):

Fever (gorączka)

Ear pain (ból ucha)

Tinnitus (szumy uszne)

Transmission hearing loss (przewodnikiowe zaburzenia słuchu)

Perforation (perforacja błony bębenkowej)

🧠Porównanie form ostrej i przewlekłej:

Cecha

Forma ostra

Forma przewlekła

Wyciek

Tympanic membrane perforation

Eardrum perforation

Błona bębenkowa

Nienaruszona

Gruba, żółto-brązowa

Wydzielina

Zwykle nieobecna

Czasem obecna (otitis media adhesiva)

Leczenie

Tympanostomia, tympanocentez

Drainage wentylacyjny

🧠Kluczowe leki:

Anti-inflammatory drugs (np. NSAIDs)

Decongestants (np. pseudoephedrine)

Expectorants (np. guaifenesin)

🧠Kliniczny pearl:

Przewlekłe zapalenie ucha środkowego wymaga długotrwałego monitorowania i interwencji chirurgicznej (np. implantacja rurki

wentylacyjnej) w przypadku niepowodzenia leczenia konserywacyjnego.

Etiology

Gram-negative rods (Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Proteus spp., Enterobacteriaceae), anaerobic bacteria and streptococci; often polymicrobial

infection!

Chronic Simple Middle Ear Inflammation

Most common form of ear inflammation in the developmental stage.

Clinical Symptoms

Persistent or intermittent purulent or mucopurulent discharge (worsening during upper respiratory tract infections)

Loss of middle ear membrane

Conductive hearing loss

Destruction of ossicles

Progression without pain

Treatment

Application of careful ear drops

Suctioning of exudate

Sterile irrigation with eardrops

Protection of the ear from immersion

Removal of inflammatory foci in the upper respiratory tract

Surgical treatment

Chronic Perlakowicz Middle Ear Inflammation

Perforation characterized by spreading destruction of middle ear space and mastoid bone

Most commonly seen in children with hearing impairment, frequent upper respiratory tract infections and middle ear inflammation, as well as in

children with immunodeficiencies

Clinical Symptoms

Persistent or intermittent discharge from the ear

Loss of middle ear membrane characterizing a tense (affects the tympanic part) or retracted state

Conductive or mixed hearing loss

In case of acute exacerbations: fever, pain at the mastoid process may occur

Can progress with complications

Treatment

Only surgical

🧠Memory Aid

Key Differences Between Chronic Simple and Perlakowicz Middle Ear Inflammation

| Feature | Chronic Simple Middle Ear Inflammation | Chronic Perlakowicz Middle Ear Inflammation |

|-------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|

| Perforation | No | Yes (spreading destruction) |

| Common in | General population | Children with hearing impairment, immunodeficiency|

| Hearing loss type | Conductive | Conductive or mixed |

| Treatment | Medical + surgical | Only surgical |

Etiology Mnemonic:

PPE = Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Proteus spp., Enterobacteriaceae (Gram-negative rods involved in infection).

Clinical Pearl:

Perlakowicz inflammation is always surgical due to structural destruction, unlike simple chronic inflammation which may have medical

management options.

4 secWybrane problemy pulmonologii i alergologii wieku dziecięcego

Strona 209

36 secRecurrent Granulomatous Middle Ear Inflammation

Definition

- Chronic recurrent, purulent middle ear inflammation with bone destruction despite absence of perforation.

Clinical Symptoms

- Significant bony atrophy, mainly in the tense part with granules.

- Painful, thick purulent ear discharge.

- Complicated course with polyps in the eustachian tube and nasal cavity.

Treatment

- Exclusively surgical.

Complications of Recurrent Middle Ear Inflammation

Intra-cranial

Peripheral facial nerve damage

Inner ear inflammation

Mastoid bone pyramid inflammation

Intra-cerebral

Temporal lobe abscess

Subdural abscess

Otitis media with effusion

Cerebrospinal fluid meningitis

Brain or brain abscess

54 secAtopic Dermatitis (Eczema, AZS)

Definition

- Chronic, relapsing condition with:

- Intense itching

- Typical localization and characteristic skin appearance

- Co-occurrence of other atopic diseases in the patient or family

Epidemiology

- Most common childhood disease

- Frequency in children <7 years: 15–20%

- 90% of cases: Symptoms appear by age 5

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics for Complications

Intra-cranial: PIM (Peripheral facial nerve damage, Inner ear inflammation, Mastoid bone inflammation)

Intra-cerebral: TSSO (Temporal lobe abscess, Subdural abscess, Otitis media with effusion, Cerebrospinal fluid meningitis)

🧠Atopic Dermatitis Key Points

Chronic, Relapsing, Itching, Typical skin changes, Atopy in family

CRITICA mnemonic for quick recall

🧠Comparison Table: Recurrent vs. Non-Recurrent Middle Ear Inflammation

Feature

Recurrent Granulomatous Inflammation

Non-Recurrent Inflammation

Bone destruction

Yes (without perforation)

No

Surgical treatment

Exclusive

May include medical therapy

Complication risk

High (intra-cranial/intra-cerebral)

Lower

2 minAtopia (AZS)

Clinical Features

In most children, AZS symptoms manifest as mild cow's milk protein intolerance ("milk allergy"), with symptoms resolving in early childhood.

Only 10% persist until age 10.

AZS is often the first manifestation of atopy in children predisposed to allergic diseases.

40% of children with AZS develop asthma or allergic rhinitis later in life.

Etiopathogenesis

Genetic Factors

30–50% risk of atopic disease if one parent has it.

50–80% risk if both parents have atopic disease.

Environmental Factors

Food allergens:

Cow's milk protein (β-lactoglobulin, α-lactoglobulin)

Egg white (ovalbumin, yolk)

Fish, soy, wheat, legumes, serums, moldy fruits, seaweed, red wine

Environmental derivatives:

House dust mites (Dermatophagoides pteronyssinus)

Plant pollen, animal dander, fungi, and spores

Infections:

Bacterial: Staphylococcus aureus

Viral: HSV

Fungal: Pityrosporum ovale, C. albicans

Irritants:

Soap, detergents, solvents, dyes, lacquers, wool

Extreme temperatures, dry/cold air, high humidity

Scratching

Other factors:

Humoral/cellular immune response defects

Entodermal barrier defects

Neurovegetative disturbances

Stress

Diagnosis and Recognition

Diagnosis based on confirmation of at least 3 out of 4 main symptoms (per Hanifin and Rajka criteria) or at least 3 from numerous minor

symptoms.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Acronym for Environmental Factors: G-E-N-E-S

G: Genetic factors (risk from parental atopy)

E: Environmental allergens (food, dust mites, pollen)

N: Infections (bacterial, viral, fungal)

E: Irritants (chemicals, temperature extremes)

S: Stress and neurovegetative disturbances

🧠Clinical Pearl

Milk allergy is often transient, resolving by age 3–5 in most children.

Hanifin and Rajka criteria emphasize objective symptoms (e.g., eczema, pruritus) over subjective complaints.

"Polished" cheeks and "polished" nails are hallmark features of neonatal and adult AZS, respectively.

Lichenification is a key sign in late childhood and adulthood stages.

1 minTreatment and Preventive Measures

1. Breastfeeding:

2. Extend breastfeeding (exclusive until 6 months).

3. Eliminate strong food allergens from the breastfeeding mother’s diet.

4. Mixed feeding:

5. Introduce formula milk.

6. For high-risk children:

Hypoallergenic formulas (whey protein hydrolysates).

Casein hydrolysates for cow’s milk protein allergy.

7. Dietary introduction:

page 201

8. Gradually introduce highly allergenic foods as late as possible.

9. Environmental control:

10. Avoid airborne allergens.

11. Ensure proper room ventilation to reduce humidity.

12. Remove feather pillows and duvets from the bedroom.

4 secSelected Issues in Pediatric Pulmonology and Allergology

Page 213

25 secEnvironmental Management for Atopic Dermatitis

Temperature control: Maintain room temperature ≥ 22°C

Dust reduction:

Use appropriate dust extractors

Clean wet surfaces

Remove curtains and furniture with fabric covers from the bedroom

Eliminate plush toys and pets from the bedroom

Sun exposure: At least 3 hours daily

Pollen avoidance:

Keep windows closed during peak pollen hours

Air rooms at night or after rain

24 secSkin Care Measures

Moisturization: Use daily skincare preparations

Gentle cleansing:

Mild soaps with minimal foam and neutral pH

Clothing care:

Wash new clothes before first use

Use liquid detergents

Avoid non-breathable fabrics (e.g., synthetic materials)

Apparel:

Loose, breathable cotton clothing

Prevent excessive sweating

Skin protection:

Avoid injuries and scratching

Apply short-term bandages to rashes

Wear gloves at night to prevent blisters

19 secTreatment of Exacerbations

Topical Therapies

Corticosteroids:

Clobetasol propionate (Dermovate)

Piroxicam metazone (Elocom)

Fluticasone propionate (Cutivate)

Hydrocortisone ointment (Laticort, Hydrocortison)

Calcineurin inhibitors:

Tacrolimus

Pimecrolimus

Antihistamines: Oral or topical use

Antibiotics: For bacterial infections

Tailoring Treatment

Adjust based on:

Severity of atopic changes

Frequency of exacerbations

Individual patient needs

10 secContraindications to Protective Vaccinations

The current immunization schedule for mandatory and recommended vaccines (updated annually) is available on the websites of health and

epidemiological stations.

52 secFactual Contraindications to Vaccination

1. Acute infectious diseases or exacerbation of chronic illness.

2. Severe congenital or acquired immune deficiencies – vaccination against live vaccines (measles, mumps, rubella, chickenpox).

3. Blood transfusion or immunoglobulin preparations – contraindicated in relation to live vaccines for 3–6 months following administration.

4. Documented severe allergic reactions of anaphylactic type after a previous dose of the same vaccine or evidence of identical reaction to any

component of that vaccine.

5. Progressive neurological diseases, congenital metabolic disorders with central nervous system damage, children undergoing

neurodevelopmental assessment.

6. Body weight below 2000 g and children born before 32 weeks gestation – contraindicated for measles vaccination.

7. Exercise great caution in the administration of vaccines in cases of children with autoimmune diseases – use an individualized immunization

schedule.

35 secStates and Conditions Not Constituting Contraindications to Vaccination (According to WHO)

1. Upper respiratory tract infections or diarrhea with a temperature below 38.5°C and other non-progressive signs of infection.

2. Allergy, asthma (without exacerbation), or other symptoms of atopic dermatitis, rhinitis, or nasal congestion.

3. Prematurity, low birth weight.

4. Malnutrition.

5. Breastfeeding.

6. Family history indicating the presence of allergies in the family.

7. Antibiotic therapy, low doses of steroids or local application (e.g., on the skin or locally).

8. Skin inflammation, rash, or localized skin infection.

40 secMemory Aid

Mnemonic for Factual Contraindications

Acute illness, Severe immunity, Blood transfusion, Allergy, Neurological issues, Weight/gestation, Autoimmune diseases → ASBANWA (remember key

categories).

Comparison Table: Contraindications vs. Non-Contraindications

Contraindications

Non-Contraindications

Live vaccines in immune deficiencies

Mild infections (<38.5°C)

Allergic reactions to vaccines

Asthma (stable)

Neurological disorders

Prematurity

Low birth weight (<2000g)

Breastfeeding

Autoimmune diseases

Family history of allergies

Clinical Pearls

Live vaccines (e.g., MMR, varicella) are contraindicated in severe immune deficiencies.

Wait 3–6 months after blood transfusion/immunoglobulin before administering live vaccines.

Mild illnesses (e.g., colds, diarrhea without fever) do not contraindicate vaccination.

Autoimmune diseases require individualized schedules – not absolute contraindications.

2 secCongenital and Acquired Infections

6 secSeptic Shock

Definition: Multiple organ failure with significant drop in blood pressure, causing impaired organ perfusion.

8 secEpidemiology

Incidence of sepsis in newborns by birth weight:

2500 g: 1–3%

2500–1000 g: 4–10%

< 1000 g: 50%

14 secEtiopathogenesis

Primary cause: Bacterial infection (most common).

Other causes: Viral and fungal infections.

Common gram-positive bacteria:

Staphylococcus epidermidis

Group B Streptococcus

Pathophysiology:

Bacterial endotoxins trigger generalized inflammatory response.

Release of cytokines and inflammatory cascade.

22 secClinical Symptoms in Newborns

Sepsis suspected if at least three of the following are present:

Digestive tract dysfunction:

Reluctance to suck

Retention of food content

Vomiting

Abdominal distension

Behavioral changes:

Anxiety or apathy

Respiratory disorders:

Rapid breathing

Apnea

Body temperature instability:

Hyper- or hypothermia

Peripheral perfusion disorders:

Tachycardia or bradycardia

Skin changes:

Pallor

Cyanosis

Jaundice

Petechiae

4 secClinical Manifestations

Acute renal failure:

Oliguria

Anuremia

Increased creatinine and urea

38 secDiagnostics

Initial diagnosis: Based on clinical picture.

Confirmed by laboratory tests: At least 3 of the following criteria (not fully listed in text).

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Sepsis Symptoms in Newborns

Digestive dysfunction

Behavioral changes

Respiratory issues

Temperature instability

Perfusion disorders

Skin changes

🧠Incidence Rates by Birth Weight

Birth Weight

Sepsis Incidence

> 2500 g

1–3%

2500–1000 g

4–10%

< 1000 g

50%

🧠Clinical Pearl

Key pathogens: Group B Streptococcus and Staphylococcus epidermidis are most commonly isolated in neonatal sepsis.

Early signs: Nonspecific symptoms (e.g., temperature instability, apathy) require high suspicion for sepsis in vulnerable infants.

2 secContraindications to Vaccinations

Page 215

16 secKey Contraindications

9. Chronic heart, lung, kidney, and liver diseases (in the stable phase)

10. Stable neurological condition in diseases of the nervous system such as cerebral palsy and Down syndrome

11. Prolonged physiological jaundice of the neonatal period

43 secRisk Assessment

Contracting an infectious disease carries a higher risk of complications than complications from vaccination.

Children with the above conditions are particularly at risk of contracting certain infectious diseases (e.g., whooping cough, measles).

Timely completion of all protective vaccinations is more important for these children than for their healthy peers.

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for Contraindications:

Chronic diseases (heart, lung, kidney, liver)

Neurological stability (cerebral palsy, Down syndrome)

Jaundice (prolonged neonatal)

Clinical Pearl:

Children with chronic conditions or neurological disorders require urgent vaccination to prevent severe complications from diseases like

pertussis or measles. Prioritize vaccines even if contraindications exist in the stable phase.

Pytania testowe z pediatrii

2 secLEK Exam Practice: Pediatrics (Questions 1–4)

12 secQuestion 1

The TORCH syndrome includes all infectious agents except:

a) toxoplasmosis

b) rubella

c) cytomegalovirus

d) varicella zoster virus

e) herpes virus

Correct answer: d) varicella zoster virus

12 secQuestion 2

Intracranial hemorrhages are typical of:

a) congenital rubella

b) congenital toxoplasmosis

c) congenital syphilis

d) CMV infection

e) answers b + d

Correct answer: e) answers b + d

25 secQuestion 3

The finding of unequal knee height in an infant lying flat on its back with the knees and hips flexed is called:

a) a positive Allis or Galezzi sign

b) a negative Allis or Galezzi sign

c) a positive Trendelenburg sign

d) a negative Trendelenburg sign

e) none of the above

Correct answer: a) a positive Allis or Galezzi sign

37 secQuestion 4

Screening for congenital hypothyroidism in newborns includes the determination of:

a) fT4

b) fT3

c) T4

d) TSH

e) T4 + TSH

Correct answer: e) T4 + TSH

🧠Memory Aid

TORCH Syndrome Acronym:

Toxoplasmosis

Other (e.g., syphilis)

Rubella

Cytomegalovirus

Herpes (e.g., HSV)

Exception: Varicella zoster virus (VZV) is not part of TORCH.

Intracranial Hemorrhage in Congenital Infections:

CMV and toxoplasmosis → common causes of intracranial hemorrhage.

Allis/Galezzi Sign Mnemonic:

"Unequal Knees = Positive Allis" (implying hip dislocation).

Congenital Hypothyroidism Screening:

TSH + T4 are the gold standard tests for newborn screening.

- a) IgA

Correct answer: d) IgG

Definition of "kernicterus"

- b) single-nucleated cells found in cerebrospinal fluid during bacterial meningitis

- d) static encephalopathy

6. Mnemonic: M-C-W-S (remember the first letters)

7. Serological Conflict Antibodies

8. IgG crosses the placenta → responsible for hemolytic disease in newborns.

9. IgM does not cross placenta.

10. Kernicterus

11. Untreated neonatal jaundice → neurotoxic bilirubin levels → basal ganglia damage.

12. Clinical pearl: Kernicterus is a preventable complication with timely phototherapy.

13. Anti-D IgG Administration

14. Given in: stillbirth, manual placenta, multiple pregnancies, cesarean.

15. Excluded in: amniocentesis (no fetal-maternal hemorrhage risk).

🧠Comparison Table: Anti-D Indications

Situation

Requires Anti-D?

Stillbirth delivery

✅ Yes

Manual placenta

✅ Yes

Multiple pregnancies

✅ Yes

Cesarean section

✅ Yes

Amniocentesis

❌ No

Neonatal Resuscitation and Respiratory Distress

Sodium Bicarbonate Administration

Body weight of newborn × acid deficit × 0.3

Risks: Rapid administration may cause intracranial bleeding.

Questions and Answers

1. What is the most common cause of aseptic viral meningitis?

a) Enteroviruses

b) Coronaviruses

c) Arboviruses

d) Retroviruses

e) Orthomyxoviruses

Correct answer: a) Enteroviruses

2. Displacement of the structures in the posterior part of the brain (brainstem and cerebellum) into the spinal canal, mechanical

hydrocephalus and defects of the bony structures in the area of the base of the skull are the features of:

a) Arnold-Chiari

b) Dandy-Walker

c) Sturge-Weber

d) Lennox-Gastaut

e) Bourneville syndrome

Correct answer: a) Arnold-Chiari

3. Polyhydramnios in the obstetric history, increasing dyspnea and cyanosis, and runny discharge from the oral cavity raise the suspicion of:

a) choanal obstruction

b) pulmonary sequestration

c) Pierre Robin syndrome

d) congenital diaphragmatic hernia

e) esophageal atresia

Correct answer: c) Pierre Robin syndrome

Memory Aid

- Aseptic viral meningitis: Enteroviruses are the most common cause (think "Enteric" viruses).

- Arnold-Chiari malformation: Mnemonic: Posterior brain structures displaced into spinal canal → "Arnold-Chiari = Brainstem and cerebellum issues."

- Pierre Robin syndrome: Polyhydramnios + Dyspnea + Oral discharge → "PR = Pierre Robin (facial abnormalities, airway obstruction)."

- Key distinction: Arnold-Chiari vs. Dandy-Walker → Arnold-Chiari involves brainstem/cerebellum displacement; Dandy-Walker involves cerebellar

vermis hypoplasia and posterior fossa cysts.

🧠Differentiating hypercalciuria types

Feature

Cause

🧠Acute pyelonephritis in infants

Remember: Fever, vomiting, and apathy (options 1, 3, 5) are most specific – diarrhea and meningismus are red herrings.

Diagnostic Criteria for Sepsis

Elevated procalcitonin levels in blood plasma.

Elevated CRP protein level.

Leukocyte count:

Above 30,000 or below 4000.

Platelet count: Below 100,000.

Metabolic disorders:

Metabolic acidosis.

Increased lactate levels.

Coagulation disorders:

Increased FDP (fibrinogen degradation products) and D-dimer levels.

Decreased antithrombin III activity.

Elevated IL-6 and IL-8 levels in blood plasma.

Confirmation of Diagnosis

Positive bacterial culture result (blood culture).

Diagnostic Imaging

Localization of infection focus:

Chest X-ray (chest cavity and abdominal area).

Ultrasound of the abdomen and head.

CT scan.

Differential Diagnosis

Other infections:

Cytomegalovirus.

Varicella virus.

Enteroviruses.

Toxoplasmosis.

Candida.

Congenital heart defects:

Left ventricular hypoplasia.

Respiratory dysfunction syndrome or congenital respiratory disorders.

Genetically inherited metabolic block:

Galactosemia.

Congenital endocrine disorders:

Hypopituitarism.

Nephrogenic diabetes insipidus with salt loss.

Hemorrhage to the umbilical cord.

Pneumonia.

Neonatal hemolytic disease.

Metabolic disorders:

Hypoglycemia.

Hypocalcemia.

Hyponatremia.

Treatment

Empirical antibiotic therapy until a positive culture is obtained.

Initial treatment:

Administer 2 antibiotics from groups such as:

Penicillin derivatives.

Aminoglycosides.

Memory Aid

Mnemonics for diagnostic criteria:

CRP, Leukocytes, Platelets, Metabolism, Coagulation, ILs → Use "CRP-LP-M-C-IL" to recall key parameters.

Differential diagnosis grouping:

Infections: Viruses (CMV, varicella, enteroviruses, toxoplasmosis, Candida).

Congenital: Heart defects (left ventricular hypoplasia), endocrine disorders (hypopituitarism, nephrogenic diabetes insipidus).

Metabolic: Galactosemia, hypoglycemia, hypocalcemia, hyponatremia.

Clinical pearl: Empirical antibiotic therapy is critical before culture results; prioritize penicillin derivatives + aminoglycosides for broad

coverage.

Options:

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Question 30: Meningitis Pathogen

Key Clue: Lumbar puncture findings (pleocytosis with lymphocytes, elevated protein, low glucose) suggest bacterial meningitis.

Differential Diagnosis Mnemonic:

Neisseria meningitidis → Neisseria (common in children, rapid onset, purulent pharynx)

Streptococcus pneumoniae → Streptococcus (often in adults, may follow upper respiratory infection)

Enteroviruses → Enterovirus (viral, typically lower pleocytosis, normal glucose)

🧠Question 31: Hypertension & Sports

Mnemonic for Grades:

Grade I: Systolic 140–159 mmHg or diastolic 90–99 mmHg → Allowed with monitoring

Grade II: Systolic ≥160 mmHg or diastolic ≥100 mmHg → Contraindicated if uncontrolled

Key Point: Uncontrolled Grade II hypertension is the only contraindication for competitive sports.

🧠Key Points for Neonatal GI Bleeding

Most common cause: Enterohemorrhagic E. coli (e.g., HUS in neonates).

Other options (ulceration, rotavirus) are less common in neonates.

🧠UTI in Children: Critical Thresholds

10⁷ CFU/mL E. coli in a 4-year-old → treatment required.

10⁴ CFU/mL Pseudomonas aeruginosa → contamination, repeat test.

Neonatal UTI most commonly caused by gram-negative rods (e.g., E. coli), not Klebsiella (common in adults).

🧠Contraindications: Signs vs. Symptoms

Brudzinski's, Kernig's, neck stiffness → signs of meningitis (not contraindications).

Jellinek's sign → not a contraindication (rarely used clinically).

🧠Mnemonic for Question 47

"Jellinek is not a contraindication" → Remember Jellinek’s sign is the only option not a contraindication.

listed options (e.g., Down syndrome, sinusitis) are not standard contraindications.

Question 49: Rhabdomyosarcoma (embryonal type) is the most common pediatric soft tissue tumor. Mnemonic: "Rhabdo" =

rhabdomyosarcoma.

Question 50: Duhring’s disease (dermatitis herpetiformis) is the skin manifestation of celiac disease. Compare to other gluten-related

syndromes:

Celiac disease = intestinal damage

Duhring’s disease = skin rash (blisters, itching)

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for Class A vs. Class B:

- Class A = Acute/Specific Conditions (e.g., anaphylactoid reaction, myocardial infarction).

- Class B = Chronic/Complicated Conditions (e.g., chronic reactions, pyelonephritis).

Duplicate Sections Note:

- Sections 6 and 7 are identical. Treat them as redundant in the original text—likely a formatting error.

Comparison Table:

| System | Class A (Acute/Specific) | Class B (Chronic/Complicated) |

|--------|--------------------------|-------------------------------|

| Allergic Reactions | Anaphylactoid, Sjögren’s | Atopic, Chronic |

| Hematological | Hemolytic anemia, leukemia | Thrombocytopenia, hemorrhage |

| Respiratory | Asthma, bronchiolitis | Pneumonia, bronchopneumonia |

| Urinary/Reproductive | Nephropathy, hematuria | Pyelonephritis, hydronephrosis |

| Cardiovascular | Myocardial infarction, Churg-Strauss | Pulmonary embolism, cerebral hemorrhage |

Clinical Pearl:

- Hemolytic anemia and myeloblastic leukemia are repeated in sections 2, 6, and 7. Highlight these as high-yield topics for the LEK exam.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics for Abdominal Pain Classification (Barr's)

Functional, Recurrent, Organic, Psychogenic, Somatic → FROPS

🧠Key Hematologic Terms

ALL (Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia) vs. ANLL (Acute Nonlymphoblastic Leukemia)

ALL = Lymphoid blasts

ANLL = Myeloid blasts

🧠Diarrhea Types

Secretory = Excess fluid (e.g., cholera)

Osmotic = Unabsorbed solutes (e.g., lactose intolerance)

Infectious = Pathogens (e.g., E. coli)

Post-antibiotic = Dysbiosis

🧠Congenital Anomalies

Arnold–Chiari malformation = Cerebellar tonsils herniate through foramen magnum

Ebstein's anomaly = Tricuspid valve displaced downward

🧠Renal Conditions

Renal agenesis = Absent kidney (unilateral or bilateral)

Renal aplasia = Underdeveloped kidney (often with other anomalies)

2 secZAKAŻENIA WRODZONE I NABYTE | 27

3 minManagement of Septic Shock

Key Interventions

Fluid therapy:

Crystalloids

5% albumin

Ringer's solution with dextrose

Supportive measures:

Nutritional support

Correction of acid-base balance

Management of electrolyte disturbances

Respiratory support:

Mechanical ventilation

Oscillatory ventilation

Nitric oxide

Cardiac support:

Dopamine

Dobutamine (drugs increasing cardiac muscle contractility)

Circulatory improvement:

Pentoxifylline (for peripheral and pulmonary circulation)

Steroids:

Hydrocortisone (in septic shock)

Fungal prophylaxis:

Fluconazole

Anticoagulant/anti-inflammatory agents:

Recombinant human activated protein C (rhAPC)

Antithrombin III

Thrombosis prophylaxis:

Low molecular weight heparin

Pain relief:

Fentanyl

Morphine

Pyralgina

Immunoglobulin infusions:

Premature infants with body mass <1500 g

Leukopenia treatment:

Granulocyte colony-stimulating factor (G-CSF)

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Key Drugs in Septic Shock

DOPES

- Dopamine

- Oscillatory ventilation

- Pentoxifylline

- Endogenous anticoagulants (rhAPC, antithrombin III)

- Steroids (hydrocortisone)

🧠Comparison Table: Critical Interventions

Category

Intervention

Purpose

Fluid Resuscitation

Crystalloids, 5% albumin

Restore intravascular volume

Respiratory Support

Mechanical ventilation, nitric oxide

Improve oxygenation and lung compliance

Cardiac Support

Dopamine, dobutamine

Enhance cardiac output

Anti-inflammatory

rhAPC, steroids (hydrocortisone)

Reduce systemic inflammation

Thrombosis Prophylaxis

Low molecular weight heparin

Prevent venous thromboembolism

🧠Clinical Pearl

In septic shock, early administration of fluids and vasopressors (e.g., dopamine) is critical to prevent multi-organ failure. Pentoxifylline is

uniquely indicated for improving microcirculation in sepsis. rhAPC is a high-cost agent with anti-inflammatory properties, but its use is limited

due to cost and availability.

🧠Mnemonics and Clinical Pearls

Hypercalcemia types:

Absorptive (e.g., hyperparathyroidism)

Renal (e.g., sarcoidosis)

Resorptive (e.g., malignancy)

Mnemonic: A-R-R (Absorptive, Renal, Resorptive)

Wilms tumor:

Page 139–184 – Remember it’s a pediatric kidney tumor; associate with abdominal mass and hematuria.

Hypoglycemia:

Pages 38–39, 80 – Link to insulinoma or starvation. Use "Hypoglycemia = Low glucose, High insulin".

Huntington disease:

Pages 99–100 – Chorea, dementia, psychiatric symptoms – "Huntington = Chorea, Dementia, Psychosis".

Herpes zoster:

Pages 86–89 – Unilateral rash, pain, postherpetic neuralgia – "Shingles = Shingles, Herpes, Zoster".

Hodgkin’s disease:

Pages 93, 181 – B symptoms (fever, night sweats, weight loss), Reed-Sternberg cells – "Hodgkin = B symptoms, Reed-Sternberg".

Mnemonics

Memory Aid

- Mnemonics for Key Terms:

- Ospa wietrzna (16, 86–89): Remember "Ospa" as a rash (86–89).

- Osteosarcoma (189–190): "Osteo" = bone, "sarcoma" = cancer.

- Otitis media acuta (204–206): "Otitis" = ear infection.

- Persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn (53–54): "Persistent" = prolonged, "hypertension" = high pressure.

- Phenylketonuria (PKU) (45–47): "Phenyl" = amino acid, "ketone" = metabolic byproduct.

Comparison Table:

| Term | Page Range | Key Association |

|-----------------------------|----------------|-------------------------|

| Osteosarcoma | 189–190 | Bone cancer |

| Persistent pulmonary hypertension | 53–54 | Newborn respiratory issue |

| Phenylketonuria (PKU) | 45–47 | Amino acid metabolism |

Clinical Pearls:

Ospa wietrzna is a viral infection causing a rash, often with fever and malaise.

Osteosarcoma is the most common primary bone cancer in children and adolescents.

Persistent pulmonary hypertension in newborns requires immediate intervention to prevent hypoxia.

Phenylketonuria is a genetic disorder requiring strict dietary management to prevent intellectual disability.

59 secMemory Aid

Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Glaucoma Types:

Open-angle glaucoma (55–56): Chronic, asymptomatic, intraocular pressure elevation.

Closed-angle glaucoma (36–37): Acute, painful, vision loss, requires urgent treatment.

Mnemonic: "O for Open (chronic), C for Closed (crisis)."

Autoimmune Diseases:

Celiac disease: Diagnosed with tTG (116) and EMA (126) antibodies.

Mnemonic: "T and E for Celiac Disease."

Cancers:

Rhabdomyosarcoma (RMS): 174, 192.

Retinoblastoma: 180–181.

Mnemonic: "RMS and Retinoblastoma: R for Rhabdo and Retino."

Adrenal Insufficiency:

Primary adrenal insufficiency (130) vs. Chronic adrenal insufficiency (131).

Pearl: Primary is due to adrenal gland failure; chronic may be iatrogenic (e.g., steroid use).

Duplicates to Watch For:

Stensen's duct appears under A and S (101). Ensure consistency in context.

ARDS:

Acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS): 43–44 and 42–43.

Pearl: ARDS is a severe lung injury with hypoxemia and bilateral infiltrates.

Neonatal Emergencies:

Neonatal sepsis (24–27) and Septicemia (24).

Mnemonic: "Sepsis = Septicemia + systemic infection."

Memory Aid

- VAPP: Remember "Vaccine-Associated Paralysis" (VAPP) as a rare complication of live attenuated polio vaccines.

- VIPoma: Use the acronym VIP (Vasoactive Intestinal Peptide) to recall the hormone secreted by VIPomas, causing watery diarrhea and flushing.

- VDRL: Recall Venereal Disease Research Laboratory test for syphilis.

- Williams team: Link to Williams syndrome (cardiofacial features, hyperacusis) for quick recall.

- Wilms tumor: Remember Wilms Tumor = Womb Tumor (though it’s renal, the mnemonic helps distinguish from ovarian tumors).

- Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome: Think Waterhouse-Friderichsen = Widespread Fulminant Sepsis (due to meningococcal infection).

CHOROBY WYMAGAJĄCE NIEZWŁOCZNEJ INTERWENCJI CHIRURGICZNEJ

1. WADY UKŁADU ODDECHOWEGO

1.1 Zarośnięcie nozdrzy tylnych (atresia choanae, choanal atresia)

Przyczyna: Rozwojowa anomalia spowodowana wrodzonym zatorami w nozdrzach tylnych.

Występowanie: Może być jednostronna lub dwustronna. Częstsza u kobiet.

Związane choroby:

Przepuklina niebieska (cleft palate)

Przepuklina tracheo-esofagalna (tracheoesophageal fistula)

Przepuklina przeponowa (diaphragmatic hernia)

Hydrops fetalis (fetal hydrops)

Objawy kliniczne:

Trudności w oddychaniu

Zwiększona częstotliwość oddechowa (>60/min)

Wsuniecia międzyżebrowe (intercostal retractions)

Cyjanozę (cyanosis)

Charakterystyczna cecha: Poprawa tlenowania podczas płaczenia.

Kluczowe zasady diagnostyczne:

W każdym noworodku z ostrą niewydolnością oddechową należy najpierw wykluczyć atresię nozdrzy tylnych (wstrzykanie kathetera przez

nozdrze noworodka).

Leczenie:

Umieszczenie rurki oralno-nasalnej lub tracheostomia.

1.2 Wrodzona przepuklina przeponowa (hernia diaphragmatica congenita)

Przyczyna: Wrodzona anomalia spowodowana przemieszczaniem się organów z jamy brzusznej przez defekt przepony do jamy piersiowej.

Skutki:

Nierozwój płuc (hipoplazja płuc)

Niewydolność oddechowa w wyniku ucisku na płuca

Związane choroby:

Przepuklina niebieska (cleft palate)

Przepuklina tracheo-esofagalna (tracheoesophageal fistula)

Hydrops fetalis (fetal hydrops)

🧠MEMORY AID

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Atresia choanae:

CRA (Cyanosis during Crying, Relieves Airway) – cyjanozę poprawia płaczenie.

Kluczowe pytanie: "Czy noworodek z niewydolnością oddechową ma poprawę tlenowania podczas płaczenia?"

Hernia diaphragmatica congenita:

DHL (Diaphragm Hernia, Lung hypoplasia) – łączenie przepukliny przeponowej z hipoplazją płuc.

Związane choroby: Pamiętaj o przepuklinie niebieskiej i tracheo-esofagalnej.

🧠Porównanie kluczowych cech

Zarosnięcie nozdrzy tylnych

Przepuklina przeponowa

Objawy: Trudności w oddychaniu, cyjanozę

Objawy: Niewydolność oddechowa, ucisk na płuca

Leczenie: Rurka oralno-nasalna/tracheostomia

Leczenie: Interwencja chirurgiczna (najpóźniej w 24 godziny)

Kluczowy znak: Poprawa tlenowania podczas płaczenia

Kluczowy znak: Hipoplazja płuc, ucisk na płuca
