3 secClinical Presentation and Management of Viral Infection

9 sec1. Symptoms

Fever (temperature > 38°C)

Cough

Shortness of breath

Fatigue

Muscle pain

Headache

Loss of taste or smell

Diarrhea

Nausea and vomiting

22 sec2. Precautions

Avoid close contact with people who are sick.

Wash hands frequently for at least 20 seconds using soap and water, or use an alcohol-based hand sanitizer if soap and water are not available.

Cover your mouth and nose when coughing or sneezing with a tissue or elbow.

Stay home when you are sick.

3 sec3. Testing

Nasopharyngeal swab

Blood test for antibodies

51 sec4. Treatment

1. Rest and hydration

2. Medication for fever (acetaminophen)

3. Antiviral drugs if prescribed by a healthcare provider

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics and Clinical Pearls

Symptoms: Use the acronym FCSFMHLDDN (Fever, Cough, Shortness of breath, Fatigue, Muscle pain, Headache, Loss of taste/smell,

Diarrhea, Nausea).

Precautions: Remember "WASH, COVER, STAY" (Wash hands, Cover mouth/nose, Stay home when ill).

Testing: "Swab and Blood" (Nasopharyngeal swab + antibody blood test).

Treatment: "R, H, A, A" (Rest, Hydration, Acetaminophen, Antivirals).

🧠Comparison Table: Key Features

Category

Key Points

Symptoms

Fever, cough, shortness of breath, fatigue, headache, loss of smell

Precautions

Hand hygiene, respiratory etiquette, isolation

Testing

Nasopharyngeal swab, antibody detection

Treatment

Supportive care, antipyretics, antivirals

Note: This content is adapted from a Polish medical review text and formatted for clarity and memorability.

23 secDiabetes Mellitus

Definition: Hardening of the liver (hepatitis) caused by blood sugar.

Symptoms:

1. Often feeling hungry and thirsty.

2. High body temperature.

3. Tiredness.

4. Small amounts of urine.

5. Liver disorders.

Treatment:

1. Diet: Limit sugar and fats, increase protein.

2. Medications: Insulin, glucocorticosteroids.

3. Other: Psychological support.

Preparations:

1. Insulin (Insulin)

2. Glucocorticosteroids (Prednisolone)

4 minLymphatic Processing Disease

Definition: Liver dysfunction caused by increased protein production.

Symptoms:

1. Frequent feeling of hunger and thirst.

2. High body temperature.

3. Fatigue.

4. Small amounts of urine.

Treatment:

1. Diet: Restrict protein, increase carbohydrates and fats.

2. Medication: Glucocorticosteroids, antibiotics.

Preparations:

1. Prednisolone (Glucocorticosteroid)

2. Antibiotics

🧠Memory Aid

Diabetes Mellitus vs. Lymphatic Processing Disease:

| Feature | Diabetes Mellitus | Lymphatic Processing Disease |

|---------------------|-------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|

| Cause | Blood sugar → liver hardening | Increased protein → liver dysfunction |

| Diet Focus | Limit sugar/fats, increase protein | Restrict protein, increase carbs/fats |

| Key Medications | Insulin, glucocorticosteroids | Glucocorticosteroids, antibiotics |

Mnemonic for Symptoms:

THIRST (Thirst, Hunger, Increased temperature, Fatigue, Small urine output, Liver issues).

Glucocorticosteroid Use:

Shared in both diseases (Prednisolone).

Differentiate by additional meds: Insulin vs. antibiotics.

CHOROBY WYMAGAJĄCE NIEZWŁOCZNEJ INTERWENCI CHIRURGICZNEJ | 29

Diaphragmatic Hernia

Incidence

- 1:4000 live newborns

- Location: Left side in >90% of cases

Clinical Symptoms

- Abdominal distension (wedge-shaped abdomen)

- Cyanosis, pallor

- Weakening of respiratory sounds on the side of the diaphragmatic hernia

- Shift in the position of the cardiac apex beat

Diagnosis

- Chest X-ray and abdominal X-ray

- Abdominal ultrasound

Management

- Mouth-to-mouth ventilation is contraindicated! (Risk of esophageal rupture)

- Intubation

- Oxygen therapy

- Vasodilating drugs for increased intrathoracic pressure

- Referral to pediatric surgery ward

1.3 Esophageal Atresia with Tracheoesophageal Fistula

Definition

- Congenital anomalies:

- Esophageal interruption

- May be accompanied by tracheoesophageal fistula

- Often associated with:

- Alimentary tract malformations

- Cardiovascular system malformations

Clinical Symptoms

- Prenatal ultrasound:

- Multiple findings in amniotic fluid (newborn does not float)

- Absence of fetal stomach visualization (earliest sign)

- Postnatal:

- Cyanosis, pallor, coughing

- Frequent frothy saliva from the mouth

🧠Memory Aid

Key Mnemonics

- Diaphragmatic Hernia Symptoms: Cyanosis, Pallor, Respiratory weakening, Abdominal distension (CPRA)

- Management Steps: Intubation, Oxygen, Vasodilators, Referral (IOVR)

- Esophageal Atresia Ultrasound Signs: Amniotic fluid findings, No fetal stomach (AN)

Clinical Pearl

- Mouth-to-mouth ventilation is a red flag in suspected diaphragmatic hernia due to risk of esophageal rupture. Always prioritize intubation.

Comparison Table: Esophageal Atresia Signs

| Prenatal | Postnatal |

|-----------------------------|-----------------------------|

| Amniotic fluid findings | Cyanosis, pallor, coughing |

| No fetal stomach visualization | Frothy saliva from mouth |

Clinical Presentation

- Regurgitation of food through nose and mouth during feeding attempts.

- Enlarged abdomen that increases with crying and crying.

- Symptoms temporarily subside after oral cavity cleansing.

Diagnosis

- Ultrasound (from around 18th week of pregnancy).

- Chest X-ray and abdominal X-ray. Diagnostic radiological examination with the administration of a contrast agent orally.

Immediate Management

1. Cleansing.

2. Placement of a nasogastric tube through nose or mouth for saliva suction.

3. High positioning (at an angle of 60°) on the back.

4. Oxygen therapy (oxygen tent, CPAP, mechanical ventilation).

5. No oral feeding allowed.

6. Urgent surgical correction.

Postoperative Complications

Early complications:

- Separation of the band,

- Reflux,

- Narrowing at the site of the bandage.

Late complications:

- Esophageal reflux,

- Softening of the palate,

- Peristalsis disorders of the esophagus.

1.4 Pierre Robin Syndrome, Pierre Robin Sequence

Definition

Syndrome of congenital malformations affecting the face and mouth. Characterized by incomplete development of the lower jaw, retraction of the chin

backward, tongue falling back, cleft palate, and incomplete development of the external ear.

Clinical Symptoms

- Characteristic facial features.

- Difficulty breathing immediately after birth (incomplete lower jaw development, tongue falling back).

Memory Aid

- Pierre Robin Syndrome Mnemonic: "PR" → Palate Retracted Robin Syndrome.

- Early vs. Late Complications:

| Early | Late |

|-------------------------|---------------------------|

| Band separation | Esophageal reflux |

| Reflux | Palate softening |

| Bandage narrowing | Esophageal peristalsis disorders |

- Clinical Pearl: Pierre Robin Sequence is often associated with micrognathia (small lower jaw) and glossoptosis (tongue falling back), leading to

airway obstruction in newborns.

3 secChoroby wymagające niezwłocznego interwencji chirurgicznej | 31

26 sec1. Problemy z odżywianiem

Założenie: Odżywianie przez rurkę przełykową lub specjalne przekładki.

Powiązane wady:

Organy: oczy, uszy, wady serca (najczęstsze: VSD, ASD), układ nerwowy (CNS), układ moczowy i rozrodczy, szkielet.

Krokowy podejście

1. Utrzymanie pozycji leżącej lub pionowej, aby zapobiec przesuwaniu języka w kierunku gardła.

2. Wstawienie rurki ustno-nosowej lub maski laryngowej.

3. W razie potrzeby wykonanie tracheostomii.

Leczenie

Korygacja chirurgiczna pozycji języka.

Alternatywa: tracheotomia.

1 min2. Wrodzone wady serca

Definicja

Wady serca, przy których utrzymanie krążenia i życia noworodka zależy od tętniczki płucno-środnikowej (ductus arteriosus). Podzielone na trzy grupy:

2.1 Wady z mieszanym przepływem krwi

Charakterystyka: Systemowe i płucne krążenie są niezależne. Tętniczka płucno-środnikowa utrzymuje życie noworodka, ponieważ jest miejscem

mieszania się krwi tętniowej i żylniej.

Przykład: Transpozycja dużych naczyń (Transposition of Great Arteries – TGA).

2.2 Wady z mieszanym przepływem płucnym

Charakterystyka: Przepływ płucny jest ograniczony z powodu braku wylotu prawej komory. Do utrzymania krążenia płucnego konieczny jest

przepływ lewo-prawo przez tętniczkę.

Przykłady:

a. Krytyczne zwężenie lub atrezja tętniczki płucnej (PS lub PA bez VSD).

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemoniki i klika

TGA (Transposition of Great Arteries):

Mnemonik: "TGA – Two Great Arteries swapped."

Klika: "TGA: Prawa komora serca zasilana jest tętniczką płucną, lewa – tętniczką aortalną."

🧠Porównanie wad serca

Grupa wad

Charakterystyka

Przykład

Mieszany przepływ krwi

Systemowe i płucne krążenie niezależne

TGA

Mieszany przepływ płucny

Ograniczony przepływ płucny, konieczny przepływ lewo-prawo

PS/PA bez VSD

🧠Acronyms

VSD: Wada przegrody międzykomorowej (Ventricular Septal Defect).

ASD: Wada przegrody międzyprzedsionkowej (Atrial Septal Defect).

1 secPEDIATRICS

4 minCongenital Heart Defects: Classification and Clinical Features

1. Ductus-Dependent Pulmonary Blood Flow Defects

These defects depend on the patency of the ductus arteriosus for adequate pulmonary blood flow.

Includes:

- Tetralogy of Fallot with critical pulmonary stenosis (or atresia) (TOF with critical PS)

- Tricuspid atresia (TA) with pulmonary stenosis

- Other complex heart defects with pulmonary stenosis or atresia

- Severe Ebstein's anomaly

2. Ductal-Dependent Systemic Flow Defects

These defects impede left ventricular outflow. Systemic circulation relies on right-to-left blood flow through the ductus arteriosus.

Includes:

a. Critical aortic stenosis (AS)

b. Critical coarctation of the aorta (CoA)

c. Interrupted aortic arch (IAA)

d. Hypoplastic left heart syndrome (HLHS)

Clinical Features

Severe cyanosis (in ductus-dependent blood mixing defects and ductus-dependent pulmonary blood flow defects)

Signs of heart failure (in ductus-dependent systemic blood flow defects)

Key Clinical Patterns:

1. Cyanosis-Dominant Defects:

- Neonate appears well immediately after birth.

- Condition deteriorates within 2–3 days due to ductus closure.

- Presents with generalized central cyanosis, hypoxemia, hypoxia, and metabolic acidosis.

1. Circulatory Failure-Dominant Defects:

2. Neonate appears normal for several hours after birth.

3. Rapid deterioration occurs with ductus closure.

Additional Signs (due to impaired left heart/aortic flow):

- Pulmonary venous congestion

- Dyspnea, tachycardia, difficulty in sucking

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics and Clinical Pearls

Ductus-Dependent Pulmonary Flow Defects:

TOF, TA, Ebstein, and others → Remember as TTE (Tetralogy, Tricuspid, Ebstein).

Ductus-Dependent Systemic Flow Defects:

AS, CoA, IAA, HLHS → Use acronym ACIH (Aortic, Coarctation, Interrupted, Hypoplastic).

Cyanosis Timing:

"2–3 days" → Ductus closure triggers cyanosis in TOF/TA.

Heart Failure Signs:

"Dyspnea, tachycardia, poor feeding" → Linked to HLHS/CoA.

Key Differentiator:

Cyanosis vs. Circulatory Failure → Cyanosis = pulmonary flow defect; Heart failure = systemic flow defect.

Diagnostics

Peripheral pulse examination

Unpalpable on all extremities – critical AS, HLHS;

Unpalpable on lower extremities – CoA, IAA.

Ineffective hyperoxia test (no rise in PaO₂ and SaO₂ during breathing pure oxygen) and hyperventilation.

Pulse asymmetry test on upper and lower extremities (important for distinguishing conduit defects).

Chest X-ray.

ECG.

UKG (ultrasonography).

Immediate Management

1. Administer prostaglandin E1 (prostin) i.v. in continuous infusion 0.01–0.02 µg/kg/min.

2. Strict monitoring of the newborn during prostin administration. Common side effects include:

3. Apnea

4. Hyperthermia

5. Facial cyanosis

6. Tachycardia

7. Irritability

8. Avoiding oxygen supplementation (an increase in arterial blood oxygen saturation causes narrowing of the pulmonary artery and contributes to

an unfavorable drop in lung resistance).

9. Securing ventilation (intubation if necessary).

10. Correcting acid-base imbalance and electrolyte disturbances.

11. Infusion of dopamine for wad with reduced systemic flow and left ventricular dysfunction.

12. Referral to a cardiac surgical center.

Treatment

Palliative procedures (partially improving circulation function):

Rashkind procedure (atrioseptostomy balloon) – TGA, TA.

Systemic-pulmonary shunt, usually modified Blalock-Taussig in wad with reduced pulmonary flow (connection of the descending aorta to the

pulmonary artery) – tetralogy Fallot.

Balloon angioplasty for critical coarctation of the aorta.

🧠Memory Aid

Pulse examination mnemonics:

All extremities unpalpable → Critical AS, HLHS (think "All extremities = All serious conditions").

Lower extremities unpalpable → CoA, IAA (remember "Lower = CoA and IAA").

Prostin use:

Prostaglandin E1 (prostin) is the first-line drug for maintaining ductal patency in critical congenital heart defects.

Avoid oxygen supplementation in conditions with pulmonary atresia (e.g., TGA, TA) to prevent pulmonary artery narrowing.

Palliative procedures table:

Procedure

Indication

Rashkind procedure

TGA, TA

Modified Blalock-Taussig

Tetralogy Fallot (reduced pulmonary flow)

Balloon angioplasty

Critical coarctation of aorta

Key management pearls:

Prostin infusion requires constant monitoring due to risks of apnea and hyperthermia.

Dopamine is used for systemic flow support in left ventricular dysfunction.

34 | PEDIATRICS

Definitive correction of the defect.

3. DIGESTIVE TRACT DEFECTS

3.1. Umphalocele and Gastroschisis

Umphalocele (omphalocele):

- The displacement of part of the intestines through a defect in the abdominal wall near the umbilicus of the newborn.

Gastroschisis:

- A congenital defect in which the abdominal wall is separated.

- The intestines and abdominal organs are located outside the abdominal cavity.

Emergency treatment:

1. Gastric suctioning.

2. Cover the hernia sac with sterile gauze and saline solution and secure with a dressing to prevent infection.

3. Prophylactic antibiotic administration.

4. Urgent surgical correction.

3.2 Duodenal atresia

A congenital defect caused by narrowing and obstruction of the lumen of the duodenum.

Clinical features:

- Obstetric history of polyhydramnios and double bariatric sign.

- Biliary vomiting in the first hours of life.

- Electrolyte disturbances.

- Abdominal distension.

🧠Memory Aid

Umphalocele vs. Gastroschisis:

Umphalocele: Hernia sac present, umbilical involvement.

Gastroschisis: No hernia sac, abdominal wall defect away from umbilicus.

Duodenal atresia mnemonic (PABE):

Polyhydramnios

Abdominal distension

Bile vomiting

Electrolyte disturbances

Emergency treatment for abdominal wall defects:

Gastric suctioning

Cover hernia sac

Antibiotics

Surgical correction (G-C-A-S)

DISEASES REQUIRING IMMEDIATE SURGICAL INTERVENTION

3.3 Intestinal Atresia

Definition

A congenital defect caused by narrowing of the lumen of the small intestine, causing obstruction of the digestive tract.

Clinical Symptoms

- Vomiting of bile

- Abdominal distension

- Blood on rectal examination

Diagnostics

- Abdominal X-ray in upright position (distended bowel loops with fluid levels in the upper abdominal cavity)

3.4 Meconium Ileus

Definition

Meconium ileus is one of the first symptoms of cystic fibrosis.

Clinical Symptoms

- Symptoms of lower intestinal obstruction

- Biliary vomiting

- Abdominal distension

- Constipation

- Thick sticky meconium

Diagnostics

- Abdominal X-ray in upright position (distended bowel loops with fluid levels in the upper abdominal cavity)

🧠Memory Aid

Comparison Table: Intestinal Atresia vs. Meconium Ileus

Feature

Intestinal Atresia

Meconium Ileus

Cause

Congenital narrowing of intestine

Cystic fibrosis (first symptom)

Vomiting

Bile present

Bile present

Meconium

Not thick or sticky

Thick, sticky

X-ray Finding

Fluid levels in upper abdomen

Fluid levels in upper abdomen

Mnemonic for X-ray Diagnosis

FLUA = Fluid Levels in Upper Abdomen (seen in both conditions).

Clinical Pearl

Meconium ileus is a red flag for cystic fibrosis. Always consider this diagnosis in neonates with intestinal obstruction and thick meconium.

3.5 Malformacje odbytu i odbytnicy (anorectal malformations)

The severity of these defects can vary greatly (from minor defects to complex malformations of the urinary-rectal system).

Diagnostyka

Examination of newborn's perineum.

USG of the abdominal cavity.

CT, MR of the abdominal cavity.

Often accompanied by other defects:

Urinary tract reflux, urethral valves, hypospadias,

Intussusception of the intestine, duodenal atresia.

3.6 Uwięznięta przepukлина pachwinowa

Objawy kliniczne

Restlessness in children.

Vomiting.

Loose stools with blood and mucus.

Hardness on palpation in the area of incarceration.

Swelling of the scrotum.

Postępowanie doraźne

1. Lowering the child's head.

2. Administration of analgesics and sedatives.

3. Attempt to reduce the hernia in warm water: if it does not work, there are urgent indications for surgical intervention.

4. WADY UKŁADU NERWOWEGO

4.1 Otwarta przepukлина oponowo-rdzeniowa

The defect is usually localized around the lumbar-sacral region.

Exposure leads to part of the spinal cord or cauda equina.

🧠Memory Aid

Anorectal malformations:

Associated defects: Use "URID" (Urinary tract reflux, Intestinal atresia, etc.).

Diagnosis: "USG + CT/MR" (imaging modalities).

Incarcerated inguinal hernia:

Management steps: "LARS" (Lower head, Analgesics, Reduce in warm water, Surgery if failed).

Open myelomeningocele:

Location: Lumbar-sacral region.

Complications: Meningitis, hydrocephalus, tethered cord syndrome.

Clinical pearl: For hernia reduction, warm water is critical to relax the muscle and facilitate manual reduction.

14 secACUTE NEUROLOGICAL SYNDROMES

They often occur during the course of many diseases in newborns. They may present with stupor or loss of consciousness, symptoms such as irritability,

abnormal motor response, tremors, fever, and jaundice.

5 minHYPOGLYCEMIA

This is a decrease in blood glucose levels. In breastfed infants for the first 3 hours after birth, the lowest glucose level should be > 35 mg%, by the end

of the first day > 40 mg%, and during the first week of life > 45 mg%.

Hypoglycemia in the neonatal period can lead to damage to the central nervous system!

Factors Increasing Risk of Hypoglycemia in Newborns:

Prematurity.

Intrauterine growth restriction.

Newborns with excessive body weight.

Maternal diabetes.

Neonatal asphyxia or trauma.

Cold exposure.

Infections, sepsis.

Congenital heart defects.

Serological conflict.

Clinical Symptoms

The symptoms of hypoglycemia in newborns are non-characteristic and may be subtle. Therefore, all infants at risk should have their blood glucose

levels checked depending on the results and condition of the child: every 6 hours during the first day, every 12 hours during the second day, and every

18 hours during the third day.

The symptoms of hypoglycemia in infants are also non-specific. They may include apathy, hypotonia, lethargy, tremors, jaundice, hypothermia, and

respiratory disturbances.

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for hypoglycemia risk factors: Prematurity, IUGR, Excessive weight, Maternal diabetes, Aspyxia, Cold, Infection, Heart defects,

Serological conflict (PIEMACHIS).

Key glucose thresholds:

<35 mg% (first 3 hours) → urgent intervention.

<40 mg% (day 1) → monitor closely.

<45 mg% (week 1) → normal range.

Clinical pearl: Non-specific symptoms (e.g., lethargy, tremors) necessitate routine glucose monitoring in high-risk infants to prevent CNS

damage.

page 276

OSTRE ZESPOŁY NEUROLOGICZNE | 39

Hypoglycemia in Neonatal Period

Hypoglycemia occurring during the neonatal period is part of Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome (hypoglycemia, large birth weight, umbilical hernia,

hepatosplenomegaly, adrenal cortex cell hypertrophy, increased susceptibility to embryonal tumors – e.g., neuroblastoma, Wilms tumor,

hepatoblastoma, rhabdomyosarcoma [enlarged tongue]), which should be excluded during physical examination.

Diagnosis

Glucose level

Calcium level

Sodium level

Potassium level

Insulin level

Blood gas analysis

Urine culture

Cortisol, growth hormone, prolactin, ketone bodies levels

Differential Diagnosis

Endocrine disorders (growth hormone deficiency, hypokortizolemia)

Glycogenolysis pathway disorders, glycogenesis process disorders, galactose to fructose conversion disorders

Hyperinsulinism (increased insulin level, increased C-peptide level)

Nesidioblastosis (hyperplasia of B cells outside the pancreatic islets)

Pancreatic juice

Treatment

Administer 10% glucose at a dose of 2 ml/kg (200 mg/kg).

The feed should be given every 2 hours or more frequently.

Treatment involves administering hydrocortisone (5 mg/kg/24 hours) or glucagon to neonates whose mothers have diabetes.

HYPOCALCEMIA

Neonatal hypocalcemia is diagnosed if the calcium level is below 8 mg% (2 mmol/l), and in premature infants, it is below 7 mg% (1.75

mmol/l).

🧠Memory Aid

Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome: Use the acronym H-L-U-H-A-E for key features:

Hypoglycemia

Large birth weight

Umbilical hernia

Hepatosplenomegaly

Adrenal cortex cell hypertrophy

Embryonal tumors (e.g., neuroblastoma, Wilms tumor)

Hypocalcemia thresholds:

Neonates: 8 mg% (2 mmol/l)

Premature infants: 7 mg% (1.75 mmol/l)

Treatment for neonatal hypoglycemia:

Glucose (10%): 2 ml/kg

Hydrocortisone: 5 mg/kg/24 hours (for infants of diabetic mothers)

Glucagon: Alternative to hydrocortisone

Clinical pearl: Nesidioblastosis is a rare cause of hyperinsulinism, characterized by B-cell hyperplasia outside pancreatic islets.

Early Hypocalcemia in Newborns

Premature infants

Newborns with perinatal asphyxia

Newborns of mothers with diabetes or hyperglycemia

Late Hypocalcemia in Newborns

Congenital hypoparathyroidism

Excessive intake of phosphorus (e.g., cow's milk)

Hypomagnesemia

Reduced intestinal calcium absorption

Clinical Symptoms

Symptoms are less characteristic the earlier the hypocalcemia occurs

May include:

Periodic apnea

Cyanosis

Tremors

Exaggerated muscle tone

Vomiting

Difficulty with feeding

Diagnosis

Measurement of ionized calcium in blood

Measurement of magnesium levels

Treatment

Symptomatic hypocalcemic tetany:

Administer 10% calcium gluconate solution at a dose of 1 mL/kg

Monitor cardiac rhythm; normalize before discontinuing medication

Hypocalcemia with hypomagnesemia (serum Mg < 0.4 mmol/L or 1 mg%):

IV bolus: 25% magnesium sulfate solution at 0.2 mL/kg

Oral maintenance: 2 mmol/kg/24 hours of magnesium preparations

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonics for Early vs. Late Hypocalcemia

- Early causes: PAD (Premature, Asphyxia, Diabetes)

- Late causes: HPHA (Hypoparathyroidism, Phosphorus, Hypomagnesemia, Absorption)

Treatment Comparison Table

| Scenario | Treatment | Dose/Method |

|-------------------------------|------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|

| Symptomatic tetany | Calcium gluconate | 1 mL/kg IV |

| Hypomagnesemia + hypocalcemia | Magnesium sulfate IV + oral Mg | 0.2 mL/kg IV bolus + 2 mmol/kg/day |

Clinical Pearl

- Always monitor cardiac rhythm during calcium administration to prevent arrhythmias.

- Cow’s milk is a common cause of late hypocalcemia due to high phosphorus content.

33 secHypernatremia

Definition

Hypernatremia is an increase in sodium levels above normal in blood plasma:

- Newborns: 133–148 mmol/l

Causes

- Hypertonic dehydration

- Excessive administration of physiological saline solution

- Simple nephrogenic diabetes insipidus

- Primary hyperaldosteronism

Clinical Symptoms

- Increased thirst

- Dry skin

- Irritability

- Restlessness

- Muscle twitching

- Seizures

- Increased risk of intracranial hemorrhage

Treatment

1. Rehydration with isotonic saline (0.45% NaCl)

2. Gradual restoration to normal state

3. Electrolyte control

4. Renal function and creatinine monitoring

1 minDRGAWKI

Etiology

- Neonatal asphyxia

- Intracranial hemorrhage

- Metabolic disorders

- Infection

- Fetal abstinence syndrome (e.g., mother is a drug addict)

Diagnosis

- History taking

- Physical examination

- Basic laboratory tests:

- Blood glucose

- Blood gas analysis

- Electrolytes

- Infection parameters

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Hypernatremia Mnemonics

TIRMS: Thirst, Irritability, Restlessness, Muscle twitching, Seizures (key symptoms)

SALT: Sodium, Albumin, Loss of water, Treatment with isotonic saline

🧠DRGAWKI Etiology Acronym

NIMIF:

- Neonatal asphyxia

- Intracranial hemorrhage

- Metabolic disorders

- Infection

- Fetal abstinence syndrome

🧠Comparison Table: Hypernatremia vs. DRGAWKI

Feature

Hypernatremia

DRGAWKI

Definition

Elevated sodium in blood plasma

Neurological disorder (unspecified)

Key Symptoms

Thirst, seizures, dry skin

Not explicitly listed (diagnosis via tests)

Causes

Dehydration, saline overload, etc.

Neonatal asphyxia, infection, etc.

Treatment

Isotonic saline rehydration

N/A (diagnosis-focused)

Clinical Pearl: Hypernatremia requires gradual correction to avoid cerebral edema. Always monitor electrolytes and renal function during

treatment.

11 secPyridoxine-Dependent Seizures

Clinical presentation: Manifest shortly after birth; resistant to conventional anticonvulsants.

Diagnosis:

Termination of seizures with intravenous vitamin B₆.

Complete resolution of EEG changes during B₆ administration.

35 secIntraventricular Hemorrhage (Volpe Classification)

1. Grade I: In situ, <10% of ventricular volume, subventricular

2. Grade II: Intraventricular hemorrhage filling 10–50% of ventricular space

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics

Pyridoxine-Dependent Seizures: "B6 Stops Seizures" (Vitamin B₆ is the key treatment).

Intracranial Bleeding Types: Subdural, Subchorionic, Cerebellar, Peri/intraventricular (SSCP).

🧠Clinical Pearl

Volpe Classification: Remember "10–50%" for Grade II intraventricular hemorrhage.

Risk Factors: Group by Pregnancy, Perinatal, and Postpartum for structured recall.

🧠Comparison Table

Risk Factor Category

Examples

Pregnancy

Prematurity, EPH gestosis

Perinatal

Hypoxemia, metabolic acidosis

Postpartum

Mechanical ventilation, hyperosmolar IV solutions

1 secAcute Neurological Syndromes

16 secIntraventricular Hemorrhage Grading

Grade III

Criteria: Intraventricular hemorrhage filling more than 50% of the lateral ventricle lumen

Characteristics: Usually massive, with ventricular dilatation

Grade IV

Criteria: Interstitial bleeding and all other changes in the parenchyma

Complications: Serious complication is purulent meningitis

Godula-Stuglik U. (ed.), Selected issues in neonatal pathology, SAM, Katowice 2003

Kubicka K., Kawalec W., Pediatrics. Textbook for students, PZWL Medical Publishing House, Warsaw 2004

Szczapa J., Basics of neonatology, PZWL Medical Publishing House, Warsaw 2008

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for Grades III and IV:

III: "Massive Ventricular Hemorrhage" (focus on ventricle lumen and dilatation)

IV: "Interstitial + Meningitis" (remember purulent meningitis as a key complication)

Comparison Table:

| Grade | Hemorrhage Location | Key Feature | Major Complication |

|-------|-----------------------------|------------------------------|----------------------------|

| III | Lateral ventricle lumen | >50% filling, ventricular dilatation | None explicitly stated |

| IV | Parenchyma (interstitial) | Purulent meningitis | Purulent meningitis |

Clinical Pearl: Grade IV hemorrhage is associated with parenchymal involvement and a higher risk of infection (e.g., purulent

meningitis).

5 minSELECTED ACUTE METABOLIC SYNDROMES

GALACTOSEMIA (galactosaemia)

This is an autosomal recessive genetic disorder caused by the inability to convert galactose into glucose.

Epidemiology

Frequency of clinically significant, classic galactosemia: 1:35,000.

Etiopathogenesis

Caused by mutations in the gene responsible for the correct function of galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase (GALT).

Clinical Symptoms

Newborns initially healthy after first milk feeding develop:

Rapidly increasing vomiting, diarrhea, jaundice, liver and spleen enlargement, coagulation disorders, acidosis, hypoglycemia, glucosuria.

Untreated disease leads to mental retardation, intellectual disability, and persistent liver insufficiency.

Diagnosis

Galactose levels elevated in milk and urine.

Lack of GALT activity in erythrocytes (test performed on dried blood; not part of routine screening).

Blood morphology (+ reticulocytes count).

Bilirubin elevation with separation into free (indirect) and conjugated (direct) fractions.

AspAT, AIAT levels.

Glucose and electrolyte levels.

Blood gas analysis.

Milk fat content.

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for GALT enzyme deficiency:

- Genetic disorder (autosomal recessive)

- Altered galactose metabolism (GALT enzyme defect)

- Liver and spleen enlargement (hepatosplenomegaly)

- Testing: erythrocyte GALT activity, galactose in urine

Clinical Pearls:

- Classic symptoms of galactosemia: vomiting, diarrhea, jaundice, and liver enlargement.

- Key diagnostic tests: GALT activity in erythrocytes, galactose in urine, and bilirubin fractionation.

- Untreated consequences: irreversible intellectual disability and liver failure.

Wybrane ostre zespoły metaboliczne | 45

Koagulologia

Ocena parametrów hemostazy.

Badanie genetyczne

Analiza mutacji.

Różnicowanie diagnostyczne

Galaktozemia

Fenylketonuria typu I

Niedowład fruktozy

Inne przyczyny wrodzonych uszkodzeń wątroby i nerek

Leczenie

Leczenie choroby trwa przez całe życie i zależy od wczesnego wykluczenia galaktozy z diety (dieta bez laktozy).

Galaktoza występuje w mleku krowim, mleku kozim i produktach mlecznych.

Musi być całkowicie zastąpiona substytutami laktozy, takimi jak:

hydrolyzaty orzechów migdałowych

preparaty na bazie soi

Fenylketonuria (PKU)

Charakterystyka

Zaburzenie genetyczne (dziedziczone w sposób autosomalny recesywny), charakteryzujące się brakiem zdolności do przekształcania fenylalaniny

w tyrozynę.

Epidemiologia

Częstość w Polsce: około 1:15 000.

Patogeneza

Dziedziczone monogenetycznie w sposób autosomalny recesywny.

Przyczyna: niedobór fenylalaninohydroksylazy (PAH), która bierze udział w metabolizmie fenylalaniny na tyrozynę.

Powoduje to gromadzenie się fenylalaniny i jej metabolitów.

Objawy kliniczne

Po urodzeniu i w pierwszych tygodniach życia noworodka stan jest dobry.

Pierwsze objawy pojawiają się stopniowo.

Opóźnienie rozwoju wynikające z umyślnego zaćmienia, a następnie znaczne zaburzenia ruchowe.

Memory Aid

PKU (Fenylketonuria):

Mnemonik: PAH (Fenylalaninohydroksylaza) → brak → PKU.

Dieta: Lactose-Free (bez laktozy) → życie całe.

Porównanie zaburzeń metabolicznych:

| Zaburzenie | Przyczyna | Kluczowy enzym/substrat |

|--------------------|----------------------------|--------------------------|

| Galaktozemia | Niedobór galaktozidazy | Galaktoza |

| PKU | Niedobór PAH | Fenylalanina |

| Niedowład fruktozy | Niedobór fruktozydazy | Fruktoza |

Kliniczny pearl: W PKU niedobór PAH prowadzi do akumulacji fenylalaniny – główny czynnik neurotoksykologiczny.

Clinical Presentation

Development of mental and motor skills shows tendencies toward:

Frequent vomiting and diarrhea

Skin changes (jaundice)

Characteristic "musty" odor

Tremors, hyperactivity, hypotonia, and lethargy

Diagnosis

Screening examination:

Performed on day 3 of life in newborns

Detects phenylketonuria (PKU) before neurological symptoms appear

Method:

Blood drawn from each newborn onto a Guthrie card

Differentiation

Mild hyperphenylalaninemia (BH4 deficiency):

Clinical symptoms similar to classic PKU

Does not respond to dietary treatment

Hyperphenylalaninemia, mild:

Slight increase in phenylalanine levels in blood

No clinical symptoms

May lead to maternal phenylketonuria

Hyperphenylalaninemia, transitional:

Periodic increases in phenylalanine levels in urine and feces of newborns

Likely caused by enzymatic maturation delay

Treatment

1. Early recognition and treatment prevent disease symptoms

2. Dietary intervention:

3. Introduce low-phenylalanine diet within 3 weeks after birth

4. Maintain diet throughout life

5. Limit phenylalanine content in foods (hydrolysates)

6. Phenylalanine levels:

7. Target range: 2–6 mg%

8. Minimum threshold: 2 mg% (excessive reduction may cause:

Growth retardation

Lethargy

Hypotonia

Seizures)

9. Pregnancy considerations:

10. Maintain phenylalanine levels within 2–6 mg% throughout pregnancy

11. Failure to do so may lead to fetal complications

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for PKU symptoms:

Musty odor

Tremors

Hypotonia

Yellow (jaundice) skin

Comparison Table: Hyperphenylalaninemias

Type

Cause

Response to Diet

Clinical Symptoms

Mild (BH4 deficiency)

Tetrahydrobiopterin

No

Yes

Mild

Slight increase

Yes

No

Transitional

Yes

No

Clinical Pearl:

- Guthrie card testing is the gold standard for newborn PKU screening.

- Phenylalanine levels must be strictly monitored to avoid both toxicity and deficiency.

7 secMaternal Phenylketonuria Complications

Fetal damage may occur.

Maternal phenylketonuria progresses with:

Delayed psycho-motor development

Congenital heart defects

Microcephaly

28 secLiterature

Kubicka K., Kawalec W., Pediatria. Podręcznik dla studentów. Wydawnictwo Lekarskie PZWL, Warszawa 2004.

Szczapa J., Podstawy neonatologii. Wydawnictwo Lekarskie PZWL, Warszawa 2008.

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for fetal complications of maternal phenylketonuria:

D-C-M

- Delayed psycho-motor development

- Congenital heart defects

- Microcephaly

Clinical Pearl:

Maternal phenylketonuria is a preventable cause of congenital anomalies. Early screening and strict dietary management (low-phenylalanine

diet) during pregnancy are critical to avoid fetal damage.

CARDIOPULMONARY DISORDERS

THE MOST COMMON CAUSES OF CARDIOPULMONARY DISORDERS

1. Respiratory Distress Syndrome (RDS)

Epidemiology

- Diagnosed in almost 70% of newborns born between 26 and 30 weeks of gestation.

- Diagnosed in only 5% of newborns born after 37 weeks of gestation.

Etiopathogenesis

- Caused by surfactant deficiency, morphological immaturity of the lungs, and delayed absorption of pulmonary fluid.

- Surfactant is produced by type II pneumocytes from the 22nd to 24th week of fetal life.

- Surfactant deficiency leads to:

- Collapse of pulmonary alveoli.

- Atelectasis.

- Hypoxia.

- Hypoxemia.

- Metabolic acidosis.

Clinical Symptoms

- Acceleration of breathing rate.

- Exhaust grunt.

- Intercostal contraction.

- Decreased vesicular sounds or bronchial sounds and crackles.

- Cyanosis.

Differentiation

- Temporary breathing disorders.

- Pneumonia.

- Heart defects.

- Congenital infections.

Memory Aid

- RDS Mnemonic: SMD (Surfactant Deficiency, Morphological Immaturity, Delayed Absorption).

- Clinical Symptoms Acronym: AGICC (Acceleration, Grunt, Contraction, Crackles, Cyanosis).

- Differentiation Checklist:

- Temporary breathing disorders.

- Pneumonia.

- Heart defects.

- Congenital infections.

Diagnostics

Chest X-ray:

Presence of a reticulogranular pattern of the lungs

Bronchogram

In more severe stages: generalized atelectasis, ground glass pattern

Lecithin to sphingomyelin ratio < 2

Phosphatidylglycerol concentration < 2 µg/ml

Arterial blood gasometry

Treatment

Respiratory support:

nCPAP

Mechanical or oscillatory ventilation

Administration of exogenous surfactant

Antibiotics:

In all newborns with symptoms of respiratory failure until pneumonia is excluded

Correction of hematological disorders:

Pharmacological closure of the ductus arteriosus with indomethacin or surgical ligation

Symptomatic treatment and monitoring of the radiological image of the lungs

Prevention:

- Prenatal administration of steroids to pregnant women

2. Transient Tachypnea of the Newborn (TTN)

Definition: Mild respiratory disorder in full-term or near-term newborns caused by delayed absorption of amniotic fluid from the lungs

Risk factors:

- Caesarean section (especially if performed before uterine contractions begin)

- Perinatal hypoxia

- Maternal diabetes

- Breech position

- Polycythemia in the newborn

🧠Memory Aid

Diagnostics Mnemonic:

CXR, L/S, PG, ABG (Chest X-ray, Lecithin/Sphingomyelin ratio, Phosphatidylglycerol, Arterial Blood Gasometry)

TTN Risk Factors Acronym:

CHAMP:

Caesarean section

Hypoxia

Antenatal Maternal Diabetes

Breech position

Polycythemia

Comparison Table (TTN vs. RDS):

Feature

TTN

RDS (Respiratory Distress Syndrome)

Cause

Delayed amniotic fluid absorption

Surfactant deficiency

Onset

Mild, early postnatal

Severe, immediate postnatal

X-ray Findings

Hyperlucency, perihilar streaks

Reticulogranular pattern, ground glass

Treatment

Oxygen, monitoring

Surfactant, mechanical ventilation

Clinical Pearl:

TTN typically resolves within 24–72 hours, while RDS requires prolonged respiratory support.

Circulatory and Respiratory Disorders | 51

Clinical Symptoms

Increased respiratory rate to about 80–120/min with slightly increased breathing effort.

Bowel-like chest (dilation of the peripheral parts of the lungs).

Increased heart rate above 160/min due to left ventricular overload.

Cyanosis.

Symptoms usually subside within 24–72 hours.

Differential Diagnosis

Respiratory disorders syndrome.

Pulmonary infection.

Heart defects.

Congenital infections.

Diagnostic Methods

Chest X-ray:

Enhanced pulmonary vascular pattern.

Dilation of the peripheral parts of the lungs (no bronchogram).

Enlargement of the heart silhouette.

Presence of fluid in the right pleural space and costophrenic angles.

Treatment

Continuous oxygen therapy.

Gastric tube feeding.

Symptomatic treatment and monitoring of vital functions.

3. Meconium Aspiration Syndrome (MAS – meconium aspiration syndrome)

Definition: Respiratory disorders associated with aspiration of amniotic fluid containing meconium.

Predominant population: Full-term or preterm newborns, and those with intrauterine developmental abnormalities.

Causes:

Release of meconium.

Deep breathing movements by the fetus.

Triggered by hypoxia and compression from the umbilical nerve.

🧠Memory Aid

Symptoms Mnemonic: B.R.I.C.

Bowel-like chest (lung dilation).

Respiratory rate >80–120/min.

Increased heart rate (>160/min).

Cyanosis.

MAS Causes: Hypoxia + Umbilical Compression (remember as H.U.C.).

Diagnostic X-ray Signs:

| Finding | Description |

|------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|

| Pulmonary vascular pattern | Enhanced (increased blood flow). |

| Lung dilation | Peripheral parts (no bronchogram). |

| Heart silhouette | Enlarged. |

| Pleural fluid | Right pleural space and costophrenic angles. |

Clinical Pearl: MAS symptoms typically resolve within 24–72 hours with supportive care.

Indications for the Risk of Developing MAS

Pathological KTG record (late deceleration)

Decrease in pH of fetal blood

Presence of meconium in amniotic fluid – greenish yellow urine

Clinical Signs

Green, thick amniotic fluid in newborn respiratory passages

Green discoloration of skin, nails, and mucous membranes

Generalized state of the newborn after birth:

Bradycardia

Reduced muscle tone

Bluish coloration

Absence of breathing activity

Breathing disorders:

Tachypnea

Retraction of chest wall

Intercostal retractions

Flaring nostrils

Signs of stridor (wheezing, rales over the lungs)

Low Apgar score

Diagnosis

Blood gas analysis:

Severe hypokalemia

Hypercapnia

Acidosis

Chest X-ray:

Irregular opacities in dense cases (snowstorm appearance)

Treatment

1. Clearing mucus from the nose and mouth immediately after birth of the baby's head (before performing the first breath)

2. Resuscitation if necessary:

3. Intubation

4. Removal of the airway

5. Bronchial lavage with 0.9% NaCl solution

6. Mechanical ventilation

7. Surfactant administration

8. General stabilization

9. Monitoring vital signs

10. Sedation, as needed

11. Treatment for respiratory insufficiency

🧠Memory Aid

MAS Mnemonic: Meconium Aspiration Syndrome – Remember the 3 key signs: Green amniotic fluid, Low Apgar score, and Snowstorm

X-ray.

Clinical Pearl: "Snowstorm appearance" on chest X-ray is a hallmark of severe MAS.

Treatment Steps: Use the acronym CLEAR:

Clear airway

Lavage with saline

Enter mechanical ventilation

Administer surfactant

Resuscitate as needed.

Acronym for Indications: PATH – Pathological KTG, Acidosis (low pH), Trace meconium, High risk (greenish amniotic fluid).

CARDIAC AND RESPIRATORY DISORDERS

General Management Principles

Adequate fluid and calorie intake

Physical therapy

Antibiotic therapy

Treatment of complications

Persistent Pulmonary Hypertension of the Newborn (PPHN)

Definition

A clinical syndrome manifesting early after birth, characterized by persistent right-to-left shunting through the patent ductus arteriosus and foramen

ovale, causing severe oxygenation disorders in the newborn.

Epidemiology

Occurs in 1:1000 to 1:1400 live-born infants

Etiopathogenesis

Most commonly associated with:

Perinatal hypoxia

Respiratory distress syndrome

Meconium aspiration

Systemic infection

Pathogenic factors disrupt normal relaxation of pulmonary vascular smooth muscle post-birth

Persistent high pulmonary vascular resistance → pulmonary hypertension → right ventricular strain → increased shunting through ductus

arteriosus and patent foramen ovale

Clinical Symptoms

Cyanosis unresponsive to oxygen therapy

Tachypnea (accelerated breathing)

Murmur over the tricuspid valve

Signs of congestive heart failure

Severe hypoxemia

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for PPHN causes:

Perinatal hypoxia

Respiratory distress syndrome

Meconium aspiration

Systemic infection

Key Clinical Pearl:

PPHN cyanosis does not improve with oxygen – distinguish from other causes of neonatal cyanosis (e.g., tetralogy of Fallot).

Comparison Table:

| Feature | PPHN | Tetralogy of Fallot |

|-------------------------|-------------------------------|-----------------------------|

| Cyanosis response to O₂ | No improvement | Improvement |

| Shunting | Right-to-left via PDA/foramen | Right-to-left via ventricular septal defect |

| Echocardiography finding | Elevated pulmonary vascular resistance | Ventricular septal defect |

Część 1

2 secPediatric Diagnostics and Treatment

20 secDiagnostics

Hyperoxia-hyperventilation test:

No improvement after oxygen therapy.

Improvement after hyperventilation.

Test for pre- and post-ductal blood oxygenation difference (initial shunt assessment):

Simultaneous measurement of PaO₂ in arterial blood or blood saturation from the right upper limb and left lower limb.

Chest X-ray.

Arterial blood gasometry.

UKG (ultrasonography of the kidneys).

4 secDifferentiation

Parenchymal-internal changes in the lung tissue.

Cyanotic congenital heart defect.

26 secTreatment

1. Causal treatment:

2. Examples: Sepsis, inflammatory changes in the lungs.

3. Mechanical ventilation:

4. Oscillating ventilation.

5. Surfactant supply.

6. Catecholamine administration:

7. Dopamine: 2–10 μg/kg/min.

8. Respiratory and metabolic alkalization:

9. pH > 7.45–7.55 (hyperventilation).

10. Additional therapies:

11. Nitric oxide, thiazoline, prostacyclin, magnesium sulfate.

12. Symptomatic treatment.

Note: Ductal-dependent heart defects are described in the chapter "Diseases requiring immediate surgical intervention."

Hyperoxia-Hyperventilation test → Help identify shunt vs. parenchymal disease.

🧠Key Dose Reminder

Dopamine: 2–10 μg/kg/min → Dose 2–10 for Dopamine.

1 secDevelopmental Abnormalities

57 secDevelopmental Abnormalities of the Nervous System

Key Features of Dysraphic Conditions

- Types:

- Open: Break in skin continuity.

- Closed: Intact skin.

- Affected Structures: Brain and/or spinal cord.

Clinical Presentation (Spinal Dysraphism)

- Physical Exam: Swelling localized to the lumbosacral region.

- Neurological Symptoms:

- Lower limb weakness or paralysis.

- Sensory disturbances.

- Bowel and bladder dysfunction.

Diagnostic Methods

1. Prenatal:

- Ultrasound (USG) of the fetus.

- Alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) levels in amniotic fluid.

2. Postnatal:

- X-ray: Detect vertebral anomalies.

- MRI: Optional.

- USG of the spine: Routine.

Differential Diagnosis

- Spinal dysraphism differs from:

- Bony protrusions (tori).

- Vascular malformations.

- Lipomas.

- Dermoids.

- Cysts.

Prophylactic Folic Acid Recommendations

- Women with prior spina bifida child:

- 4 mg/24 hours during preconception and subsequent pregnancies.

- All pregnant women:

- 0.4 mg/day as primary prevention.

38 secMyelomeningocele (Przepuklina Oponowo-Rdzeniowa)

Most common dysraphic condition.

Location: Lumbosacral region.

Contents: Meninges and fluid.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Folic Acid Dosing Mnemonic

4 mg for 4th pregnancy (if prior spina bifida).

0.4 mg for all other pregnancies.

🧠Comparison Table: Open vs. Closed Dysraphism

Feature

Open Dysraphism

Closed Dysraphism

Skin Integrity

Break in continuity

Intact skin

Visible Lesion

Yes (e.g., myelomeningocele)

No (e.g., lipoma)

🧠Clinical Pearl

Myelomeningocele is the most common dysraphic condition and typically presents in the lumbosacral region.

🧠Prophylaxis Reminder

4 mg folic acid is critical for women with a history of spina bifida to prevent recurrence.

1 secPEDIATRIA

28 secClinical Diagnosis

Key point: Clinical diagnosis is often so evident that diagnostic imaging can be limited to ultrasound (USG) in most cases.

Postoperative imaging: Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is useful for evaluating spinal canal anomalies and detecting other vertebral canal

malformations.

Head imaging: Always include head imaging (transverse USG, CT, or MRI) due to the frequent association of fatty arachnoid cysts with Arnold-

Chiari complex and severe cranial/spinal malformations.

14 secLipomeningomyelocele

Classification: A closed malformation.

Pathology: Contains fatty tissue that invades the arachnoid membrane into the soft meninges of the spine.

Preferred imaging: MRI, where fatty tissue shows a characteristic strong signal in T1-weighted images.

7 secSyringomyelia (Jamistość Rdzenia)

Definition: Presence of fluid spaces within the spinal cord.

Associated malformations:

Arachnoido-vertebral cysts

Arnold-Chiari complex

49 secArnold-Chiari Complex

Pathology: Displacement of posterior fossa structures (cerebellum and medulla) into the vertebral canal.

Associated conditions:

Mechanical hydrocephalus

Skull base malformations (most common: platyspondyly and basilar invagination)

Platyspondyly:

Definition: Flattening of the skull base, especially at the 枕bone.

Effect: Reduces posterior fossa volume.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Acronyms & Mnemonics

Arnold-Chiari Complex:

CIMIVC (Cerebellum and Medulla Invade Vertebral Canal).

Syringomyelia:

FSSC (Fluid Spaces in Spinal Cord).

Key Finding

Fluid spaces in spinal cord

🧠Clinical Pearl

Arnold-Chiari complex is frequently associated with fatty arachnoid cysts and severe cranial/spinal malformations. Always consider

head imaging in these cases.

2 secDevelopmental Anomalies | 57

32 secDepression of the Base of the Skull

Characterized by indentation around the large foramen (foramen magnum).

Diagnosis:

Lateral skull X-rays show:

Posterior arch of the atlas displaced above Chamberlain’s line and McGregor’s line.

Chamberlain’s line: Junction between the posterior border of the occipital bone and the superior border of the large foramen.

McGregor’s line: Junction between the posterior border of the occipital bone and the lowest point of the mastoid process.

MRI of cerebellar structures may confirm the diagnosis.

17 secDandy-Walker Syndrome (DWS)

Etiology: Developmental disorders of the fourth ventricle and excessive cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) production.

Diagnosis:

CT or MRI confirms:

Agenesis or hypoplasia of the cerebellum.

Enlargement of the fourth ventricle.

Torcular expansion of the posterior fossa.

Hydrocephalus.

Clinical Symptoms:

Headaches.

Vomiting.

17 secClefts of the Septum Pellucidum and Vermis

Common anomalies:

Cavum septi pellucidi (cystic septal cavity).

Cavum Vergae (vermal cleft).

Prevalence:

80% of premature infants.

20% of newborns.

Diagnosis:

First-trimester ultrasound.

MRI.

ITK (imaging technique, likely a typo for CT or IKT—keep as given).

14 secDevelopmental Lung Disorders

Pulmonary Hypoplasia

- Definition: Underdevelopment of the bronchial tree and pulmonary vessels.

- Prognosis: Usually fatal in early childhood.

- Clinical Presentation:

- (Details incomplete in original text—see original source for full context).

30 secMemory Aid

Dandy-Walker Syndrome (DWS)

Mnemonic: DWS = Dandy-Walker Syndrome.

Key Diagnostic Features:

| Feature | Description |

|--------|-------------|

| Cerebellum | Agenesis/hypoplasia |

| Fourth Ventricle | Enlarged |

Pulmonary Hypoplasia

Clinical Pearl: Always consider pulmonary hypoplasia in infants with respiratory failure and abnormal chest imaging.

12 secRespiratory Tract Infections Rebound

Complete hypoplasia:

X-ray (RTG): Massive lung opacity, mediastinal shift to the affected side, reduced chest cavity volume.

CT scan: Confirms bronchial tree obstruction or tracheal malformation.

31 secLung Sequestration

Definition: A lung segment separated from the bronchial tree and pulmonary arteries, forming an isolated, non-functional fragment.

Location:

External: Outside the lung cavity.

Internal: Within the lung cavity (e.g., rounded mass protruding from the lower part of the left lung).

Symptoms:

No connection to trachea: Asymptomatic until radiological examination.

Connection to bronchial tree: Infection, abscess, empyema.

Radiographic findings: Thickening and mottling in the affected area.

Diagnosis: Aortography shows occlusion of pulmonary arteries.

Treatment: Surgical intervention.

15 secUrinary Tract Developmental Defects

Associated conditions: Urinary tract infections, bladder dysfunction.

Common defects:

Ectopic ureter

Ureterocele (simple or ectopic)

Megaureter

Posterior bladder neck obstruction

Diagnostic methods:

Ultrasound (USG)

Cystography

Voiding cystourethrogram

Urography

Scintigraphy

Cystometry (for bladder dysfunction)

5 secEctopic Ureter

Definition: Abnormal positioning of the ureter opening outside the bladder trigone.

1 minUreterocele

Definition: Dilation of the ureter resembling a tumor.

Types:

Simple: Ureter opens normally into the bladder trigone.

Ectopic: Ureter opens outside the bladder trigone.

🧠Memory Aid

Respiratory Tract Infections Rebound

- X-ray signs: "Massive opacity, mediastinal shift, small chest cavity" → MMS (Massive, Mediastinal, Small).

- Lung Sequestration: "No connection = no symptoms; connection = infection, abscess, empyema" → NCE (No Connection = No Symptoms;

Connection = Emphysema/Infection).

Urinary Tract Defects

- EMMA acronym:

- Ectopic ureter

- Megaureter

- Ureterocele

- Anterior/posterior bladder neck obstruction

Ureterocele vs. Ectopic Ureter

| Feature | Ureterocele | Ectopic Ureter |

|-----------------------|--------------------------------------|------------------------------------|

| Location | Dilation of ureter | Abnormal ureter opening site |

| Trigone connection| Yes (simple) or no (ectopic) | No (always outside trigone) |

| Common symptoms | Infection, hydronephrosis | Urinary incontinence, infection |

Clinical Pearl: For lung sequestration, remember "Aortography confirms occlusion" → AOC (Aortography, Occlusion, Confirm).

2 secWady Rozwojowe | 59

6 secMegaureter

Definicja: Wysokiego stopnia rozszerzenie przewodu moczowego towarzyszonego hydronefrozą i pyelectazją o nieznanej przyczynie.

18 secPrzedniego przewodu moczowego (PUV)

Definicja: Występuje wyłącznie u chłopców. Powstaje w wyniku gruczołowania i zagięcia się błony śluzowej do światła przewodu moczowego

tylnego lub obecności przegrodek dzielących przewód moczowy.

Skutki:

Obstrukcja przepływu moczu.

Rozszerzenie ściany pęcherza moczowego.

Powstanie zwrotu moczu (refluks) z pęcherza do nerek.

2 minInne rzadziej spotykane wady rozwojowe układu moczowego

1. Agenesis nerek

Definicja: Wrodzone brak tkanek nerek. Brak również przewodu moczowego i pęcherza nerkowego.

2. Ektopia nerek

Definicja: Występuje w przypadkach, gdy rozwój nerek zostaje przerwany w życiu płodowym, co prowadzi do atrofii.

Charakterystyka:

Nerek nadmiernych nie produkuje moczu.

Ektopowe nery mogą znajdować się w jamie brzusznej, na kości miednicznej na poziomie dolnych odcinków kręgosłupa lędźwiowego, nawet w

jamie piersiowej.

W około 50% przypadków towarzyszy zwrot moczu (refluks) i hydronefroza.

3. Hipoplazja nerek

Definicja: Wrodzone niedorozwój nerek, charakteryzujący się małymi nerkami, które nadal produkują mocz.

Różnica od innych wad:

Odróżnia się od aplazji i dysplazji.

Hipoplazja i aplazja towarzyszą nadrozwojowi drugiej nerek.

4. Podwójny pęcherz nerkowy

Definicja: Górny przewód moczowy odprowadza górne kalice, a dolny przewód odprowadza środkowe i dolne kalice.

5. Podwójny przewód moczowy

Definicja: Odrębne przewody odprowadzają górny i dolny przewód moczowy.

Skutki:

Możliwość powstania zwrotu moczu (refluks).

Zwiększone ryzyko infekcji układu moczowego.

Hydronefroza.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

PUV: "Only in Boys" (tylko u chłopców).

Ektopia nerek: "Pelvis, Pelvic Bone, Thoracic Cavity" (miejsca występowania).

Hipoplazja vs. Aplazja:

Hipoplazja: Nierki małe, ale funkcjonalne.

Aplazja: Brak nerek.

Refeksy w wadach rozwojowych:

PUV → zwrot moczu + powiększenie pęcherza.

Ektopia + hydronefroza → 50% przypadków towarzyszy refluks.

Tabela porównawcza wad nerek:

| Wada | Cecha kluczowa | Skutki |

|------------------|----------------------------------------|--------------------------------|

| Agenesis | Brak nerek i przewodu moczowego | Brak funkcji nerek |

| Ektopia | Nierki w nieprawidłowych miejscach | Hydronefroza, refluks |

| Hipoplazja | Nierki małe, ale funkcjonalne | Nadrozwój drugiej nerek |

| Dupleks | Podwójne przewody moczowe | Refluks, infekcje moczu |

2 secInherently Common Congenital Defects

16 secCleft Palate (Schisis Palati, Cleft Palate)

Definition: A developmental defect of the palate.

Associated Condition: Often occurs with an upper lip cleft.

Surgical Treatment:

Lip surgery: Performed around 6 months of age.

Palate surgery: Performed around 18 months of age.

18 secHypospadias (Hypospadias)

Definition: Defect due to abnormal positioning of the urethral opening in males.

Common Presentation: Urethra localized on the ventral side of the penis.

Complications:

Difficulty with urination.

Sexual dysfunction.

Psychological problems.

Surgical Treatment: One- or multi-stage procedures performed around 6–24 months of age.

28 secPhimosis (Stulejka)

Definition: Narrowing of the urethral opening that prevents retraction of the foreskin.

Physiological Note: In boys aged 2–3 years, the foreskin does not retract due to adhesion to the glans.

Complications:

Difficulty with urination.

Sexual dysfunction.

Risk of glans adhesion from vigorous retraction.

Treatment:

Conservative: Hot compresses, steroid creams.

Surgical: Removal of part or all of the foreskin (e.g., circumcision) or urethral opening plastic surgery if conservative measures fail.

2 minEpispadias

Definition: Congenital defect of the penis caused by a dorsal cleft in the urethra.

Key Features:

Urethral opening on the dorsal surface of the glans.

Short, poorly developed penile erection.

Incontinence is a main symptom.

Treatment: Complex multi-stage surgical correction required.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Cleft Palate Surgery Timing:

Lip at 6 months, Palate at 18 months (6-18).

Hypospadias vs. Epispadias:

Hypospadias: Urethral opening on the ventral side.

Epispadias: Urethral opening on the dorsal side.

Phimosis Management:

Conservative first (steroids), surgery if failed.

Epispadias Red Flag: Incontinence is a defining symptom.

1 secPEDIATRIA

4 secChoroba hemolityczna noworodka (morbus haemolyticus neonatorum, erythroblastosis fetalis/hemolytic disease of

2 minthe newborn)

This is a severe and life-threatening condition caused by an immunological reaction between maternal IgG antibodies and fetal red blood cell antigens

in the ABO, Rh system, and less commonly Kidd, Kell, Duffy, and Lewis.

As a result of this disease, there is destruction of fetal and neonatal red blood cells leading to anemia, jaundice with severe consequences potentially

damaging the central nervous system.

Epidemiology

Hemolytic disease may appear in the first pregnancy without previous sensitization but has a milder course compared to Rh-incompatibility

hemolytic disease.

Etiopathogenesis

Maternal antibodies are formed due to early fetal red blood cell passage into maternal circulation during childbirth, spontaneous or induced

abortion, and as a result of group incompatible blood transfusion.

Only IgG antibodies play a pathogenic role in the serological conflict because they have the ability to cross the placental barrier.

Clinical Symptoms

Hemolytic anemia

Hyperbilirubinemia (increased unconjugated bilirubin due to red blood cell breakdown)

Hepatosplenomegaly

Severe jaundice (conjugated bilirubin level over 20 mg/dL or increasing conjugated bilirubin > 0.5 mg/kg mc./hour)

Fetal edema

🧠Memory Aid

Key Mnemonics:

- ABO, Rh, Kidd, Kell, Duffy, Lewis → Remember as "ARKKDL" (first letters of systems involved in hemolytic disease).

Comparison Table: ABO vs. Rh Incompatibility

Feature

ABO Incompatibility

Rh Incompatibility

Onset

Often first pregnancy

Usually second or later pregnancies

Severity

Mild

Severe

Antibody Type

IgG

IgG

Common Complication

Mild jaundice

Severe anemia, fetal edema

Clinical Pearl:

- IgG antibodies are the only pathogenic antibodies in hemolytic disease because they cross the placenta.

- Conjugated bilirubin > 20 mg/dL is a critical threshold for severe jaundice in neonates.

2 secHemolytic Disease of the Newborn

30 secDiagnostics

Blood count (hematocrit, hemoglobin, reticulocyte count).

In Rh incompatibility, erythroblasts are demonstrated.

In ABO incompatibility, detection of microspherocytes.

Blood type and Rh factor.

Bilirubin concentration with separation into free (indirect) and conjugated (direct).

Concentration of glucose, calcium, electrolytes, protein.

Direct Coombs test (antibodies detected on the surface of red blood cells).

Blood gas analysis with pH determination.

Urine culture (to exclude urinary tract infection).

Testing for galactosemia.

TSH, T4, fT4 levels (exclude hypothyroidism).

9 secDifferentiation

Infections.

Diabetes.

Jaundice associated with breastfeeding.

Enzymatic deficiencies of erythrocytes.

Defect of the erythrocyte cell membrane.

Hypothyroidism.

Polycythemia.

Intestinal insufficiency.

Hematomas.

Galactosemia.

1 minTreatment

Intrauterine fetal diagnosis with amniocentesis and cordocentesis in the case of an anti-D antibody titer higher than or equal to 16 (critical titer)

or a negative obstetric history.

Phototherapy (due to the phenomenon of photooxidation, it leads to a reduction in the concentration of bilirubin in the skin and subcutaneous

tissue into non-toxic and water-soluble substances).

Blood exchange transfusion (transfusion of double the amount of circulating blood with a volume of 80–100 ml/kg, which allows for the

exchange

ABO vs. Rh incompatibility:

ABO: Mild jaundice, microspherocytes, less severe.

Rh: Severe anemia, erythroblasts, higher risk of hydrops fetalis.

1 secPEDIATRIA

14 secBlood Transfusion Guidelines for Pregnant Women and Newborns

Blood selection: Must be performed by the Consultative Laboratory of RCKIK (Polish Blood Transfusion Service).

Requirements:

Leukocyte-poor (filtered)

Irradiated (dose: 25 Gy)

Fresh (up to five days old)

32 secIndications for Exchange Transfusion

Hemoglobin in cord blood < 8 g%

Bilirubin in cord blood > 6 mg%

Bilirubin level at 2 hours of life > 7.5 mg%

Increasing bilirubin level > 0.5 mg/kg/hour

Average bilirubin level > 20 mg% (non-hemolytic jaundice)

Average bilirubin level > 10–15 mg% (ChHN associated with fetal edema, placental insufficiency, infection)

Glucose infusion (precursor of glucuronic acid)

Phenobarbital dosage (luminal):

Dosage: 1–6 mg/kg/24 hours (single or divided doses)

Administration: Orally or intramuscularly

Side effects: Respiratory depression

9 secComplications

Jaundice of the liver nuclei (kernicterus)

Respiratory and circulatory insufficiency

Severe anemia at the end of the second week of life

Death

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Indications for exchange transfusion:

Hemoglobin <8 g%

Bilirubin >6 mg% (cord) or >7.5 mg% (2 hours)

Increasing bilirubin >0.5 mg/kg/hour

Average bilirubin >20 mg% (non-hemolytic) or >10–15 mg% (ChHN with fetal edema)

Phenobarbital:

Dosage: 1–6 mg/kg/24h

Administration: Oral or IM

Side effect: Respiratory depression

Kernicterus:

Key complication of severe jaundice

Effects: Brain damage, hearing loss, motor deficits

Remember: "Kernicterus = Kill Neurons In Cerebral Tissue Excess Release Under Stress"

Bilirubin thresholds:

| Condition | Bilirubin Threshold |

|---------------------------|---------------------|

| Non-hemolytic jaundice | >20 mg% |

| ChHN with fetal edema | >10–15 mg% |

2 secSero-logical Conflict (RhD-, ABO-alloimmunisation)

12 secOverview

Caused by IgG antibodies from the mother reacting with fetal red blood cell antigens in the ABO, Rh, Kidd, Kell, Duffy, and Lewis systems.

Contributes to neonatal hemolytic disease.

10 secEpidemiology

ABO system: Conflict may occur during the first pregnancy.

Rh system: Conflict typically occurs only during the second pregnancy (first pregnancy has an immunizing effect).

12 secEtiopathogenesis

Maternal antibodies form due to:

Fetal blood entering maternal circulation during:

Childbirth

Spontaneous or induced abortion

Non-group-compatible blood transfusion

Pathogenic factor: IgG antibodies (able to cross the placenta).

9 secClinical Symptoms

Due to fetal red blood cell destruction:

Hemolytic anemia

Jaundice (elevated unconjugated bilirubin – indirect, from red blood cell breakdown)

44 secDiagnosis

Blood morphology:

Hematocrit, hemoglobin, reticulocyte count

Rh incompatibility: Detection of erythroblasts

ABO incompatibility: Detection of microspherocytes

Blood group and Rh factor

Bilirubin levels:

Free (indirect) and bound (direct) bilirubin

Additional tests:

Glucose, calcium, electrolyte, protein levels

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Antigen Systems

ABO, Rh, Kidd, Kell, Duffy, Lewis → ARKKDL (pronounce as "ark-k-dl")

🧠Comparison Table: ABO vs. Rh Incompatibility

Feature

ABO Incompatibility

Rh Incompatibility

Timing

Can occur in first pregnancy

Typically occurs in second pregnancy

Diagnostic Finding

Microspherocytes

Erythroblasts

Antibody Type

IgG (same for both)

IgG (same for both)

🧠Clinical Pearl

IgG antibodies are the key pathogenic factor in serological conflict due to their ability to cross the placenta.

1 secPEDIATRICS

15 secDiagnostic Tests

Direct Coombs test (antibodies detected on the red blood cell surface)

Blood gas analysis with pH measurement

Urine culture (to rule out urinary tract infection)

Testing for galactosemia

TSH, T4, fT4 levels (to rule out hypothyroidism)

51 secProphylaxis and Management

Serological Incompatibility During Pregnancy

All women up to 12 weeks of pregnancy should have ABO blood type and alloantibodies tested.

All women around 28 weeks of pregnancy should have alloantibodies tested.

IgG Anti-D Administration (Rh-Negative Women)

After delivery:

Normal delivery: 150 µg

Abnormal delivery (cesarean section, manual placenta extraction, stillbirth): 300 µg

Twin delivery: 300 µg

After spontaneous miscarriage or termination of pregnancy, amniocentesis, removal of ectopic pregnancy:

Up to 12 weeks of pregnancy: 50 µg

After 12 weeks of pregnancy: 150 µg

Timing for IgG Anti-D

Administer within 72 hours after delivery or miscarriage.

Monitoring for Alloantibodies

Women with alloantibodies should have their levels identified and monitored systematically.

Anti-D alloantibody count should be less than 16.

Possible interventions: Intrauterine fetal diagnosis and treatment.

31 secDifferential Diagnoses

Infection

Erythrocyte enzymatic deficiencies

Erythrocyte cell membrane defect

Hypothyroidism

Polycythemia

11 secWrodzona Niedoczynność Tarczycy

(hypothyreosis congenita, hypothyroidism congenita, congenital hypothyroidism)

A disorder of physical and intellectual development caused by the deficiency of thyroid hormones during pregnancy and in newborns.

4 secEpidemiology

Wrodzona niedoczynność tarczycy występuje u noworodków z częstością 1:4000.

44 secEtiopathogenesis

Thyroid hormones are the most important factor controlling normal physical and mental development. From the third month of pregnancy, fetal

thyroid begins to produce its own hormones. In case of their deficiency, central nervous system development is delayed. Neonatal hypothyroidism is

characterized by:

- Elevated (or within normal limits) TSH level

- Reduced levels of fT3 and fT4

Possible causes of congenital hypothyroidism:

1. Anomalies in thyroid development (hypoplasia, aplasia, dysgenesis, ectopia)

2. Iodine deficiency (supplementation for pregnant women during pregnancy and breastfeeding)

3. Unbalanced maternal hypothyroidism (maternal treatment is necessary)

4. Autoimmune processes and inflammation

5. Defects in the synthesis of thyroid hormones

6. General resistance to thyroid hormones

57 secClinical Symptoms

Initially asymptomatic

Clinical symptoms appear only in the first weeks of life (transplacental passage of maternal thyroid hormones)

🧠Memory Aid

Key Points for Congenital Hypothyroidism:

- Mnemonic for causes: IDEAL

- Iodine deficiency

- Developmental anomalies (hypoplasia, ectopia)

- Enzymatic defects (synthesis issues)

- Autoimmune/inflammatory causes

- Low maternal thyroid function

Critical time for thyroid hormone impact: Third trimester of pregnancy

Early detection: Newborn screening (TSH/fT4 levels)

Management: Lifelong levothyroxine replacement (dose adjusted based on age and weight)

Comparison Table: Causes vs. Management

| Cause | Management |

|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|

| Iodine deficiency | Maternal iodine supplementation |

| Thyroid developmental anomalies | Levothyroxine replacement |

| Maternal hypothyroidism | Maternal thyroid hormone treatment |

| Autoimmune/inflammatory processes | Levothyroxine replacement |

| Enzymatic defects | Levothyroxine replacement |

| Resistance to thyroid hormones | Higher levothyroxine doses, regular monitoring |

2 secCongenital Hypothyroidism in Pediatrics

7 secEarly Recognition and Treatment

Widespread use of neonatal screening tests enables early detection and initiation of treatment.

16 secSymptoms of Hypothyroidism

Newborn symptoms:

Prolonged jaundice

Decreased muscle tone, lethargy, apathy

Bradycardia

Facial expression resembling a mask

Large tongue

Developmental symptoms:

Mental retardation

Hearing loss and speech disorders

Delayed skeletal development

Metabolic symptoms:

Poor appetite

Low weight gain

15 secDiagnosis

1. Neonatal screening test:

2. Measure TSH levels on the 3rd–5th day after birth

3. Laboratory tests:

4. Thyroid hormones: TSH, rT4, fT3

5. Imaging and serology:

6. Ultrasound of the thyroid gland

7. Anti-TPO antibodies

4 secDifferential Diagnosis

Congenital hypothyroidism

Excessive iodine intake

Retro-orbital or parapharyngeal mass

4 secTreatment

Supplementation with L-thyroxine

Long-term treatment, usually until adulthood

28 secReferences

Kubicka K., Kawalec W., Pediatria. Podręcznik dla studentów. Wydawnictwo Lekarskie PZWL, Warszawa 2004.

Szczapa J., Podstawy neonatologii. Wydawnictwo Lekarskie PZWL, Warszawa 2008.

🧠Memory Aid

Symptoms Mnemonic:

"MASK" for facial features (Mask-like expression, Apnea, Slowed reflexes, Kyphosis).

Diagnosis Acronym:

TSH, rT4, fT3 → Remember as "Three Ts" (TSH, Thyroxine, Triiodothyronine).

Differential Diagnosis:

Use CER to recall: Congenital, Excessive iodine, Retro-orbital/parapharyngeal mass.

Treatment Tip:

"L-thyroxine for life" → Emphasizes lifelong supplementation.

4 secROZWOJOWA DYSPLAZJA STAWÓW BIODROWYCH

(DDH – developmental dysplasia of the hip)

27 secDefinition and Overview

DDH refers to various abnormalities of the hip joint in infants and children.

Characterized by a tendency for spontaneous improvement or worsening over time.

Most common cause: inadequate positioning of the femoral head and improper modeling of the acetabular roof.

Untreated DDH leads to hip joint dislocation.

Modern terminology replaces "congenital dysplasia of the hip," emphasizing that hip joint development can occur even in infancy.

7 secEpidemiology

Division:

Typical DDH: 1 in 85 newborns.

Teratogenic DDH: 1 in 25,000 newborns.

Higher prevalence in females.

6 minEtiopathogenesis

Dysplasia is associated with:

- Anteversion and stiffness of the femoral head.

- Thinness and collapse of the acetabular roof.

- Stretching of the joint capsule.

Contributing Factors

a. Genetic factors:

- Increased risk if dysplasia occurs in parents or offspring.

b. Gynecological-pregnancy factors:

- Prematurity (especially in first-borns).

- Twin pregnancy.

- Fetal presentation.

- Manual assistance during childbirth.

c. Hormonal factors:

- Estrogens relax ligaments and joint capsules.

- Higher estrogen sensitivity in females explains increased prevalence in female infants.

d. Iatrogenic factors:

- Obstetric/neonatal procedures (e.g., manual assistance during delivery, birth asphyxia resuscitation).

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Etiopathogenesis Factors: GGH I

Genetic (family history).

Gynecological-pregnancy (prematurity, twin pregnancy).

Hormonal (estrogen effects).

Iatrogenic (obstetric procedures).

🧠Clinical Pearl

DDH is more common in females due to hormonal influences (estrogen) and anatomical differences in pelvic structure.

Early detection (via physical exam and imaging) is critical to prevent long-term joint damage.

Teratogenic DDH is rare but often linked to intrauterine constraints (e.g., oligohydramnios, fetal crowding).

Hip Dysplasia in Newborns: Clinical Signs and Diagnostic Tests

Teratogenic Factors

- Characterized by permanent changes in hip joints and poor prognosis.

- Often accompanied by other deformities.

Clinical Signs

1. Length of lower limbs

- Unilateral dislocation: Shortening of relative limb length (measured from mid-femoral to anterior superior iliac spine).

1. Interpubic gap width

2. Increased with unilateral subluxation.

3. Symmetry of inguinal folds

4. Deepening occurs on the affected side.

5. Symmetry of sacroiliac joint

6. Fold is higher on the dislocation side.

7. Symmetry of pubic symphysis

8. Fold is higher on the dislocation side.

9. Hip joint abduction

10. Physiological angle: 70–80°.

11. Pathological limitation after dislocation (critical in neonatal period).

Additional Signs (Assessed After 3 Months)

7. Ortolani Sign (Positive)

- Method: Infant supine, lower limbs flexed to 90°. Gently abduct and internally rotate thigh while performing slight abduction in hip joints.

- Finding: Snapping of femoral head into acetabulum on dislocation side.

1. Barlow Sign (Positive)

2. Method: Same position as Ortolani.

3. Finding: Easy dislocation of femoral head from acetabulum in dysplastic hip.

Memory Aid

- Clinical Signs Mnemonic: LIMB

- Length discrepancy

- Interpubic gap widening

- Muscle fold asymmetry (inguinal/sacroiliac)

- Bilateral abduction angle (70–80°)

Ortolani vs. Barlow

| Sign | Purpose | Finding |

|----------------|--------------------------|--------------------------------------|

| Ortolani | Detects subluxation | Snapping of femoral head into acetabulum |

| Barlow | Detects instability | Easy dislocation of femoral head |

Key Tip: Both signs require the infant to be supine with hips flexed to 90°. Ortolani confirms reduction, Barlow confirms instability.

RODENTIAL DYSPLASIA OF HIP JOINTS | 73

Clinical Presentation of Unstable Hip Joints

Feeling of snapping of the femoral head with a shallow socket.

Symptoms of Hip Joint Dislocation During Immobility

Visible shortening of the leg

Asymmetry of the posterior folds

Limitation of abduction and external rotation

In standing position:

Increased lumbar lordosis

Scoliosis

Gait abnormalities:

Limp in unilateral dislocation

Corkscrew gait in bilateral dislocation

Positive signs:

Pumping sign: Sliding of the thigh along the midline during pressure

Allis/Galeazzi sign: Affected side is below the healthy knee when the child is supine with hips and knees flexed

Trendelenburg sign: When the child stands on one leg (other leg bent at knee and pelvis), a drop of the pelvis indicates dysfunction of the middle

gluteal muscle (consequence of hip dislocation)

Diagnosis

Early detection: Multiple delicate examinations during pregnancy and up to 1 year after birth. First examination should occur at the beginning of

childhood.

Diagnostic methods:

Ultrasonography (USG): Best method in the first months of life

Radiography (RTG): Used later if USG is unavailable or inconclusive

MRI or CT: Reserved for specific cases

Targeted USG timing:

Perform between 3rd and 4th week of life in children with risk factors or unclear examination results

If USG unavailable, perform RTG at 4 months

Graf Method for USG:

Child placed on their side with hips abducted

Special stand stabilizes the trunk, pelvis, and one lower limb

Memory Aid

Mnemonic for signs of hip dislocation: P-A-T (Pumping, Allis, Trendelenburg)

Diagnostic timeline:

| Age | Preferred Method |

|------------------|----------------------|

| 0–6 months | USG (Graf method) |

| >6 months | RTG |

| Specific cases | MRI/CT |

Trendelenburg sign: "Pelvis drops when standing on one leg" → indicates gluteus medius weakness.

Galeazzi sign: "Affected leg is shorter when supine" → key for DDH diagnosis.

CONGENITAL AND ACQUIRED INFECTIONS

Neonatal infections are divided into:

Congenital infections (early)

Acquired (secondary) infections

Early infections are caused by intrauterine infection. Symptoms of the disease develop within 72 hours of birth. Secondary infections result from

infection acquired after birth.

1. CONGENITAL INFECTIONS

1.1 Toxoplasmosis (toxoplasmosis)

Definition: A parasitic zoonotic disease caused by infection with the protozoan Toxoplasma gondii.

Classification: Belongs to the group of diseases known as TORCH.

Etiopathogenesis

Definitive host: Cats

Intermediate host: Humans

Routes of infection:

Congenital toxoplasmosis: Primary infection of a pregnant woman or reactivation of infection; passage of the parasite through the placenta.

Acquired toxoplasmosis:

Consuming undercooked meat or contaminated fruit with cysts containing T. gondii bradyzoites.

Transmission via blood transfusions or organ transplantation from an infected donor.

Clinical Symptoms

Characteristic feature: The Sabin-Pinkerton triad, consisting of:

Microcephaly or hydrocephalus

Retinitis and chorioretinitis

Intraparenchymal calcification

Additional manifestations:

Cataracts

Microphthalmia

Hypotrophy

Hepatoplenomegaly

Thrombocytopenia

Interstitial pneumonia

Myocarditis

Delayed psychomotor development

page 326

🧠Memory Aid

TORCH acronym:

Toxoplasmosis

Other (e.g., syphilis, varicella)

Rubella

Cytomegalovirus

Herpes simplex

Sabin-Pinkerton triad mnemonic:

Microcephaly/ Hydrocephalus

Retinitis/ Chorioretinitis

Intraparenchymal Calcification

Comparison Table: Congenital vs. Acquired Toxoplasmosis

| Feature | Congenital | Acquired |

|---------------------------|----------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|

| Transmission | Placental passage | Undercooked meat, blood transfusion, etc. |

| Onset of symptoms | Within 72 hours of birth | After birth |

| Common symptoms | Triad (microcephaly, retinitis, calcification) | Systemic (fever, lymphadenopathy) |

2 secHip Ultrasound Evaluation and Graff Classification

38 secUltrasound Image Analysis: Lines and Angles

Base line:

Starts at the acetabular bony transition on the ilium.

Runs perpendicular to the external femoral head echo.

Bone roof line:

Connects the external femoral head edge with the lower edge of the ilium.

Scapular roof line:

Connects the middle of the articular surface with the external femoral head edge.

Used to evaluate scapular protrusion.

Angle alpha:

Formed between the base line and the bone roof line.

Evaluates scapular protrusion.

Angle beta:

Formed between the base line and the scapular roof line.

Evaluates acetabular coverage of the femoral head.

2 minGraff Classification of Hip Development

Type Ia and Ib - Normal Hip Joints

Angle alpha: ≥60°

Angle beta: >55°

Type II - Physiologically Immature Hips

Features: Dysplasia features without femoral head centralization.

IIa - Children ≤3 months old

Bony roof: Not fully developed.

Angle alpha: 55–59°

Angle beta: >55°

IIb - Children >3 months old

Angle alpha: 55–59°

Angle beta: >55°

IIc - Significant Dysplasia at Centralization Border

Angle alpha: 43–49°

Angle beta: >55°

Type III - Dysplastic Hip Joints

Centralization of the femoral head: Present.

IIIa - Hypoechogenicity of the roof

Bony trabeculae: Normal.

IIIb - Hyperechogenicity of the roof

Bony trabeculae: Transform into fibrous tissue.

Angle alpha: <43°

Angle beta: >77°

Type IV - Dislocated Hip Joints

Femoral head displacement: Present.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Graff Classification Types

Ia/Ib: "Ideal" hips (normal).

IIa: "Infant" (≤3 months).

IIb: "Beyond" 3 months.

IIc: "Critical" dysplasia.

IIIa/IIIb: "Dysplastic" (abnormal echogenicity).

IV: "Violation" (dislocation).

🧠Clinical Pearl

Angle alpha evaluates scapular protrusion (though likely a mistranslation; should be acetabular coverage).

Angle beta evaluates acetabular coverage of the femoral head.

Type IIIb is the most severe dysplasia (alpha <43°, beta >77°).

2 secDifferentiation Between Pathological and Post-Inflammatory Dislocations

1 secTeratogenic Dislocations

4 secDislocations in the Formation of Myelodysplasia, Arthrogryposis, and Congenital Systemic

1 secDiseases

17 secKey Diagnostic Criteria

Pathological dislocations: Structural abnormalities, congenital conditions (e.g., myelodysplasia, arthrogryposis).

Post-inflammatory dislocations: Result from joint inflammation or trauma.

Teratogenic dislocations: Caused by maternal exposure to teratogens (e.g., alcohol, drugs).

Congenital systemic diseases: Associated with systemic disorders (e.g., Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, Larsen syndrome).

36 secTreatment Approach

1. Immediate Postnatal Management

Goal: Prolong the transition period of the joint from fetal to extended position.

Joint positioning: Place the joint in a fetal-like position to ensure congruence.

Method: Extensive swaddling immediately after birth.

2. Infants ≤ 6 Months of Age

First-line treatment: Pavlik harness (lowest risk of femoral head necrosis).

3. Infants > 6 Months or Pavlik Harness Failure

Recommended: Closed reduction with adductor tenotomy, followed by immobilization in a cast.

4. Children > 18 Months or Failed Closed Reduction

Referral: Surgical treatment (e.g., open reduction, osteotomy).
