Obuchowicz A. (ed.), Personal history and physical examination in pediatrics. SAM, Katowice 2001.

Szczapa J., Basics of neonatology. PZWL Medical Publishing House, Warsaw 2008.

37 secMemory Aid

Mnemonic for Treatment Timelines

Pavlik harness → 6 months of age

Closed reduction → 6–18 months of age

Surgery → >18 months of age

Clinical Pearl

Pavlik harness is contraindicated in teratogenic dislocations due to risk of femoral head displacement.

Swaddling mimics the fetal position to stabilize the hip joint.

3 secAlcohol-Related Fetal Syndrome (FAS – Fetal Alcohol Syndrome)

6 secEpidemiology

FAS is a common and well-established cause of low birth weight and intellectual delay.

14 secEtiopathogenesis

Alcohol crosses the placenta, damaging the fetal brain.

This leads to:

Learning difficulties

Behavioral disorders

ADHD

Key recommendation: The harmful dose of alcohol is unknown. Women should completely abstain from alcohol during pregnancy.

22 secCriteria Confirming Diagnosis of FAS

1. Maternal alcohol consumption during pregnancy (confirmed via interview).

2. Physical anomaly syndrome:

3. Minor or severe deformations of limbs, face, muscles, or internal organs.

4. Growth parameters:

5. Low birth weight

6. Low height

7. Small head circumference

8. Cognitive dysfunction:

9. Decline in physical, intellectual, and social functions.

36 secCharacteristic Dysmorphic Features

Short philtrum

Small ears

Hypertelorism (widely spaced eyes)

Flat central part of the nose

Smooth nasal bridge

Thin upper lip

Small chin

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for Dysmorphic Features:

Short philtrum

Small ears

Hypertelorism

Flat nose

Smooth bridge

Thin lip

Small chin

Acronym: S.S.H.F.S.T.S. (though not a perfect mnemonic, it helps recall the key features).

Clinical Pearl:

- FAS diagnosis requires all four criteria (maternal alcohol use, physical anomalies, growth deficits, and cognitive dysfunction).

- Prevention: Absolute alcohol abstinence during pregnancy is the only way to avoid FAS.

1 secAlcohol Fetal Syndrome

22 secKey Features

Many children with this syndrome are born with delirium tremens symptoms.

Developmental defects:

Heart defects

Malformations of other organs (e.g., joints)

Central nervous system impairments:

Cognitive and physical impairments

Hyperactivity

Difficulty with attention

Muscle tone may be reduced or increased

Older children:

Lower IQ

Attention deficit

Poor problem-solving skills

Learning difficulties

Impulsivity

8 secDiagnosis

Toxicology: Ethanol concentration in blood

Electrolytes: Sodium, potassium, calcium, magnesium, phosphorus

Imaging: USG of the fetus

Neurological exam: EEG examination

10 secDifferential Diagnosis

1. Down syndrome

2. Other genetic diseases

3. Brain damage not related to excessive alcohol consumption during pregnancy

4. Early signs of childhood onset

11 secTreatment

Immediate interventions:

Correcting electrolyte imbalances

Appropriate hydration

Anti-spasmodic drugs

Long-term interventions:

Use of child care and education centers

Training, workshops, and therapeutic groups for:

Parents

Children

Educators

Pharmacology – Basic Drugs Used in Neonatal Resusitation

1. Adrenaline

Mechanism of action: Catecholamine that acts on β1 and β2 receptors; higher doses also activate alpha receptors.

Concentration for administration: 1:10,000.

Dose: 0.1–0.3 ml/kg body weight.

Route: IV or intratracheal (et).

Intratracheal administration: Requires several times higher doses. Dilute with saline to a volume of 1–2 ml.

Side effects: Excessively increased heart rate, decreased coronary perfusion, vomiting.

2. Sodium Bicarbonate (8.4% NaHCO₃)

Indications: Acidosis.

Mechanism of action: Alkalization by buffering H⁺.

Concentration for administration: 1:1.

Administration: Slow IV infusion.

Use restrictions: Only in properly ventilated neonates. Only if metabolic acidosis is suspected.

Dose: Calculated from formula: 0.3 × weight × base deficiency. In resuscitation: 2–4 ml/kg body weight.

Side effects: Disturbances of acid-base balance, increased blood osmolarity, extravasation causes tissue necrosis.

3. Naloxone Hydrochloride (Narcan)

Mechanism of action: Antagonist with no agonist activity for natural and synthetic analgesics.

Dose: 0.1–0.25 ml/kg body weight.

Route: IV and intratracheal.

Side effects: Possible seizures.

🧠Memory Aid

Adrenaline:

ABC: Acts on Alpha, Beta, and Cardiac receptors.

Dilute: Always dilute to 1–2 ml for intratracheal use.

Sodium Bicarbonate:

BASIC: Used for Bicarbonate in Acidosis, Slow infusion, In ventilated neonates, Calculation-based dosing.

Avoid: Never use without proper ventilation.

Naloxone:

NARCAN: Remember the Name and Route (IV/ET).

Seizures: Risk of Seizures with overdose.

11 secManagement of Hypoglycemia

Do not give 10% glucose in doses of 2 ml/kg (200 mg/kg).

Administer infusion at a rate of 8–15 mg/kg/min under control of blood sugar.

14 secManagement of Hypocalcemia and Hypomagnesemia

25% magnesium sulfate solution (magnesium sulfate) for intravenous administration in doses of 0.2 ml/kg.

Do not give 10% calcium gluconate solution in a dose of 1 ml/kg over 10 minutes.

11 secManagement of Cardiac Conduction Disorders

Administer prostaglandin E1 (prostigmin) i.v. as continuous infusion at rates of 0.01–0.02 µg/kg/min.

Side effects:

Apnea

Hyperthermia

Facial flushing

Tachycardia

Tremors

Hypotension

58 secEmpirical Antibiotic Therapy in Meningitis

Ampicillin:

200 mg/kg/24 hours for premature infants and newborns

300 mg/kg/24 hours for preterm infants

Cefotaxime:

100 mg/kg/24 hours for premature infants

150–200 mg/kg/24 hours for newborns and preterm infants

🧠Memory Aid

Hypoglycemia:

"2 ml/kg, then 8–15 mg/kg/min" – use this sequence to avoid rapid glucose overload.

Calcium gluconate:

"Avoid 1 ml/kg over 10 minutes" – rapid infusion risks cardiac arrhythmias.

Magnesium sulfate:

"0.2 ml/kg IV" – key dose for hypomagnesemia.

Prostaglandin E1:

"0.01–0.02 µg/kg/min" – low infusion rates to prevent side effects.

Meningitis antibiotics:

Ampicillin vs. Cefotaxime:

| Group | Ampicillin | Cefotaxime |

|------------------|---------------------|------------------------|

| Premature infants | 200 mg/kg/24h | 100 mg/kg/24h |

| Newborns | 200 mg/kg/24h | 150–200 mg/kg/24h |

| Preterm infants | 300 mg/kg/24h | 150–200 mg/kg/24h |

Clinical Pearl:

Calcium gluconate is contraindicated in hyperkalemia due to risk of cardiac toxicity.

Część 2

5 minSELECTED INFECTIOUS DISEASES OF CHILDHOOD

The priority in treating infectious diseases is an appropriate selection of drugs (symptomatic medications, antiviral agents, antifungal agents,

chemotherapy, antibiotics) in relation to the observed clinical picture of the disease. An adequate antibiotic choice must always be made based on

current medical evidence (especially in the context of clinical trials based on EBM principles), taking into account local epidemiology and changing

susceptibility/resistance patterns.

MEASLES (MORBILLI)

Etiopathogenesis

Infection by measles virus (DNA - Paramyxoviridae).

Transmission via droplets, the source is an infected person.

Incubation period – 4 days before and 3 days after the rash appears.

Rash resolution – 9–11 days.

Clinical Symptoms

Pre-rash phase:

- Fever up to 39°C, dry cough, runny nose, sore throat, conjunctivitis, redness of the eyelids, photophobia;

- Koplik's spots appear in the 2nd or 3rd day of the pre-rash phase – white spots with a red border localized on the buccal mucosa of the mouth at the

height of the lower incisors and premolars, preceding the rash by 1–2 days.

Rash phase:

- Lasts 3–4 days; fever up to 40°C, worsening general condition;

- Small, erythematous spots that later become papules localized mainly behind the ears along the hairline, quickly spread over the face and trunk,

showing a tendency to merge.

🧠Memory Aid

Koplik's Spots Mnemonic: Before Rash, Buccal Mucosa, Red Border, White Spots (BRRB).

Incubation Period: 4 days before rash + 3 days after rash = 7-day window for diagnosis.

Rash Progression: Behind ears → face → trunk (useful for localization in exams).

Measles Virus Note: Despite being classified as DNA in this text, measles virus is actually an RNA virus (Paramyxoviridae family) – a key

correction for clinical accuracy.

Recovery Period

Duration of recovery – lasts several days; subsiding rash and physical symptoms of viral infection, normalization of body temperature.

Complications

Incidence: Affects 15% of patients; risk doubles or triples in those over the age of 10.

Most common complications:

Lung inflammation

Pleurisy

Severe laryngitis

Middle ear inflammation

Rarely: Guillain-Barré syndrome, partial paralysis, retinal inflammation

In Third World countries: orchitis (due to vitamin A deficiency), meningitis, myocarditis, secondary bacterial infections

Severe Form of the Disease

Severe form of the disease.

Meningoencephalitis and myelitis (1:1000 cases) – most severe complication; mortality rate 10–30%.

Subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE) – 4–10 years after measles infection (1:1000 cases).

Laboratory Diagnosis

Findings: Leukopenia, no characteristic deviations in test results.

Treatment

Approach: Symptomatic treatment.

Prevention

Vaccination: According to the vaccination schedule (Priorix, MMR).

Passive immunity:

Gamma globulin 0.25 ml/kg mc. (max. 15 ml) for up to 5 days after contact in:

Infants

Children with malnutrition

Children undergoing immunosuppressive treatment

Pregnant women

Infants <6 months of age: Gamma globulin not recommended after exposure if the mother was vaccinated and had measles.

HIV-infected children or AIDS patients: Give 0.5 ml/kg mc. post-exposure, even if vaccinated.

🧠Memory Aid

Complication Mnemonic: LPELGR (Lung, Pleurisy, Laryngitis, Ear, Guillain-Barré, Retinal) – common complications.

SSPE Timing: Remember "4–10 years" after measles infection.

Gamma Globulin Use:

High-risk groups: Infants, malnourished children, immunosuppressed patients, pregnant women.

Exclusion: Infants <6 months if mother had measles.

Vaccine Names: Priorix vs. MMR – both used for prevention.

Severe Complication: Meningoencephalitis (1:1000 cases) has 10–30% mortality – critical to note.

12 Pediatrics

Diagnostics

Serological tests:

Determination of anti-toxoplasmosis antibody titer in the IgM and IgG class by ELISA, antibody avidity test.

Treatment

Sulfadiazine, pyrimethamine + folic acids are used in newborns and spiramycin in pregnant women – the dosage is determined individually

depending on the clinical symptoms.

1.2 Rubella (German measles)

A disease caused by a single-stranded RNA virus.

Infection of the fetus occurs transplacentally (bloodborne).

Infection in the first weeks of pregnancy can lead to fetal death and miscarriage.

The risk of infection and fetal defects is greatest if the pregnant woman becomes ill during the first trimester.

Infection after the 22nd week of pregnancy is not dangerous to the fetus.

Clinical symptoms

Often in the form of Gregg syndrome.

The syndrome includes:

Cataracts

Glaucoma

Microphthalmia

Deafness

Heart defects

Diagnostics

Serological tests – determination of IgM antibody titer in serum.

A four-fold increase in IgG antibody titer within a 2-week interval, stable IgG concentration indicates a past infection and immunity.

Isolation of the virus (culture) or its RNA from urine, blood or cerebrospinal fluid.

Treatment

Symptomatic only.

🧠Memory Aid

Gregg syndrome mnemonic: "CGMHD" (Cataracts, Glaucoma, Microphthalmia, Hearing loss, Heart defects).

Rubella vs. Toxoplasmosis diagnostics:

| Test | Rubella | Toxoplasmosis |

|------------------------|----------------------------|----------------------------|

| Serological tests | IgM titer, IgG increase | IgM/IgG ELISA, avidity test|

| Virus isolation | Urine, blood, CSF | Urine, blood, CSF |

Key treatment drugs:

Pregnant women: Spiramycin

Newborns: Sulfadiazine + pyrimethamine + folic acids

Rubella risk timeline: First trimester → high risk; >22 weeks → low risk.

4 minRÓŻYCZKA (rubella, german measles, three-day measles)

Etiology and Pathogenesis

Cause: Infection with rubella virus (RNA - Togaviridae).

Transmission:

Droplet or direct contact with contaminated material.

Transmission through the placenta (source of infection: infected person).

Incubation period: 7 days before rash onset, 5 days after rash appears.

Contagiousness duration: 14–21 days.

Pregnancy risk: A pregnant woman with rubella can be contagious up to 18 months.

Clinical Symptoms

Prodromal phase:

Headache

Fever up to 38°C

Loss of appetite

Lymphadenopathy:

Mild, painful swelling of posterior cervical lymph nodes.

Appears 24 hours before rash onset.

Lasts about 1 week.

Swollen lymph nodes may be the only symptom of illness.

Rubella rash:

Pinkish, macular or maculopapular rash.

Appears on the face (involves skin between cheek folds – triangular Filatov sign).

Spreads to trunk and extremities.

Does not involve palms and soles.

Forschheimer spots:

Enanthema on the palate.

Not a diagnostic criterion for rubella.

Note: Clinical symptoms are not definitive for diagnosing rubella.

Complications

Common complications:

Joint swelling

Thrombocytopenic purpura

Encephalitis

Congenital rubella syndrome (in pregnant women):

Highest risk: First trimester.

Triad of Gregg:

1. Eye defects: Microphthalmia, glaucoma, cataract.

2. Ear defects: Microcephalia.

3. Heart defects: Persistent ductus arteriosus, pulmonary artery stenosis.

🧠Memory Aid

Gregg Triad Mnemonic: E.E.H. (Eye, Ear, Heart) – remember the three key congenital defects.

Filatov Sign: Visualize the triangle between cheek folds on the face – a hallmark of rubella rash.

Contagiousness Duration: 14–21 days (think "14 to 21" like a calendar).

Forschheimer Spots: Not diagnostic – remember this to avoid misdiagnosis.

Incubation Period: 7 before, 5 after – "7-5" as a simple ratio.

Laboratory Diagnosis

Confirmation of IgM antibodies appearing during the 2nd week of rash with increasing exudate over 2–3 weeks.

In congenital varicella, IgM may persist for 3–4 years.

IgG antibodies indicate naturally acquired or vaccine-induced immunity.

The diagnosis is confirmed by isolation of virus from blood, urine, or cerebrospinal fluid (PCR or in situ hybridization).

Treatment

Symptomatic:

Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs for joint inflammation.

Glucocorticoids and potentially plasma concentrate in hypoproteinemia.

Prevention

Vaccination according to the vaccination schedule (Priorix, MMR).

Post-exposure prophylaxis in pregnant women who have not been vaccinated – mandatory IgG seronegative women in the 1st trimester of

pregnancy are advised to receive immunoglobulin 0.55 mL/kg (lack of data on the effectiveness of this approach).

Chickenpox (varicella) and Shingles (herpes zoster)

Etiopathogenesis

Etiological factor: varicella-zoster virus (VZV);

Primary infection – chickenpox, followed by activation of latent infection in sensory ganglia (mainly trigeminal and dorsal root ganglia) –

shingles.

Transmission: droplet, airborne, contact; reservoir is only the person with chickenpox or shingles (rarely herpes zoster).

Infectious period (chickenpox and shingles): 2 days before rash to the appearance of crusts.

Incubation period: 10–21 days; in immunosuppressed patients, it can last up to 28–35 days.

🧠Memory Aid

IgM timeline:

2nd week of rash → IgM appears; 2–3 weeks → exudate increases.

Congenital varicella: IgM persists 3–4 years.

IgG: Immunity (natural or vaccine-induced).

VZV transmission: Droplet, airborne, contact; reservoir = infected person.

Infectious period: 2 days before rash to crusts form.

Incubation period:

10–21 days (normal), 28–35 days (immunosuppressed).

Prevention:

Vaccinate with Priorix/MMR.

Pregnant women (IgG seronegative, 1st trimester): 0.55 mL/kg immunoglobulin.

Chickenpox vs. Shingles:

| Chickenpox | Shingles |

|----------------|--------------|

| Primary VZV infection | Reactivation of latent VZV |

| Children | Adults (often immunocompromised) |

| Systemic rash | Unilateral, dermatomal rash |

Wybrane Choroby Infekcyjne Wieku Dziecięcego | 87

37 secClinical Symptoms of Chickenpox

Prodromal symptoms (1–2 days before rash):

- Fever

- Malaise

- Headache

- Muscle aches

- Gastrointestinal symptoms

Rash characteristics:

- Varies in intensity and duration, covers entire body or mucous membranes

- Plaques rapidly progress to vesicles in the groin area

- At peak: Vesicles contain clear fluid, crust over, and dry (crusts form)

- After drying: Shallow scars remain

- Key feature: Presence of vesicles in different stages simultaneously

Rare atypical rash forms:

- Oozing

- Pustular

- Scaly

- Hemorrhagic

Diagnosis: Based on clinical appearance

32 secClinical Symptoms of Herpes Zoster

Primary symptoms:

- Tingling, pain, burning sensation

- Vesicular rash confined to one or more adjacent dermatomes (does not cross midline)

Systemic symptoms:

- Fever

- Malaise

- Headache

Special complications:

- Ocular herpes zoster:

- Corneal involvement

- Cranial nerve damage (III, IV, VI)

- Herpes zoster oticus (Ramsay-Hunt syndrome):

- Involves cranial nerve VIII and glossopharyngeal nerve (IX)

- Symptoms: Severe ear pain, hearing loss, vertigo, facial nerve paralysis

Diagnosis: Based on clinical appearance

57 secComplications of Chickenpox

1. Incidence: ~7–8/1000 cases

2. Neurological complications:

3. Cranial nerve dysfunction

4. Meningitis

5. Spinal cord inflammation

6. Brain inflammation (15% mortality; 15% permanent cranial nerve damage)

7. Bacterial superinfections:

8. S. aureus and S. pyogenes group A

9. Most common complication

10. Other complications:

11. Generalized infections

12. Soft tissue infections

13. Reye's syndrome:

14. Associated with salicylate use in children

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Chickenpox Rash Stages:

Vesicles in Various Stages (VVS) – key diagnostic feature

🧠Herpes Zoster Cranial Nerves:

Ramsay-Hunt Syndrome:

- VIII (vestibulocochlear) + IX (glossopharyngeal)

🧠Chickenpox vs. Herpes Zoster Comparison Table:

Feature

Chickenpox

Herpes Zoster

Age group

Children (common)

Adults (common)

Rash distribution

Widespread, mucous membranes

Unilateral dermatome(s)

Complications

Reye’s syndrome, bacterial superinfections

Cranial nerve palsies, Ramsay-Hunt

Virus

VZV (varicella-zoster)

VZV (reactivated)

🧠Clinical Pearl:

Reye’s syndrome is a contraindication to salicylates in children with viral illnesses.

1 secPEDIATRICITY

23 secClinical Manifestations and Complications

Varicella (chickenpox) complications:

In utero infection (20 weeks gestation):

Skin scarring

Limb hypoplasia

Low birth weight

Microcephaly

Encephalitis

Visual disturbances (chorioretinitis, cataracts)

Severe rash

Pneumonia

Hepatitis

Neuralgia

Cranial nerve damage

Meningitis

Encephalitis

Myelitis

Serological diagnostics

7 secTreatment of Chickenpox and Shingles

Symptomatic treatment

Acyclovir:

Dose: 80 mg/kg/24 h PO

Administration: 3–4 divided doses

50 secPrevention and Prophylaxis

Recommended vaccination:

Varilrix

2 doses, at least 6 weeks apart

Children over 9 months of age (preferred over 1 year for better immune response)

Post-exposure management:

Active prophylaxis:

Vaccination of healthy/susceptible individuals within 72 hours of exposure

Prevents disease or modifies its course

Passive prophylaxis:

Varicella-zoster immunoglobulin (VZIG) for high-risk individuals

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Varicella Complications in Newborns:

"SCREAM"

- Skin scarring

- Chorioretinitis

- Rash severity

- Encephalitis

- Antibody response (immunoglobulin)

- Mortality (30%)

🧠Comparison Table: Active vs. Passive Prophylaxis

Active Prophylaxis

Passive Prophylaxis

Vaccination within 72h

VZIG administration

Prevents disease/course

Modifies disease severity

Long-term immunity

Short-term protection

🧠Clinical Pearl:

Varicella in newborns with maternal infection near delivery is life-threatening – prioritize VZIG and acyclovir in high-risk cases.

3 secSelected Infectious Diseases of Childhood | 89

23 secRisk Factors for Severe Disease and Complications

Individuals with compromised immune systems

Newborns with measles occurring within 5 days before or up to 2 days after birth

Pregnant women

Premature infants (born < 28 weeks of gestation) or those born weighing ≤1000 g (regardless of maternal measles serological status)

Mothers not immune to measles (regardless of prematurity)

6 secVZIG (Varicella-Zoster Immune Globulin)

Duration of action: ~3 weeks

Optimal administration: Within 96 hours after exposure

2 minThree-Day Fever (Exanthema Subitum, Roseola Infantum)

Etiopathogenesis

Cause: Infection with HHV-6 (6 months–3 years of age) or HHV-7 (after 3 years; β-Herpesviridae family)

Transmission: Droplet contact

Peak age of illness: 6–18 months

Incubation period: 8–14 days

Infectivity duration: Unknown

Clinical Symptoms

Fever: 39–40°C for 3–5 days, accompanied by:

Chills

Sweating

Cough

Cervical lymphadenopathy

Nagayama spots:

Red macules (2–8 mm) resembling sand grains

Located on soft palate mucosa, base of the tongue, and tonsillar fossa

Clinical evidence of HHV-6 or HHV-7 infection

Post-fever rash:

55% of cases: Rose-like rash

40% of cases: Maculopapular rash

Distribution: Neck, chest, back, arms, legs (occasionally face)

Duration: 1–3 days

Resolution: Fades without peeling

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Nagayama spots :

Neck, Anterior, Grains of Yellow And Mucosal Areas → Nagayama spots on soft palate, tongue base, and tonsillar fossa

🧠Key Risk Groups (Acronym):

Compromised immune system, Newborns with perinatal measles, Pregnant women, Premature infants (<28 weeks or ≤1000 g), Mothers not

immune to measles → CNP PM

🧠Rash Comparison Table:

3–5 days

🧠Clinical Pearl:

VZIG is most effective within 96 hours of exposure, but may still be beneficial if administered later, especially for high-risk individuals.

6 secRecognition

Based on clinical symptoms with exclusion of other diseases with similar clinical course.

7 secLaboratory Diagnostics

HHV-6 and HHV-7: No serological confirmation.

Fever: Leukocytosis with granulocytopenia.

Rash: Leukopenia with lymphocytosis, sometimes monocytosis.

1 secTreatment

Symptomatic treatment.

1 minINFECTIOUS RASH (erythema infectiosum)

Etiopathogenesis

Cause: Infection by parvovirus B19 (DNA).

Transmission: Likely via droplets and contact.

Age group: Mainly affects preschool-aged children.

Rash onset: 10–14 days after infection.

Infectivity period: Unknown.

Clinical Symptoms

Three-phase rash progression:

Face: Red, polymorphic eruption resembling a butterfly (no nasal bridge or mouth involvement).

Trunk and extremities: Garland-like rash, lasting 7–10 days (up to 3 weeks).

Worsening: Rash exacerbation with high fever (e.g., during bathing, overheating) – occurs in 1st–3rd week of illness.

Joint inflammation: Spontaneous resolution in hands, knees, and ankles (10% of cases).

Asymptomatic course: Common in 10% of infections.

Complications

Rare: Aseptic meningitis or arthritis.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Rash Phases

Face (butterfly rash) → Trunk (garland-like rash) → Fever (exacerbation in 1st–3rd week).

🧠Key Facts Table

Feature

Detail

Cause

Parvovirus B19 (DNA virus)

Transmission

Droplets, contact

Age group

Preschool children

Rash duration

7–10 days (up to 3 weeks)

Complications

Rare: Aseptic meningitis, arthritis

🧠Clinical Pearl

"Butterfly rash" on the face is a hallmark of erythema infectiosum, distinguishing it from other viral rashes.

2 secSelected Infectious Diseases of Childhood

16 secIntrauterine Infection

May cause miscarriage or fetal damage (disorder of fetal blood cell production) – hydrops foetalis.

Vascular hemophagocytic syndrome (VAHS).

Laboratory Diagnostics

In case of diagnostic doubts:

Serological diagnosis

PCR test to confirm viral presence.

Treatment

Symptomatic treatment.

1 minInfectious Mononucleosis (Mononucleosis)

Etiopathogenesis

Caused by Epstein-Barrow virus (EBV – DNA, γ-Herpesviridae).

15–20% of asymptomatic infected individuals have EBV in saliva.

Contagiousness period:

7 days before symptoms appear

During the disease course (may persist up to 18 months).

Incubation period: 30–50 days.

Clinical Manifestations

General symptoms:

Fever

Lack of appetite

Muscle and joint pain

Respiratory symptoms:

Pharyngitis

Rhinitis

Skin findings:

Small hemorrhagic rash on the palate

Skin rashes (rubella-like, morbilliform, scarlet fever-like, often with hemorrhagic component)

Pruritic, maculopapular, morbilliform rash after penicillin or cephalosporin administration.

Lymphadenopathy:

Generalized with peri-lymphatic edema (typical in childhood)

Localized in adolescents

Organ involvement:

Spleen enlargement (50% of cases)

Liver involvement (30% of cases)

Glanzmann's sign:

Swelling of the eyelids, bridge of the nose, and eyebrows.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

EBV Contagiousness: "7 days before symptoms, 18 months after" (7-18 rule).

Rash Triggers:

Penicillin/cephalosporin → pruritic, maculopapular rash.

Glanzmann's sign: Remember "Eyelids, Nose, Eyebrows" (E-N-E).

Mononucleosis Rash Types:

Rubella-like

Morbilliform

Scarlet fever-like

Hemorrhagic component (R-M-S-H).

Feature

Rash

Rubella-like, non-hemorrhagic

1 secPediatria: Mononukleozę (EBV)

9 secSymptomy w okresie odzyskiwania

Uczenie się: zmęczenie, lekkie osłabienie, brak apetytu – zespół długotrwałego zmęczenia (długo trwająca mononukleozę).

Diagnoza oparta na obrazie klinicznym.

21 secKomplikacje

Powiększenie migdałków i węzłów chłonnych podżuchwowych z objawami obturacyjnego zapalenia dróg oddechowych.

Zapalenie płuc.

Zapalenie meninges i mózgu, zaangażowanie nerwów czaszkowych (szczególnie VII), meningitis transversa, zespół Guillaina-Barré, napady

padaczkowe, psychozę.

Miokarditis, zapalenie osierdzia, zapalenie osierdzia, zapalenie pęcherza płucnego, zator naczynia przedziałowego.

Splenomegalia (2–3 tygodnie od początku choroby).

Hemolityczna anemia, trombocytopenia, granulocytopenia, neutropenia.

20 secDiagnoza laboratoryjna

Leukocytозa 10–30 tys. z dominacją limfocytów (60–80%), w tym 10–20% limfocytów atypowych (mononuklearnych).

Pozytywny test heterofilowy dla czerwonych krwinek owiec – wykrywanie przeciwciał przeciwko czerwonym krwinekom świń.

Trombocytopenia, wzrost aktywności aminotransferaz.

Wzrost poziomu IgM, IgG i IgA – tytrowanie przeciwciał przeciw EBV (najczulszy i najbardziej specyficzny test).

7 secLeczenie

Symptomatyczne: leki przeciwgorączkowe, lokalne zmniejszanie migdałków, dieta miękka, preparaty witaminowe.

Leczenie antybiotykowe w przypadku infekcji bakteryjnych.

27 secInne formy infekcji EBV

Zespół X-linked lymphoproliferative syndrome (XLP) u chłopców z niedoborem komórek NK i agammaglobulinemią, aplazją szpiku i

hipogammaproteinemią.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonik dla komplikacji EBV:

Meningitis, Pulmonary, Neurologic (Guillain-Barré, VII), Hematologic (anemia, trombocytopenia), Splenomegaly, Cardiac (miokarditis,

pericarditis).

🧠Kluczowe testy laboratoryjne:

Leukocytозa + Atypical lymphocytes → EBV.

Heterophile test (sheep RBC) + IgM/IgG titer → EBV Diagnosis.

🧠XLP:

X-linked, Lymphoproliferative, Peripheral NK deficiency, Agammaglobulinemia, Marrow aplasia, Hypogammaglobulinemia.

3 secWybrane choroby infekcyjne wieku dziecięcego

Strona 93

20 secChronicne zakażenie EBV (chroniczna mononukleozę)

U dzieci z syndromem Chediak-Higashiego i 1% dzieci po przeszczepie narządu.

EBV ma właściwości protoonkogenne i jest czynnikiem przyczyniającym się do patogenezy:

Limfomi Burkitta,

Krainofaryngiomy,

Choroby Hodgkina.

Zwiększone replikacje wirusa obserwuje się w:

Leukoplakii (oralnej, brodawczakowatej leukoplakii),

Limfomie komórek B,

Pneumonii EBV u dzieci z AIDS.

1 minZakażenia meningokokowe

Inwazyjna choroba meningokokowa (IChM)

Etiopatogeneza

1. Zakażenie Neisseria meningitidis (13 serotypów, w tym A, B, C, Y, W135, różniących się wrażliwością na infekcję).

2. Sposoby przekazania:

3. Krople (kaszlnięcie, kichanie),

4. Kontakt bezpośredni (np. przez używanie wspólnego szkła, butelki, talerza, utensyliów).

5. W Polsce i większości krajów europejskich dominuje serotyp B, ale w epidemii dominuje serotyp C (typowy dla USA).

6. Obecnie częstość infekcji serotypów B i C jest zbliżona.

7. Największa liczba przypadków w Afryce subsaharyjskiej (serotyp A).

8. Zakażenie głównie dotyka niemowląt i dzieci w wieku przedszkolnym.

9. Najcięższy przebieg u nastolatków (śmiertność do 20%).

10. Bezobjawowy nosicielstwo w nasofaryngusie:

11. 20–30% populacji,

12. 80% Polaków ma przeciwciała wskazujące na wcześniejszy kontakt z bakterią.

13. Okres zakaźności: okres obecności meningokoków w nasofaryngusie.

14. Okres inkubacji: 2–10 dni (najczęściej 3–4 dni).

Objawy kliniczne

Zakażenie charakteryzuje się bardzo zmiennym przebiegiem, zależnym od stanu zdrowia osoby zakażonej meningokokami.

17 secMemory Aid

Mnemoniki i kliniczne wskazówki

EBV i choroby nowotworowe:

Burkitta, Crainofaryngioma, Hodgkin – BCH (mnemonik: BCH).

Serotypy meningokoków i regiony:

Afryka subsaharyjska, B Polska, C USA, W135 (epidemie).

Zakaźność meningokoków:

Nasofaryngus, Drople, Kontakt bezpośredni.

Okres inkubacji meningokokowy: 2–10 dni (3–4 dni najczęściej).

1 secInvasive Meningococcal Infections

31 secForms of Invasive Meningococcal Infections

Generalized infection (sepsis) with adrenal gland damage (Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome)

Disturbances of consciousness

Extensive petechial and thromboembolic skin lesions

Progressive signs of multi-organ and cardiorespiratory failure

Fulminant course of the disease

Mortality exceeds 90%

In the absence of adrenal gland involvement, prognosis is better but still associated with high mortality (50%)

Meningitis

Typical symptoms of neuroinfection

Permanent neurological deficits may occur

Mortality rate approx. 10%

Focal infection of one organ

Pneumonia

Arthritis

Peritonitis

Pericarditis

4 secInitial Symptoms

Very non-specific: symptoms of flu or cold

14 secSymptoms of IChM in Infants

Fever above 39.5°C

Cold hands and feet

Vomiting

Diarrhea

Loss of appetite

Restlessness of the child

Crying

Tilted head position

Drowsiness

Pulsating dark circles

Petechial rash on the skin

13 secSymptoms of IChM in Older Children

Presence of meningeal symptoms

Photophobia

Fever or a drop in body temperature below 36°C

Rapid breathing

Hyperesthesia

Nausea

Vomiting

Drowsiness

Convulsions

"Jerking" (sudden muscle spasms)

Hemorrhagic rash

1 minRisk Factors for Developing Invasive Meningococcal Disease

1. Children from 3 months to 5 years of age

2. Patients with congenital immunodeficiencies, especially complement deficiencies

3. People who are overworked and subjected to constant stress

4. People staying in poor environmental conditions, especially in large groups of people (dormitories, barracks, boarding schools, orphanages)

5. Children chronically exposed to tobacco smoke

6. Young people aged 14–19, especially when they are often in close contact with many people (e.g., discos) and smoking cigarettes and/or taking

drugs

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonic for Forms of Invasive Meningococcal Infections

S.M.F.

- Sepsis (Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome)

- Meningitis

- Focal infection

🧠Clinical Pearl: Mortality Rates

Sepsis with adrenal involvement: >90% mortality

Sepsis without adrenal involvement: ~50% mortality

Meningitis: ~10% mortality

🧠Risk Factor Grouping

Age: 3–5 years, 14–19 years

Immunodeficiency: Complement deficiencies

Environmental Factors: Crowded living conditions, tobacco smoke exposure

Behavioral Risk: Overwork, stress, substance use

🧠Symptom Differentiation: Infants vs. Older Children

Infants: Focus on non-specific systemic symptoms (fever, cold extremities, vomiting, drowsiness)

Older Children: Focus on neurological and meningeal signs (photophobia, convulsions, hemorrhagic rash)

1 secCytomegalovirus Infection

16 secEpidemiology

One of the most common intrauterine infections.

Caused by the CMV virus (cytomegalovirus).

In pregnancy, infection usually goes unnoticed.

If the mother contracts the primary infection before or during pregnancy, the virus passes to the fetus in 40% of cases.

11 secEtiopathogenesis

Routes of infection:

- Through saliva.

- After passing infected blood through the placenta.

- During passage through the birth canal (perinatal infection).

- Through maternal milk.

10 secClinical Symptoms

Enlarged lymph nodes

Hepatosplenomegaly

Bleeding tendency

Lung inflammation

Brain inflammation

Retinitis and vasculitis

Hydrocephalus

Microcephaly

Hearing loss

Auditory disturbances

Swallowing difficulties

Delayed psychomotor development

54 secDiagnosis

Ultrasound of the fetus (growth restriction, microcephaly, intracranial calcifications) + isolation of the virus from amniotic fluid.

Detection of IgM antibodies and viral DNA using PCR method.

Isolation of the virus in urine or blood samples.

In newborns, especially premature infants with immature immune systems:

Detection of IgM antibodies alone may not be sufficient for diagnosis.

Necessary to use a qualitative DNA detection method.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Routes of infection: SALM (Saliva, Amniotic fluid, Birth canal, Milk).

Key diagnostic note: In premature newborns, IgM alone is not enough – confirm with viral DNA detection (PCR).

Ultrasound findings in fetuses: Growth restriction, microcephaly, intracranial calcifications.

Clinical symptoms to remember: LHBRH (Lymphadenopathy, Hepatosplenomegaly, Bleeding, Brain inflammation, Hearing loss).

Diagnosis methods: PCR + IgM (for maternal/fetal), viral isolation (urine/blood), ultrasound + amniotic fluid analysis.

3 secInvasive Meningococcal Disease: Complications, Diagnosis, Treatment, and Prevention

9 secComplications

Acute respiratory and circulatory failure

Hypotonia resistant to treatment

Renal insufficiency

Coagulation disorders

Multiorgan dysfunction

Neurological sequelae lasting for life

Death

16 secLaboratory Diagnosis and Diagnosis

Based on clinical history and physical examination

Pathogen isolation from:

Blood

Cerebrospinal fluid

Other biological materials

Laboratory findings:

Elevated inflammatory markers (OB, CRP, leukocytosis)

Coagulopathy

Routine nasopharyngeal carriage testing is not recommended, except for epidemic focus diagnosis.

21 secTreatment

Antibiotic Therapy

Penicillin G:

Dose: 200,000–300,000 j.m./kg mc/24 hours

Frequency: Divided into 4 doses

Cefotaxime:

Dose: 50–180 mg/kg mc/24 hours

Frequency: Divided into 4–6 doses

Ceftiraxone:

Dose: 50–100 mg/kg mc/24 hours

Frequency: Once daily or in two divided doses

Treatment is symptomatic and depends on the form of invasive meningococcal disease (ICM).

41 secPrevention

Vaccination

Protective vaccines for children:

NeisVac C

Meningitec

Menveo

Nimenrix

Chemoprophylaxis and Vaccination After Exposure

Indications for vaccination (serotype C) regardless of chemoprophylaxis:

Home contact

Kindergarten/preschool contact within 7 days before illness onset

Passengers living closest to the patient during at least 8 hours of flight

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics and Clinical Pearls

Complications Mnemonic: MENINGO (Multiorgan dysfunction, Neurological sequelae, Renal insufficiency, Coagulopathy, Hypotonia,

Organ failure)

Vaccine Acronym: VAC (NeisVac C, Meningitec, Menveo, Nimenrix)

Key Prevention Tip: "3 Cs" for vaccination after exposure: Close contact, Childcare settings, Crowded flights

Antibiotic Dose Comparison:

Penicillin G: 4 doses/day

Cefotaxime: 4–6 doses/day

Ceftiraxone: 1–2 doses/day

1 secPEDIATRIA

22 secExposure Criteria for Invasive Meningococcal Disease

Direct contact with patient secretions:

Childbirth, feeding with contaminated bottles

Contact within 7 days before illness onset

Resuscitation mouth-to-mouth, intubation without pressure maintenance:

Contact within 7 days before illness onset

Frequent close contact in the same room as a sick person:

Exposure within 7 days prior to symptom onset

28 secChemoprophylaxis Schema for Invasive Meningococcal Disease

Rifampicin

<1 month old: 5 mg/kg mc. p.o. every 12 hours for 2 days

≥1 month old: 10 mg/kg mc. p.o. (max 600 mg) every 12 hours for 2 days

Adults: 600 mg mc. p.o. every 12 hours for 2 days

Ceftriaxone

<15 years old: 125 mg i.m. once

≥15 years old: 250 mg i.m. once

Ciprofloxacin

≥18 years old: 500 mg p.o. once

3 minPNEUMOKOCAL INFECTIONS

Invasive Pneumococcal Disease (IChP)

Etiopathogenesis

Cause: Streptococcus pneumoniae (80 serotypes; 7 pathogenic serotypes: 4, 6B, 9V, 14, 18C, 19F, 23F)

Asymptomatic carriage in nasopharynx; bacteria overcome immune barriers to cause localized infections

Incidence:

General population: 15/100,000 cases

Children <2 years: 166/100,000 cases

Risk Factors for Invasive Pneumococcal Disease

1. Age <2 years; artificial milk feeding

2. Kindergarten attendance

3. Humoral immunity deficiencies:

4. HIV, agammaglobulinemia

5. Complement component deficiencies

6. Autoimmune and metabolic diseases

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Pneumococcal Serotypes:

4, 6B, 9V, 14, 18C, 19F, 23F → Use "4B9 14 18C 19F 23F" as a mnemonic (e.g., "Four B Nine, Fourteen, Eighteen C, Nineteen F, Twenty-

Three F")

Chemoprophylaxis Drug Dosing by Age:

| Drug | Age Group | Dose & Duration |

|--------------|-------------------|------------------------------------------|

| Rifampicin | <1 month | 5 mg/kg p.o. every 12h x2 days |

| | ≥1 month | 10 mg/kg p.o. (max 600 mg) every 12h x2 days |

| | Adults | 600 mg p.o. every 12h x2 days |

| Ceftriaxone | <15 years | 125 mg i.m. once |

| | ≥15 years | 250 mg i.m. once |

| Ciprofloxacin| ≥18 years | 500 mg p.o. once |

High-Risk Group for IChP:

Children <2 years have 10× higher incidence than general population.

Artificial milk feeding and kindergarten attendance are modifiable risk factors.

Wybrane Choroby Infekcyjne Wieku Dziecięcego

Page 97

Risk Factors for Invasive Infections

Post-splenectomy state and changes in mucociliary function (e.g., sickle cell anemia)

Nephrotic syndrome and chronic kidney disease

Chronic lung diseases (excluding asthma), heart, and liver disease

Neoplastic diseases

Post-transplantation state, prosthetic device implantation

Children after trauma (especially with cerebrospinal fluid leakage) and OUN syndromes

Poor socioeconomic conditions and exposure to tobacco smoke

Clinical Signs and IChP Disease Forms

Forms of the disease:

1. Acute and chronic middle ear infection and sinusitis – frequent complications

2. Conjunctivitis

3. Pneumonia – most common etiology in children under 5 years old:

- Sudden onset with high fever, cough, dyspnea

- Large radiological changes on chest X-ray

- 20% cases associated with pleural effusion and bacteremia

4. Meningitis – typical signs of neuroinfection:

- S. pneumoniae tends to cause large abscesses in OUN

- Leads to seizures, altered consciousness, paralysis, weakness

- Mortality rate: 5–10%

- Persistent neurological defects: 25–35%

Invasive Disease Forms

1. Bacteremia and septicemia

2. Chronic lung infection/bronchitis

3. Septic arthritis of joints, bones, and bone marrow

4. Osteomyelitis or septicemia

5. Austrian syndrome (endocarditis, meningitis, pneumonia)

6. OPSI (overwhelming postsplenectomy infection)

Memory Aid

Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls:

- Risk Factor Grouping:

- Immunocompromised states: Post-splenectomy, sickle cell anemia, post-transplantation

- Chronic diseases: Nephrotic syndrome, chronic lung/heart/liver disease

- Environmental factors: Poor socioeconomic conditions, tobacco smoke exposure

Pneumonia in Children <5 Years:

"FAST" (Fever, Acute onset, Sudden symptoms, Thoracic imaging changes)

Meningitis with S. pneumoniae:

"P" for Pneumococcus: Predisposes to abscesses in OUN, leading to neurological deficits

Invasive Forms:

OPSI = Overwhelming Postsplenectomy Infection (linked to S. pneumoniae)

Austrian syndrome = Triad of endocarditis, meningitis, pneumonia (remember A-E-M-P)

Mortality/Neurological Defects in Meningitis:

5–10% mortality, 25–35% neurological defects – use "5-10 and 25-35" as a recall anchor.

1 secPEDIATRIA

Page 98

13 secLaboratory Diagnosis

Isolation of bacteria from biological samples

Nasal swab cultures are not recommended for pneumococci isolation.

Detection of pneumococci does not confirm causation of respiratory tract infections (e.g., otitis media, pneumonia).

2 minTreatment

Pneumococcal Meningitis

Antibiotic therapy:

Penicillin: 400,000 j.m./kg mc/24 hours every 4–6 hours

Ampicillin: 300–400 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 4–6 hours

Ceftriaxone: 100 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 12–24 hours

Cefotaxime: 300 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 8 hours

Vancomycin: 60 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 6 hours

Meropenem: 120 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 8 hours

Rifampicin: 20 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 12 hours

Invasive Pneumococcal Infections (Excluding Meningitis)

Antibiotic therapy:

Penicillin: 250,000–300,000 j.m./kg mc/24 hours every 4–6 hours

Ampicillin: 100–200 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 6 hours

Ceftriaxone: 50 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 24 hours

Cefotaxime: 100 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 8 hours

Vancomycin: 40 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 6 hours

Clindamycin: 30–40 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 6–8 hours

Oral Antibiotic Therapy for Non-Invasive Pneumococcal Infections

Amoxicillin: 80–90 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 8–12 hours

Amoxicillin/Clavulanate: 80–90 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 8–12 hours

Cefpodoxime: 10 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 12 hours

Cefdinir: 14 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 12–24 hours

Aceclofenac: 30 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 12 hours

Clindamycin: 20–40 mg/kg mc/24 hours every 8 hours

🧠Memory Aid

Mnemonic for antibiotics in meningitis:

Penicillin, Ampicillin, Ceftriaxone, Cefotaxime, Vancomycin, Meropenem, Rifampicin → PACCCVR.

Key difference:

Meningitis requires higher doses of cefotaxime (300 mg/kg) vs. invasive infections (100 mg/kg).

Oral therapy uses amoxicillin and amoxicillin/clavulanate for non-invasive cases.

Clinical pearl:

Nasal swabs are not reliable for diagnosing pneumococcal infections; blood cultures or CSF analysis are preferred.

2 secSelected Infectious Diseases of Childhood

12 secPrevention

Mandatory preventive vaccination with a 10-valent vaccine (Synflorix) in accordance with the current vaccination schedule (for children born

after January 1, 2017) or with the recommended 13-valent vaccine (Prevnar 13).

3 minCommon Infantile Paralysis (Poliomyelitis Anterior Acuta, Poliomyelitis)

Etiopathogenesis

Infection with isolated wild strains of poliovirus type 1 (epidemic type), type 2 (endemic type), and type 3 (epidemic type) (Picornaviridae).

Source of infection: A sick person or a virus carrier after an asymptomatic infection; the infection spreads mainly through the alimentary canal

and droplets.

Period of contagiousness: A few days before falling ill until 6 weeks after the illness (period of virus excretion in the feces).

Lodging period: 7–14 days.

Clinical Symptoms

Asymptomatic course (most often, 90–95%).

Abortive form: Fever, headaches, sore throat, symptoms of respiratory and gastrointestinal inflammation.

Serous meningitis: Symptoms of meningeal infection; in 1% of cases progresses to the paralytic form (flaccid, asymmetric proximal paralysis,

most often affecting the limb muscles).

Paralytic form: Three forms depending on the location of the lesions:

Spinal

Cerebral

Bulbar

Acute asymmetric flaccid paralysis mainly of the proximal muscles.

Paralysis of the cranial nerves.

Paralysis occurring with paralysis of the respiratory muscles and circulatory and respiratory failure (Landry form).

Cerebral form: Increased motor excitability, drowsiness, disturbances of consciousness.

🧠Memory Aid

Polio Vaccine Mnemonic:

Synflorix = 10-valent (remember "Syn" for "Synflorix" and "10" for valency).

Prevnar 13 = 13-valent (remember "13" in the name).

Polio Forms Acronym:

SCB = Spinal, Cerebral, Bulbar (use as a mnemonic for the three paralytic forms).

Clinical Pearl:

Landry form = Respiratory paralysis + circulatory/respiratory failure (key feature to distinguish from other forms).

Contagiousness Timeline:

Before symptoms → 6 weeks post-illness (virus excretion in feces).

Lodging Period:

7–14 days (incubation period).

Clinical Signs and Symptoms

Paralysis of cranial nerves, tremors and muscle spasms, aphasia, seizures.

Diagnosis based on clinical picture.

Complications

Limb paralysis.

Muscle weakness, muscle atrophy (years after polio).

Laboratory Diagnosis

Isolation of virus from stool sample.

Treatment

Symptomatic: antipyretic, analgesic, sedative; rehabilitation procedures.

Prevention

Vaccination according to the vaccination schedule (IPV).

Vaccine-Associated Paralytic Poliomyelitis (VAPP)

Paralytic poliomyelitis following live attenuated vaccine (OPV) or 4–75 days after contact with a person vaccinated with OPV.

Patients who have not had contact with the environment where wild poliovirus transmission is possible and in whom no wild-type virus is

detected upon double stool examination, but in which at least one attenuated strain is detected.

Salivary Gland Inflammation (Swine Fever, Parotitis)

Etiopathogenesis

Infection with paramyxovirus RNA.

Transmission by droplet contact, rarely through saliva-contaminated objects.

🧠Memory Aid

Clinical Signs Mnemonic: PAST (Paralysis, Aphasia, Seizures, Tremors).

VAPP Key Points:

VAPP occurs after OPV vaccination or exposure to OPV-vaccinated individuals.

Differentiate from wild polio by detecting attenuated strains (not wild-type virus) in stool.

Prevention Comparison Table:

| Vaccine Type | Risk of VAPP | Preferred for Routine Use |

|--------------|--------------|---------------------------|

| IPV | 0 | Yes |

| OPV | 1 in 1–2.5 million | No (due to VAPP risk) |

Parotitis Transmission: Remember droplet spread is the main route; avoid sharing utensils or close contact during outbreaks.

3 secMost Common Illnesses in Children Aged 5–15 Years

8 secPeriod of Contagiousness

2–6 days before the appearance of salivary gland swelling

Up to 9 days after the onset of swelling

3 secIncubation Period

12–25 days (average: 18 days)

44 secClinical Symptoms

Prodromal Period

Brief period with variable symptoms:

Fever

Fatigue

Nausea

Vomiting

Ear pain

Nosebleeds

Acute Symptoms

Salivary Gland Involvement:

Painful, unilateral (most often left side) or bilateral enlargement of salivary glands

Swelling appears 7–9 days after prodromal symptoms

Elevation of the cheek associated with salivary gland swelling

Swelling under the jaw

Submandibular Duct (Stensen’s duct):

Pain and redness at the duct opening

Oral Symptoms:

Redness and inflammation of the mucous membrane of the throat and cheeks at the level of the premolars

Saliva Production:

Decreased saliva production

Increased sensitivity to sour taste

Systemic Symptoms:

Headache

Difficulty swallowing

Limited head movement

Pharyngitis

15–30% of cases: asymptomatic or mildly symptomatic

14 secComplications

1. Meningoencephalitis (1–10%)

2. Peritonsillar Abscess (usually on the 8th day of illness; 4% of cases may cause infertility)

3. Pancreatitis

4. Unilateral Hearing Loss (1:20,000)

5. Testicular or Epididymal Inflammation, Orchitis, Oophoritis (rarely)

10 secLaboratory Diagnosis

Blood Tests:

Leukopenia with lymphocytosis

Increased amylase activity in blood serum

Viral Isolation:

From saliva, blood, urine, or cerebrospinal fluid

Serological Tests

2 minMemory Aid

Mnemonic for Clinical Symptoms

"Fever, Ear, Vomit, Jaw, Sour"

- Fever (prodromal)

- Ear pain (prodromal)

- Vomiting (prodromal)

- Jaw swelling (acute phase)

- Sour taste sensitivity (acute phase)

Complication Mnemonic

"MEN"

- Meningoencephalitis

- Epididymitis/orchitis

- Neurological (hearing loss)

Comparison Table: Key Features

Feature

Description

Contagious Period

2–6 days before symptoms + 9 days after onset

Incubation

12–25 days (avg. 18 days)

Common Complication

Meningoencephalitis (1–10%)

Diagnostic Marker

Elevated amylase, lymphocytosis, viral isolation in saliva/CSF

Clinical Pearl

Peritonsillar abscess is a rare but serious complication that may lead to infertility in 4% of cases. Monitor for signs of abscess formation (e.g.,

trismus, unilateral swelling) on day 8 of illness.

102 PEDIATRIA

Treatment

Symptomatic: antipyretic; warm compresses to the area around the salivary glands.

Prevention

Vaccination according to the vaccination schedule (Priorix, MMR).

Isolation of patients until clinical symptoms subside, for at least 9 days.

MOLLUSCUM CONTAGIOSUM

Etiopathogenesis

Infection by Molluscum contagiosum virus (MCV - Poxvirus).

Most common in children aged 1–5 years.

Transmission through contact with an infected person or clothing, towels, toys, and other objects.

Clinical Symptoms

Characteristic pearl-like bumps with a depression in the central part of the lesion, localized on the skin of hands, torso, face, most commonly on

the inner surfaces of the thighs, genital organs, and around the inguinal area.

When pressed, material resembling mucus is expelled from the central part of the bump.

Local signs of inflammation may appear in case of bacterial infection.

Complications

Bacterial infections.

Laboratory Diagnosis

Detection of intracytoplasmic internal bodies within tissue samples (Wright's stain, Giemsa or Gram staining).

Confirmation of "molluscum bodies" under light microscopy.

Memory Aid

Mnemonic for clinical symptoms: "Pearl-like bumps with central pits" → "Pearly Pearls with Pits".

Transmission acronym: CLOTH (Contact, Clothing, Towels, Objects, Hands).

Key lab finding: "Molluscum bodies" (intracytoplasmic inclusions seen with Wright/Giemsa staining).

Prevention highlight: MMR vaccine (Priorix) and 9-day isolation.

2 secScarlet Fever (Płonica, Szkarlatyna)

14 secTreatment of Skin Changes in Children

Isolated skin changes:

Spontaneous regression within 6–18 months.

Numerous changes or recurrent lesions:

Mechanical removal (grinding bumps after local anesthesia).

Pharmacotherapy: Rarely used in children (e.g., Imiquimod, Kantridin, Trentynoina).

20 secEtiopathogenesis

Cause: Infection with β-hemolytic group A streptococcus producing toxic erythrogenic toxin.

Transmission:

Droplets, direct contact with objects, and food (especially milk).

Seasonality:

Peaks in autumn and winter.

Does not occur before 1 year of age.

Incubation period:

Presence of streptococci in maternal vaginal secretions.

Contagiousness:

1–7 days duration.

3 minClinical Symptoms

Initial phase:

Tonsillitis with fever, chills, headache.

Rash development (24 hours after onset):

Fine papular rash (blanching or erythematous with skin indentation).

Distribution: Face, neck, extremities.

Duration: Up to 5 days.

Finkelstein sign: No rash (used to differentiate from other conditions).

Tongue changes:

Initially white coating, then strawberry-colored.

Capillary fragility signs:

Pastia lines: Linear ecchymoses in superficial/joint spaces.

Hecht sign: Linear ecchymoses after injection.

🧠Memory Aid

🧠Mnemonics & Clinical Pearls

Pastia lines: Remember as "Pastia" = "Past" (pastel-colored lines) on the skin.

Hecht sign: Think "Hecht" = "Hematoma" after injection.

Scarlet fever rash: "Blanching, fine, and fleeting" (blanching, fine papules, resolves in 5 days).

Tongue appearance: "Strawberry tongue" (red, bumpy, like a strawberry).

Treatment in children:

Isolated lesions → wait (spontaneous regression).

Recurrent lesions → grind (mechanical removal).

🧠Comparison Table: Scarlet Fever vs. Other Rash Conditions

Feature

Scarlet Fever

Measles

Rash onset

24 hours after fever

14 days after exposure

Rash appearance

Fine, blanching papules

Coarse, erythematous macules

Finkelstein sign

Negative (no rash)

Not applicable

Tongue changes

Strawberry tongue

No specific tongue changes

🧠Key Takeaway

Scarlet fever is a streptococcal infection with a distinct rash and strawberry tongue. Treatment in children depends on lesion severity—

wait for isolated changes, grind for recurrent ones.

Measles Rash Characteristics

- Skin peeling occurs in the 3rd week of illness, mainly on feet and hands.

- Rash areas: Skin damage from cosmetic procedures, cracked vesicles of chickenpox, etc.

- Progression: No pain involved.

Complications

- Bronchitis

- Middle ear inflammation

- Kidney inflammation

- Joint inflammation

- Heart muscle inflammation and pericarditis

Laboratory Diagnostics

- Increased:

- OB (erythrocyte sedimentation rate)

- CRP (C-reactive protein)

- Leukocytosis

- Eosinophilia

- Diagnosis methods:

- Identification of Bordetella pertussis from throat swab (antigen rapid test)

- Mandatory: General urine examination (during illness or post-recovery)

Treatment

- Antibiotics:

- Benzylpenicillin: 50,000–150,000 j.m./kg mc./24 hours (4 doses)

- Cephalosporins

- Macrolides

Whooping Cough (Pertussis)

Etiopathogenesis

- Cause: Infection by Bordetella pertussis

- Transmission: Droplet infection (peak cases at age 5)

- Incubation period: 3–14 days (average 7 days)

- Infectious period: 7–14 days

Memory Aid

- Complications of measles: Use B.M.E.K.J. mnemonic:

- Bronchitis

- Middle ear inflammation

- Epididymitis (kidney inflammation)

- Knee (joint) inflammation

- Joint (heart muscle) inflammation

Treatment antibiotics: B.C.M.

Benzylpenicillin

Cephalosporins

Macrolides

Pertussis vs. Measles:

| Feature | Pertussis | Measles |

|-----------------------|----------------------------|----------------------------|

| Rash | Absent | Present (peeling in week 3)|

| Incubation period | 3–14 days (avg 7) | 10–14 days |

| Transmission | Droplets | Droplets |

| Key lab finding | B. pertussis in throat | Elevated CRP, leukocytosis |
